# Giám sát và phản hồi tử vong mẹ, tử vong chu sinh — báo cáo toàn cầu về thập kỷ triển khai

**Bản PDF nguồn:** https://drive.google.com/file/d/1IdkENdd3Sv9ZJp3K9khLsa70Q18IANdK/view

> **Trạng thái:** Chưa phải bản dịch tiếng Việt toàn văn hoặc bản Markdown lâm sàng đã kiểm định. Phần tiếng Việt ngay sau đây định vị nội dung của nguồn; toàn bộ phần văn bản có thể trích xuất được từ PDF giữ trong phụ lục theo trang để đối chiếu, kể cả cảnh báo và chú thích. Một số nội dung chỉ có ở hình/bảng/sơ đồ hoặc trang quét có thể chưa được tái tạo chính xác; cần xem PDF gốc. Không đưa tài liệu này vào lớp trả lời lâm sàng khi `clinical_ingest_ready: false`.

## Phạm vi và kiến thức trọng tâm (diễn giải tiếng Việt)

Báo cáo toàn cầu của WHO xem xét kinh nghiệm nhiều năm triển khai giám sát và phản hồi tử vong mẹ, tử vong chu sinh. Các dữ liệu quốc gia và chỉ báo phải được truy xuất kèm năm, định nghĩa, nguồn và mẫu số; không thể đồng nhất khác biệt giữa hệ thống báo cáo với nguy cơ tử vong cá nhân. Các biểu đồ, bản đồ và bảng có yếu tố trực quan trong nguồn vẫn phải đối chiếu PDF gốc.

## Cảnh báo và giới hạn ingest

- Không tự suy luận thêm chỉ định thuốc, phác đồ hay ngưỡng xét nghiệm ngoài nguồn gốc.
- Số liệu cần được giữ cùng quốc gia, mẫu số, năm và thiết kế nghiên cứu.
- Đối chiếu bản PDF gốc trước khi tách chunk có ngưỡng số, bảng, sơ đồ, điều kiện chuyển tuyến hoặc hướng dẫn cấp cứu.
- Không đánh dấu bản này là đã Việt hóa 100% hoặc đã qua thẩm định y khoa.

### Trang PDF 12

````text
Executive summary


 © WHO / Blink Media - Nana Kofi Acquah,
 Ghana. Baby Ishod, who delivered by
 caesarean section 11 days ago, has his
 first postnatal check-up at the Greater
 Accra Regional Hospital.


 Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response (MPDSR) is an approach promoted by
 the World Health Organization (WHO) and partners to reduce maternal and perinatal mortality.
 MPDSR involves stakeholders in the processes of identifying and reporting maternal and
 newborn deaths and stillbirths, understanding why the deaths occurred, taking actions to prevent
 similar deaths occurring in the future, monitoring implementation of actions and resulting
 changes, and promoting accountability for maternal and perinatal deaths.

 The aim of this report is to describe progress on strengthening MPDSR over the last 10 years, as
 a follow up to the WHO and UNFPA Time to respond: a report on the global implementation of
 maternal death surveillance and response, published in 2016. The report describes improvements
 in policies, implementation and tracking of MPDSR implementation, and highlights how MPDSR
 can be successful in various contexts, such as humanitarian and fragile settings. Eight country
 case studies highlight examples of addressing barriers to MPDSR implementation by using the
 MPDSR approach to reduce maternal and perinatal mortality and increase accountability for
 maternal and perinatal deaths. Finally, the report outlines progress on MPDSR implementation
 since 2016, improvements in barriers to implementation, and highlights focus areas to further
 strengthen to 2030.

 This report takes a systematic approach investigating in-depth the practice of using MPDSR in
 eight countries around the world with more than 160 semi-structured interviews.

 The one thing that has been significant … is the improvement in the quality of care …
 We were able to standardize treatment protocol[s] and management. We have
 enough information and feedback in some areas of reproductive health to understand
 what the challenges [are] and how to overcome them (state MPDSR committee
 member in Nigeria).

x
````

### Trang PDF 13

````text
Executive summary


Through case studies from countries at different levels of progress in MPDSR implementation, it
also reports on gaps and challenges in MPDSR implementation and shares experiences from
countries on how these have been addressed. It is expected that this report will encourage the
global health community of policy-makers, programme managers, health workers, civil society
organizations (CSOs), professional societies, academic institutions, bilateral and multilateral
agencies and foundations working in maternal, perinatal and newborn health to continue to
strengthen MPDSR implementation and mobilize efforts and funding to overcome gaps and
challenges that adversely affect maternal and perinatal survival.

Methods
A global advisory group of MPDSR experts was established to support the preparation of the
report. Survey and monitoring data periodically collected by WHO, United Nations Population
Fund (UNFPA), UNICEF and other partners were collated and analysed for the status of national
policies and implementation of MPDSR worldwide. In addition, a realist review of literature was
conducted to explore strategies that have been used to engage community members in MPDSR.

In-depth case studies on MPDSR implementation were conducted in Bangladesh, Cameroon,
Colombia, Nigeria, North Macedonia, Pakistan, Sri Lanka and Uganda through similar mixed-method
approaches with each country focusing on a specific area of its choice.

Findings
MPDSR-related policies
Data from periodic WHO and UNFPA surveys show that most countries have adopted policies
essential for the implementation of maternal and perinatal death reviews.
 • In 2018, roughly four of every five countries (81%) had national policies, guidelines and/or laws
 requiring all maternal deaths to be notified to a central authority within 24 hours. In 2023, some
 72% of countries reported having a national policy, guideline or law requiring all maternal and
 perinatal deaths in health facilities to be notified within 24 hours to a central authority; 16% of
 countries reported that this national policy/guideline/law is for maternal deaths only.
 • In 2018, some 84% of countries required review all maternal deaths, while it was less common
 to require review of stillbirths (43% of countries) or neonatal deaths (0–28 days) (67%). In 2023,
 some 79% of countries reported having a national policy, guideline or law requiring audit
 and/or review of all maternal and perinatal deaths; 13% of countries reported that this national
 policy/guideline/law is for maternal deaths only.
 • In 2018, the existence of national committees to review maternal deaths was reported by 67%
 of countries. In 2023, some 83% of responding countries reported they had a national MPDSR
 team/committee of the Ministry of Health.
 • In 2018, national policies/guidelines/law for action plans to implement recommendations
 identified in the maternal death review process were reported by 110 (73%) countries. In 2023,
 three quarters of countries (75%) affirmed that they had a guideline, policy and/or law
 requiring the MPDSR Committee at health facilities or the Ministry of Health to develop a
 response plan on quality improvements based on the analysed death reviews.

In 2023, of all responding countries, 66% reported that they had a national guideline, policy and/or
law requiring that both maternal and perinatal deaths occurring outside of health facilities be
notified to a central authority within 48 hours, while 53% reported that MPDSR reports are published.

 xi
````

### Trang PDF 14

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 MPDSR implementation
 Data on MPDSR implementation between 2016 and 2020 were available from 30–39 countries
 (number of reporting countries varied between years), mostly in sub-Saharan Africa, supported
 by UNFPA’s Maternal Health Thematic Fund. Subnational committees able to implement MPDSR
 were reportedly available in 92% of the 25 African countries in the 2020 survey, up from 76% in
 2018. In 2020, perinatal deaths were included under the MPDSR programme in 92% of these
 countries, up from 56% in 2018. MPDSR data were included in the national health information
 system in 88%, maternal death notification was used as an indicator of performance of the system
 in 68%, and annual MPDSR reports available in 48% of these countries in 2020.

 Community engagement
 Community engagement involves interaction among community members and between
 community members and health workers/the health system. The realist review recognized the
 importance of building on existing relationships between the health system and community
 members to strengthen reporting and review of maternal and perinatal deaths in the community,
 and to develop and implement actions to improve healthy survival that are appropriate to the
 context. Community engagement requires enabling environments and investment in training,
 mentoring, monitoring and supervision of community informants, volunteers and other actors.
 The community engagement process can be sustained better if strategies are used to recognize
 the value of community informants’ and community actors’ time and to ensure adequate
 compensation for the opportunity costs of their engagement. A blame culture can affect the
 willingness of health workers to collaborate with community members in joint death review
 sessions. Health workers need to be supported by the wider health system and legal frameworks
 to sustain their morale for supporting community engagement in MPDSR.

 Capacity-building
 MPDSR e-learning courses have been successfully implemented by WHO, UNFPA and UNICEF in
 eastern and southern Africa, and by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO). These two
 courses have been accessed by over 110 000 health workers. Online workshops to develop
 master trainers, led by the USAID-funded MOMENTUM Country & Global Leadership Project,
 United Nations agencies and professional associations, mostly during the coronavirus disease
 (COVID-19) pandemic, trained 250 service providers and managers in Asia and Africa.

 Implementation challenges during the COVID-19 pandemic
 Implementing MPDSR during the COVID-19 pandemic was particularly challenging. Countries
 reported various degrees of disruption, particularly at the beginning of the pandemic when health
 systems were overwhelmed. The most frequently reported issues related to notification, and
 reporting and completion of mortality reviews. Facility deaths continued to be reported, with
 some countries reporting a closer focus on them, as concern grew about the effects of COVID-19
 on pregnant women.

 Country case studies
 The case study from Bangladesh highlights the role of the community in MPDSR. Information on
 maternal and perinatal deaths is collected by trained field workers using community networks.
 During the verbal and social autopsies of these deaths, health workers, members of the community
 and families discuss the deaths collectively. Medically qualified staff assign cause of death while
 the community collectively discusses and determines root causes leading to the death and

xii
````

### Trang PDF 15

````text
Executive summary


strategies to prevent similar deaths. Discussion points from the reviews are shared with MPDSR
focal persons and these contribute to further discussions and decision-making in sub-district
and district MPDSR and quality improvement committees.

The case study from Cameroon focuses on how an incident management system (IMS) is being
used to strengthen maternal and perinatal mortality reporting and MPDSR across the country.
Through strong linkages with the Integrated Disease Surveillance and Response platform,
deaths are notified at district, regional and national levels and data are reviewed weekly.
Professional associations contributed to the development of guidelines and tools, including tools
to support planning and response tracking at all levels. IMS teams function as MPDSR steering
committees to review deaths in each region. Weekly bulletins on epidemiological aspects of
maternal and perinatal deaths help to identify gaps, create accountability and ensure follow-up of
recommendations. The IMS structure has supported significant and noteworthy progress in
expanding MPDSR, particularly by increasing national death reporting coverage, establishing
death review committees within health facilities, and strengthening coordination of maternal and
perinatal mortality surveillance training, activities and responses at all levels.

Colombia’s national public health surveillance system (SIVIGILA), established by the National
Institute of Health in 2006, collects and uses data on maternal and neonatal mortality, stillbirths,
severe maternal morbidities and congenital defects. These events are reported via the web-based
SIVIGILA Safe Motherhood application. Annually updated public health surveillance guidelines
and protocols apply to all department and district entities, health-service provider institutions
and benefit plan administration entities (insurers). This system allows for the generation of
epidemiological alerts, follow-up and mitigation of events identified as tracers of the health and
well-being of pregnant women and newborns.

Fear of blame and punitive actions can adversely affect MPDSR implementation. The case study
from Nigeria describes the efforts of professional organizations and other stakeholders to
engage with parliamentarians to address this impediment. The National MPDSR Bill passed by
parliament provides legal support for the MPDSR process and has provisions to protect against
blame along with a framework for review committees.

The case study from North Macedonia focuses exclusively on perinatal deaths. After WHO
published guidance on reviews of stillbirths and neonatal deaths, the Government established a
Perinatal Mortality Steering Committee. This Committee has developed the perinatal audit system
to collect information on all stillbirths and neonatal deaths and encourages zero reporting (i.e. no
such deaths). An interdisciplinary team identifies modifiable factors relating to perinatal deaths
and makes key recommendations focusing on service delivery, quality of care and health
information systems. The Ministry of Health ensures legal immunity for participation in the
review process and disseminates findings and recommendations from the reviews to all
institutions and professionals.

The role of digital technologies in MPDSR is the focus of the case study from Pakistan. The MDSR
mobile app was developed in Pakistan to improve maternal death reporting. Care providers in
selected hospitals have successfully used the app to complete and submit reporting forms
online, resulting in improvements in the quality and timeliness of information available for
maternal death reviews. Provincial governments and professional societies collaborated to set
up provincial MDSR task forces and review committees. With the app linked to an online
dashboard in the National Institute of Health, the Government has access to real-time data
reporting showing causes of deaths, trends and associated delays. Provincial situational reports
now include steps taken to address maternal deaths.


 xiii
````

### Trang PDF 16

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 “Response” is the focus of the case study from Sri Lanka, which has a long history of implementing
 maternal death reviews. Notification of maternal deaths is mandatory and information on
 circumstances leading to the death is collected from the community and from health facilities.
 Reviews at facility level are conducted within 14 days, with further reviews of selected cases at
 district and national levels. However, any cases involving significant issues warranting urgent
 response undergo immediate high-level review. Decisions on actions to address underlying
 factors are made at monthly progress meetings with grass-roots public health workers at the
 community level and with staff in hospitals. Recommendations are specific, measurable,
 achievable, realistic and time-bound (SMART), and identify individuals or agencies responsible
 for implementation. Implementation of recommendations is monitored regularly.

 The last case study highlights the complexities of implementing MPDSR within humanitarian
 settings in Uganda. MPDSR implementation in Ugandan refugee settings is integrated with the
 national system, but dual documentation through national monitoring systems and the
 Integrated Refugee Health Information System can be challenging. Key partners supporting and
 directly implementing MPDSR within refugee settlements in Uganda call for a customized
 approach to implementing MPDSR that best serves the immediate needs of the crisis, aligns with
 stakeholder priorities and supports health-service providers in providing care to the most
 vulnerable populations.

 Next steps
 Much has happened in the last decade in terms of national responses to global calls to end
 preventable maternal and newborn deaths, and stillbirths. Most countries have adopted policies
 and practices that support implementation, first of MDSR, and more recently of MPDSR.
 However, nearly a quarter of the world population lives in fragile settings with high rates of
 maternal and perinatal mortality and faces additional implementation challenges for MPDSR when
 possible to use. Communities should be engaged in further strengthening MPDSR implementation.
 The experiences of implementation in selected countries from different WHO regions illustrate
 how MPDSR works in practice despite the many socioeconomic and security-related challenges
 that impact on health. The country case studies highlight not only the ongoing challenges of
 MPDSR but also the many successes of MPDSR globally.

 While many of the challenges experienced in MPDSR implementation were shared by most
 countries, there were also factors common to many countries that were linked to successful
 outcomes. Political will, leadership, strong governance and accountability at the highest level all
 move the MPDSR process forward faster. Individual champions are essential, and include a range
 of individuals, including politicians, professionals (nurses, midwives, obstetricians, paediatricians
 and others), community health workers, and of course community members, pregnant individuals
 and their families.

 The slow pace of change in mortality rates resulting from dysfunctional MPDSR processes could
 demotivate health workers and lead to the discontinuation of MPDSR. To ensure that the MPDSR
 process leads to better health outcomes for mothers and their babies, implementers should
 focus on:
 • improving the data quality of reported cases, causes and underlying causes of deaths;
 • improving the effectiveness of death review meetings to foster a culture of learning for
 health workers;
 • formulating and implement recommendations to improve quality of care to strengthen service
 delivery and experience of care;

xiv
````

### Trang PDF 17

````text
Executive summary


 • strengthening community engagement;
 • adapting existing tools to address additional complexities in humanitarian settings when feasible;
 • enabling a protective legal environment supporting transparency, accountability and
 confidentiality to avoid the blame culture.

Improving data quality, including for stillbirths and newborn
deaths
Deaths continue to be underreported, especially perinatal deaths. Stillbirths are not counted and
newborn deaths when they are counted are not reviewed. In many countries, deaths in communities
are often not identified or investigated. Even for deaths in health facilities, documentation of
events is often incomplete. Medical documents may not be available. Insufficient information at
death review meetings results in poor-quality recommendations. Deaths in private health
facilities are often not reported or followed up through the system.

The continue “disconnection” existing between the “maternal” and “perinatal” components in
MPDSR does not allow professionals and policy-makers to link the causes of perinatal mortality
with the maternal conditions. Without this, they are unable to propose recommendations and
changes to correct issues at population level.

Potential solutions include: customizing and adapting reporting forms to the local context;
developing or integrating with existing electronic data collection systems; selecting a number of
perinatal death cases to review each month starting with early newborn deaths, intrapartum and
antepartum stillbirths; improving the quality of documentation in and storage of medical records;
and pre-service and in-service training and mentoring of health workers to use data collection
tools. The experiences reported from Cameroon and Pakistan illustrate some of these solutions.
Changes in regulations may be required to ensure that deaths in private health facilities are
reported and reviewed through the national MPDSR system. The experience of Colombia is a
good example of national efforts to improve data collection, with specific quality checks on
death reviews.

Improving the effectiveness of death review meetings
Some MPDSR meetings at national and subnational levels are ineffective and irregularly
convened, producing little improvement in care quality or health outcomes. Capacity-building
activities in different regions are working to ensure that all members involved in MPDSR
meetings are trained and mentored for their roles and responsibilities. Wherever feasible,
community participation should be encouraged. Formulation of actionable recommendations
and institutionalization of a no-blame culture are essential to improve the effectiveness of
MPDSR meetings. The chairperson should be skilled at running meetings that are effective and
provide a safe learning environment. Confidentiality and the no-blame principle should be
respected. All members could be asked to sign a no-blame charter that defines the code of
conduct during meetings. It is important to ensure that the right stakeholders are present and
that meetings are held regularly with effective and inclusive participation. Specific individuals
should take responsibility for owning, monitoring, championing or implementing each
recommendation, as appropriate, with clear terms of reference and expectations. Participants
should be triggered to change practice to prevent deaths and to be attuned to the quality of their
own work and that of others.


 xv
````

### Trang PDF 18

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 Formulating and implement recommendations to improve quality
 of care to strengthen service delivery and experience of care
 It is important for MPDSR committee members to work closely with quality improvement teams
 at national and subnational levels. Monitoring and evaluation of MPDSR is required to ensure
 that effective action is taking place and that quality of care is improving. At each death review
 meeting, information should be sought and documented on the implementation of recommended
 actions from previous reviews. Improvements in clinical outcomes resulting from implementation
 of recommendations (e.g. improved labour management, improved quality of care for newborns,
 particularly small and sick newborns, and improved postnatal care for mothers) should also be
 documented. The Sri Lanka case study provides an example of diligent monitoring of the
 implementation of recommendations from death reviews.

 Strengthening community engagement
 The Bangladesh case study provides a learning opportunity on community participation in MPDSR.
 Many CSOs work with communities to provide clinical services and facilitate transport to health
 facilities. Community informants could be trained to report deaths. Community health workers
 (CHWs) could be trained and mentored to conduct verbal autopsies and provide bereavement
 counselling and support for relatives who have lost a baby or a mother. Community death review
 meetings provide a channel for community feedback to health facilities and for actions at community
 level to address shortcomings. They also provide opportunities to strengthen communication
 between communities and health facilities and can lead to improved care-seeking, improved
 uptake of preventive interventions, and reduced delays in seeking and obtaining appropriate care.

 Adapting existing tools to address additional complexities in
 humanitarian settings when feasible
 Although some implementation challenges are similar to those documented in stable settings, as
 noted in the Uganda case study, these are often exacerbated by weakened national health system
 capacity, human resource shortages and turn-over, fluctuation of external funding, heightened
 political tensions, and lack of trust between community members, government authorities and
 humanitarian actors. There is need for more operational guidance and for materials that can
 accommodate the additional complexities of health programming in humanitarian settings.
 Continued exchange of lessons learned among humanitarian actors, and increased attention to
 humanitarian settings in national MPDSR initiatives, will help inform future guidance and ensure
 that MPDSR significantly strengthens health services and outcomes in all settings.

 Enabling a protective legal environment supporting
 transparency, accountability and confidentiality to avoid the
 blame culture
 Fear of blame, punitive action and litigation were highlighted by all countries and successfully
 addressed by several – either by creating a culture of success resulting in improved outcomes or
 by legislating to reduce the potential consequences of blame. Careful and ongoing attention
 needs to be paid to alleviating or mitigating this culture of blame. Those participating in the
 MPDSR process should be clearly protected against litigation and information obtained during
 death reviews should be kept confidential. Political will and national acceptance of the process at
 all levels are needed to decrease the blame culture surrounding MPDSR.

xvi
````

### Trang PDF 19

````text
Executive summary


Countries should be supported wherever necessary to adopt a comprehensive legal and regulatory
framework that creates a clear basis for the accountability that the MPDSR process seeks to
achieve. The Nigerian case study documents the process and challenges faced in the process of
creating a protective legal environment, and how the challenges were successfully addressed.

Standardizing global reporting of MPDSR implementation
Despite the variability between regions in data on MPDSR implementation collected and reported
globally, this report confirms that many countries make data available to help global, national and
subnational benchmarking.

Overall, more data were available from the WHO African Region than from any other WHO region.
However, the quality of data were variable. Questions used in surveys varied, not only between
the agencies leading those surveys, but also between surveys conducted by the same agency.
It is therefore difficult to analyse changing trends in MPDSR implementation.

United Nations agencies and other partners working in maternal, perinatal and newborn health
should actively support the standardization of tools and indicators used to assess MPDSR
implementation at national and subnational levels. These agencies and partners should work
towards closer integration between MPDSR and existing national health management
information systems (HMIS), and towards strengthening links with civil registration and vital
statistics (CRVS). This would enable better subnational, national, regional and global reporting
on MPDSR implementation and its outcomes.

MPDSR efforts are oriented to improve access to high-quality care for better maternal and
newborn health outcomes. This requires not only accurate data collection and review of cases of
maternal and perinatal deaths, but also detailed responses that address causes of death in
specific settings. In the following chapters we present details of this sustained practice where
staff involved at national, district, facility and community level had a role to play in the process.
Health systems are complex environments and require a high degree of customized solutions
based on the context. There are no magic bullets but recommendations that need to be adapted
to each context. Changes have already been achieved by the commitment of many who started
this process at a small scale, and continued believing in a better possible health system
protecting all women and babies.


 xvii
````

### Trang PDF 21

````text
1. Introduction

1.1 Background
Currently, there are 4.5 million maternal and newborn deaths and stillbirths every year, and
progress in reducing that number has plateaued since 2015 (1). Accelerated action is needed to
achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and other global targets.

One such challenge is understanding exactly why a woman died during or immediately after
pregnancy and childbirth or why a baby died before it was born or in the first month of life – a
crucial first step towards preventing other women and babies dying in the same way. In addition
to understanding the medical causes of death, it is also important to know the “how, why, where,
who and what” of a woman’s or baby’s death. The woman’s and baby’s personal stories – and
underlying contributory factors such as delays in seeking and accessing care that may have
contributed to their deaths – are essential parts of these stories, and must be taken into account
by policy-makers and managers when improving their health systems.

The maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response (MPDSR) system has been designed
to enable these tragic stories to be told, to improve the quality of care provided to women and
their babies, and to reduce the unacceptably high burden of maternal and neonatal mortality.
MPDSR can support achievement of the Ending Preventable Maternal Mortality (EPMM) and
Every Newborn Action Plan (ENAP) milestones and coverage targets and contribute to achieving
the SDG mortality targets (1).

1.2 What is MPDSR?
MPDSR is an approach promoted by the World Health Organization (WHO) and partners to end
preventable maternal and newborn mortality and stillbirths. MPDSR follows a continuous cycle
of notification, review, analysis and response (Fig. 1.1). It works by involving a diverse range of
stakeholders in the process of identifying suspected maternal or perinatal deaths, selecting
cases to analyse, understanding why, how and where the deaths occurred, and taking action to
prevent similar deaths occurring in the future, while simultaneously monitoring progress in
implementing recommendations for change. MPDSR covers maternal deaths, antepartum and
intrapartum stillbirths, and early and late neonatal deaths.

Fig. 1.1 The MPDSR cycle

 monitor response notify deaths


 Surveillance
 and
 response

 Analyze and Review maternal
 make recommendations and perinatal deaths
````

### Trang PDF 22

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 MPDSR complements other information systems, such as Integrated Disease Surveillance and
 Response (IDSR), national systems for civil registration and vital statistics (CRVS) and health
 management information systems (HMIS), but the extent of integration of MPDSR with these
 existing systems varies from one country to another.

 MPDSR is a surveillance and quality improvement system that includes deaths at facility and
 community levels. Community engagement in MPDSR can provide opportunities for community
 members to collaborate with health workers to explore and address the contributors to maternal
 and perinatal deaths. Community engagement is anchored in global policies and guidelines, such
 as the WHO technical guidelines on MDSR (2), the Global strategy for women’s, children’s and
 adolescents’ health (3) and the WHO Commission on Information and Accountability for Women’s
 and Children’s Health.

 In 2021, WHO published MPDSR materials to support implementation to complement existing
 technical guidance documents (4); since then, WHO has promoted a combined approach for
 maternal and perinatal deaths.

 As shown in Fig. 1.2 and described in more detail below, MPDSR is rooted in global efforts to
 improve maternal health, which started to gain momentum after the Safe Motherhood
 Conference in Nairobi in 1987. Please refer to Annex 1 for more information.
````

### Trang PDF 23

````text
1. Introduction


The key principles guiding the operation of the MPDSR system are summarized in Box 1.1.

Box 1.1 Key principles guiding the operation of the MPDSR system


 • Notification and investigation of all suspected maternal and perinatal deaths within
 24 hours in health facilities, or within 48 hours when a woman or a baby dies in the community.
 • Zero reporting when no suspected maternal and perinatal deaths have occurred.
 • Review of all maternal deaths and selection of cases to review for perinatal deaths based
 on top causes of death approach, thematic approach, random selection and most
 preventable cases.
 • Timely review of all probable maternal and perinatal deaths to understand the cause of
 death and the circumstances that led to death at the health facility or in the community.
 • Immediate recommendations, where possible, to enable health facilities and communities
 to prevent similar deaths, ensuring that key messages and information reach people who
 can make a difference. ­
 • Continuous monitoring of the functioning of MPDSR at subnational and national levels,
 including monitoring the implementation of recommendations.
 • Timely reviews and analyses, at national and subnational levels, to identify trends and patterns.
 • Timely communication of findings to prompt action at different levels through publication
 of findings and recommendations at national level and routine meetings with quality of care
 and MNH committees.
 Source: Adapted from WHO, 2016 (5).


Phased approach to MPDSR implementation
Implementing an MPDSR system can seem daunting. Using a phased approach that breaks the
process into more manageable pieces can be helpful. Achieving key benchmarks will show that
progress towards the final goal is being made. In the many countries where some components of
the MPDSR system have already been initiated, the next steps for strengthening and expanding
the system can be developed. Figure 1.3 provides an example of a phased approach. However,
the exact steps in a phased general introduction of the system will depend on the situation in
each country; for instance, some countries may prefer to start in urban areas, whereas others
may prefer to start with a group of districts. Figure 1.3 also shows a typical progression when
scaling up a national system. The horizontal arrow shows the expansion of the places where
deaths are identified: from only government or other selected facilities to all facilities, and finally
to complete coverage including the community.
````

````text
 Facility-based deaths are usually easier to capture than community-based deaths, and MPDSR
 should be implemented in all health facilities at a minimum. However, the long-term goal should
 be to identify all deaths, and the upper arrow in the figure captures progression toward that goal.
 The vertical arrow shows expansion of the geographical coverage of MPDSR. In this example, the
 system starts in urban areas only, then expands to include a sample of entire districts and finally
 the entire nation. The depth of the review process is shown in the centre of the figure, from a
 superficial review of a sample of deaths to a deeper review of all deaths.

 Time to respond report
 In 2016, WHO published the Time to respond report (5), outlining the implementation status of
 MDSR. In 2015, WHO and the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) conducted a global
 survey of national MDSR systems. The survey questionnaire, circulated to all WHO Member
 States, examined the status of MDSR implementation, including the availability of MDSR-related
 policies for maternal death notification and review, the availability of national and subnational
 MDSR committees, and active death surveillance and reporting. Responses were received from
 67 countries, of which 64 were lower-middle-income countries. Supplementary information for
 the report was taken from WHO’s sexual, reproductive, maternal, newborn, child and adolescent
 health (SRMNCAH) policy survey database.

 Member States were also invited to share information about successful implementation of MDSR
 in the form of case studies, describing how barriers were overcome and highlighting innovative
 approaches. These case studies, presented as “voices from the field”, included many personal
 stories of maternal deaths shared by civil society organizations (CSOs). In this report we take a
 deep dive into MPDSR implementation in eight countries to understand common factors of
 implementation in different context.

 Ongoing work to support global MPDSR implementation
 WHO convenes a global MPDSR Technical Working Group (TWG), which provides technical expertise
 and helps align the implementation at country level based on latest evidence. The MPDSR TWG has
4
````

### Trang PDF 25

````text
1. Introduction


35 institutional partners, and its focus areas include research, humanitarian and fragile settings,
capacity-building (see Annex 2 for further information), monitoring and evaluation, and dissemination.

1.3 About this report
What is the purpose of this report?
This report builds on the 2016 Time to respond report (5) to provide an updated global progress
report on the status of MPDSR implementation, using existing data sources plus eight in-depth
country case studies.

This report is intended to galvanize countries and the global health community to continue
strengthening MPDSR at country level until the entire MPDSR process is robust, well functioning
and being routinely used to respond to maternal and perinatal deaths.

Who is this report for?
This report is intended for a global audience of policy-makers, programme managers, health
workers, CSOs, professional societies, academics, bilateral and multilateral agencies, and
foundations working in maternal, perinatal and newborn health.

What is the structure of this report?
Chapter 2 describes different methods used to produce the content of the report. Chapter 3 presents
the findings of the secondary analysis of global and regional data and published MPDSR reports
provided by United Nations organizations and other stakeholders covering different aspects of
MPDSR implementation. It covers MPDSR policies and programmes at national, subnational and
facility levels, and variations across the six WHO regions. Chapter 3 also includes information
about community engagement in MPDSR, capacity-building packages and other training-related
activities, and the impact of coronavirus disease (COVID-19).

Chapters 4–11 present the findings of eight country case studies, each providing an overview of
the status of MPDSR implementation in the country with additional details on a specific part of
the MPDSR process. The final chapter summarizes opportunities and challenges for future
MPDSR implementation at a global level.

 maternal death. Geneva: World Health Organization; 2013 (https://iris.who.int/handle/10665/87340).
````

### Trang PDF 27

````text
2. Methodology


This chapter describes the methods used to discover the extent of MPDSR implementation at
policy and programme levels, to gauge community engagement in MPDSR, and to conduct the
country case studies.

During the development of this report, an Inter-Agency Steering Committee was established to:
provide feedback on planned activities, study protocols and tools, contribute to topics for
country case studies, and share available data on MPDSR implementation.

2.1 Data sources and analyses to document MPDSR policies
and implementation

MPDSR-related policies
Periodic surveys are conducted by WHO to track Member States’ progress in adopting WHO
recommendations on national health policies, strategies and guidelines relating to SRMNCAH.
Data from surveys conducted in 2009–2010, 2011–2012, 2013–2014 and 2016 were included in
the 2016 Time to respond report (1).

The most recent SRMNCAH policy surveys, in 2023 and 2018–2019 (2), were conducted online
using a modular format (MNH, child health, adolescent health, reproductive health, gender-based
violence and cross-cutting areas). Data were collected in each country by the WHO country office
through interviews with the national Ministry of Health and other United Nations organizations.
Relevant documents (policies, guidelines, laws, etc.) were collected and staff with appropriate
expertise were designated as responsible for completion of each module. For this report, data
from the modules on MNH and cross-cutting areas were extracted and analysed according to the
WHO regions.

UNFPA conducts periodic surveys in up to 32 countries, supported by its Maternal Health
Thematic Fund, with a focus on sub-Saharan Africa. Its most recent surveys are from 2015, 2016,
2017, 2018 and 2020. Along with other information, these surveys collect data on the number of
maternal deaths routinely identified in health facilities and communities that were notified to the
central level, and the number reviewed and reported centrally. Survey questionnaires are
completed by the MDSR focal points in each country’s Ministry of Health. The sources of
information about maternal deaths available to the focal points vary from country to country and
may include data from the national HMIS, and IDSR- or MDSR-specific monthly or quarterly
reports. National and United Nations maternal health experts review the data for consistency and
completeness, and transfer the data annually to the Ministry of Health and the Maternal Health
Thematic Fund coordinating team at UNFPA headquarters for compilation. For this report, the
data were analysed by the WHO regions where the Fund’s activities are implemented.

MPDSR implementation in countries
Data on programmatic implementation of MPDSR at regional and national levels were obtained
from several sources and were analysed using Microsoft Excel, by region.

East and Southern Africa: Data were collected by UNFPA, WHO and UNICEF through an online
survey sent to 23 countries, plus Zanzibar, by the UNFPA, WHO and UNICEF regional offices.


 7
````

### Trang PDF 28

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 UNFPA, WHO and UNICEF country staff worked with the MPDSR focal person in the national
 Ministry of Health to submit responses. Similar questions in English and French were used in
 three surveys (2016, 2018 and 2021). In 2021, a few questions were added concerning the impact
 of COVID-19 on MPDSR. Preliminary results were shared with country and regional
 representatives for feedback before publication (3).

 South-East Asia: MPDSR implementation information was collected by the WHO Regional Office
 for South-East Asia at different levels of the health system. Self-administered questionnaires
 were designed for use at national, subnational and facility levels to cover different aspects of
 MPDSR implementation. National and subnational questionnaires were available both online (via
 SurveyMonkey) and on paper. Paper-based questionnaires were used at facility level. Data were
 collected from 1 February to 18 April 2020 in 10 countries (Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, Indonesia,
 Maldives, Myanmar, Nepal, Sri Lanka, Thailand and Timor-Leste). A structured questionnaire was
 also developed to assess the content and quality of national MDSR/MPDSR reports. Data were
 analysed by WHO using both qualitative and quantitative methods.

 Special data collection on COVID-19 in Asia–Pacific: A rapid stock-taking process was
 undertaken to gauge the impact of COVID-19 on MPDSR by UNFPA. Data were collected through
 a survey that used a qualitative format, with questions developed in consultation with a core
 group and refined in consultation with UNFPA’s in-country sexual and reproductive health focal
 points. Data were collected through a SurveyMonkey link sent by email to the focal points, who
 were asked to complete the survey in partnership with those responsible for MPDSR. Questions
 sought to identify disruptions of general maternal and newborn services, and of the MPDSR
 system (or the reporting system in place, if not MPDSR) both before and after the commencement
 of the COVID-19 pandemic. The survey used a Likert scale to measure respondents’ agreement
 with various statements regarding MPDSR practices and service provision or disruptions. The
 scale used was from “never” to “always”, and respondents had the opportunity to comment on all
 answers. In addition, some questions required written answers only. Quantitative data about
 maternal and perinatal deaths were not requested because of delays in reporting and in
 processes for accessing verified data on deaths (4).

 2.2 Methodology for reviewing community engagement in
 MPDSR
 WHO commissioned a realist review based on both published and grey literature. Three broad
 search terms were used to identify relevant articles: “community engagement”, “maternal and
 perinatal death” and “surveillance and response” (and variations of these) (see Mbou et al. [5] for
 a complete list of the search terms). The literature review included all countries, with no language
 limitations for the search. Six databases were searched (MEDLINE, Embase, Scopus, Global
 health, CINAHL Plus and Web of Science) for papers published between 2004, when WHO
 launched Beyond the numbers (6), and August 2022. Members of the global MPDSR TWG were
 contacted to help identify additional published and grey literature on community engagement in
 MPDSR. An online search was conducted using the key search terms to identify additional
 published and grey literature. In addition, reference lists were hand-searched and followed up
 with authors for additional information. Nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) that are
 implementing MPDSR projects at community level were also contacted to find additional grey
 literature. The NGOs provided grey literature on a rolling basis until May 2023.

 A detailed protocol of the methodology has been published separately (5).


8
````

### Trang PDF 29

````text
2. Methodology

2.3 Methodology for country case studies
Following preliminary consultations with WHO regional offices, Cameroon, Colombia, Nigeria,
North Macedonia, Pakistan and Sri Lanka were selected for in-depth case studies on MPDSR
implementation. The key focus area for each case study was identified in consultation with
the countries.

A global advisory group, comprising staff from WHO, UNICEF and UNFPA and global experts in
MPDSR, was set up to review and provide feedback on the study protocol and data collection
instruments, and to provide inputs at various stages of the project.

A national consultant was recruited by each WHO country office to conduct the case study, using
a mixed-methods approach. Using a virtual platform, each national consultant was oriented by a
team from WHO headquarters on the goals and objectives of the assessment, the methods to be
followed for primary and secondary data collection, including obtaining informed consent, and
the standard templates to be used for reporting.

A common study protocol and data collection tools were used in the selected countries, with
adaptation and translations where required, to address country-specific topics identified by each
country. These were approved by the appropriate research ethics committees in each participating
country. In each country, the national consultant reviewed available documents relevant to
MPDSR implementation in general, and those relating specifically to the country case study
focus. Key information on MPDSR current and historical aspects of implementation in the
country and on the specific focus area of the country case study were extracted using
standardized data collection tools. The national consultant reviewed documents prior to
conducting interviews with key stakeholders and, when required, clarified or confirmed
information reported in available documents.

Between 15 and 20 key stakeholders in each country were selected in consultation with the national
Ministry of Health. They included: those involved with various stages of the implementation
process who were aware of the implementation’s challenges and solutions; those involved with
implementation at subnational or national level who were involved in one or more phases of the
MPDSR cycle; and representatives of professional organizations and CSOs working in health.

After obtaining informed consent, the national consultant conducted interviews either face-to-face,
using appropriate infection-prevention measures, or by telephone or videoconference, in the
language agreed with the interviewee in advance. All interviews were recorded and transcribed.
A topic guide was used to guide the interview and to explore: how ideas were conceived; the
decision-making processes; the approach to implementation, including training and local
adaptations; lessons learned; monitoring of implementation; and use of data for decision-making.
The interviewees also identified challenges faced, bottlenecks to implementation and adaptations
made. Strengths were identified as well as areas for improvement. Information from the
interviews was summarized using a standardized reporting template.

The overarching areas to be addressed in each country case study included: historical aspects of
MPDSR implementation; current status of national policies relating to MPDSR (notification, review
and response to maternal and/or newborn deaths and/or stillbirths); the current set-up for MPDSR
governance (committees, reporting, etc.); the status of MPDSR implementation (i.e. its scale); key
barriers to MPDSR implementation, and attempts made to tackle these; facilitators and key
enablers of MPDSR implementation; and key lessons learned from MPDSR implementation.


 9
````

### Trang PDF 30

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 In addition, the case studies explored the following country-specific questions.
 • What lessons have been learned from the Integrated Management System for Maternal and
 Perinatal Deaths in Cameroon?
 • What has been the experience of the Integrated Public Health Surveillance System for Safe
 Motherhood in Colombia?
 • What process has Nigeria followed in developing a MPDSR legal framework?
 • How did perinatal death reviews contribute to improving antenatal and perinatal health care in
 North Macedonia?
 • How has been the role of digital technology in Pakistan in the notification and reporting of
 maternal and perinatal deaths? What other roles could mobile health (mHealth) technologies
 play in strengthening MPDSR implementation?
 • What processes and procedures are followed in Sri Lanka in response to maternal and
 perinatal deaths?

 Transcripts of the interviews were analysed using manual inductive coding. Analyses were
 conducted at the policy and programme levels, triangulating all available information and
 perspectives from documents reviewed and interviews. Any differences in perspectives were
 resolved on a case-by-case basis.

 All country case study reports were developed collaboratively: national consultants worked closely
 with WHO teams in countries, regional offices and at WHO headquarters to finalize the reports.

 In addition to the six country case studies described above, two further studies were conducted
 in Bangladesh and Uganda.

 The Bangladesh case study used the same approach as the WHO-led case studies. It was led by
 UNFPA and focused on implementation of MPDSR at the community level.

 The Uganda case study, which showcases lessons learned from the implementation of MPDSR in
 refugee settlements, was conducted in collaboration between the global MPDSR TWG’s sub-working
 group on humanitarian and fragile settings and the Inter-Agency Working Group on Reproductive
 Health in Crises. This was a descriptive case study, examining MPDSR implementation in
 districts with refugee settlements. Desk reviews and key informant interviews were conducted
 with 15 participants, including representatives based in national and subnational offices of
 United Nations organizations (4), the Ministry of Health (1), international NGOs (7), health facility
 MPDSR focal points (2) and other organizations engaged in directly implementing or providing
 support to MPDSR within refugee settlements. Interviews were conducted virtually via Zoom in
 English, using a semi-structured interview guide, and synthesized using thematic content
 analysis.
````

````text
3.2 MPDSR implementation data from UNFPA databases
Data on MPDSR implementation between 2016 and 2020 were available from 30–39 countries
(number of reporting countries varied between years), mostly in sub-Saharan Africa, supported
by UNFPA’s Maternal Health Thematic Fund. Subnational committees able to implement MPDSR
were reportedly available in 92% of the 25 African countries in the 2020 survey, up from 76% in
 2018. In 2020, perinatal deaths were included under the MPDSR programme in 92% of these
 countries, up from 56% in 2018. MPDSR data were included in the national health information
system in 88%, maternal death notification was used as an indicator of performance of the system
 in 68%, and annual MPDSR reports available in 48% of these countries in 2020.
````

````text
Asia–Pacific
Of the 17 countries surveyed in 2021 in the UNFPA Asia Pacific region (4), 13 had implemented
 the full MPDSR system. Two had implemented MDSR but planned to implement the perinatal
 reporting component in the near future. One country uses the Demographic and Health
Surveys (DHS) to report mortality, and another uses a national system for reporting maternal
and perinatal deaths.

Of the 13 countries using the full MPDSR, seven had implemented the system throughout the
 country: four of these reported using MPDSR for more than five years, one for four to five years,
and two for two to three years. Six countries have implemented it across some but not all
 districts/divisions in the country.

 All 13 countries reported that national MPDSR committees and subcommittees exist. All but one
 reported having MPDSR operational guidelines, manuals or tools to assist with implementation
and ongoing training.

 Eleven of the 13 (85%) countries that used the MPDSR approach reported having a process in
 place for action following a death in the community. A commonly reported process was that of
deaths being reported to the village midwife or village leader, who then reported to the divisional
 health manager or nearest health facility. Verbal and social autopsies were then undertaken by
people deemed responsible at community level.

 In countries with strong MPDSR systems, such as Bangladesh, the Islamic Republic of Iran,
Myanmar, Nepal and Sri Lanka, and in China with its strong routine surveillance and data collection
systems that effectively monitor maternal and child mortality, all deaths were notified within
24–72 hours, with verbal autopsies being completed within two weeks of community-based deaths.
 19
````

### Trang PDF 40

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 The process of review at facility and central levels occurred regularly. Most countries reported
 difficulties with receiving regular information about deaths in the community, and with reporting
 of perinatal deaths where this had recently been introduced into the process. There were also
 reports of variations across districts, where deaths were routinely reported but the review
 process was less regular. These examples of irregular processes were attributed to lack of
 commitment from local governments to implementing the full cycle of MPDSR. Where MPDSR
 was not implemented across the whole country, issues around the implementation of perinatal
 death reporting and internal country conflicts (e.g. disputed zones) were mentioned, but it was
 generally unclear why MPDSR was not used throughout the whole country.

 3.3 Implementation challenges during the COVID-19 pandemic
 WHO has identified MPDSR as an essential SRMNCAH intervention to mitigate the indirect effects
 of COVID-19 on maternal and perinatal outcomes (5). Implementation of MPDSR during the
 COVID-19 pandemic was particularly challenging. All 17 countries responding to the 2021 survey
 in the Asia–Pacific region of UNFPA (4) reported various degrees of disruption, particularly at the
 beginning of the pandemic, when health systems were overwhelmed. Thirteen of the 17 countries
 were implementing MPDSR, while two were implementing MDSR. The most frequently reported
 issues related to notification and reporting, and completion of mortality reviews. Facility deaths
 continued to be reported, with some countries reporting a closer focus on them, as concern grew
 about the effects of COVID-19 on pregnant women. However, there was a significant reduction in
 notifications of deaths in the community. Numerous countries reported delays in receiving
 notifications, citing staff redeployment as the primary issue.

 Other challenges reported included:
 • health worker exhaustion and burnout;
 • verbal autopsies being halted because of families’ reluctance to accept health workers into
 their homes;
 • reluctance of personnel to perform autopsies if COVID-19 suspected or confirmed due to lack
 of personal protective equipment;
 • fewer human resources available because of COVID-19 illness or need to quarantine;
 • lack of timely collection of complete, high-quality information;
 • strict movement restrictions, with some parts of country completely inaccessible;
 • reduced MPDSR leadership and governance due to priority shift to pandemic response;
 • other burdens, including political and/or civil unrest.

 Positive outcomes relating to MPDSR implementation during the pandemic were also reported
 from countries in the UNFPA eastern and southern African and Asia–Pacific regions (3, 4).
 These included the ability to use virtual platforms to: develop protocols for care of pregnant
 women with COVID-19; conduct periodic death review meetings; implement capacity-building
 activities; and offer consultations to manage complications.

 3.4 Community engagement in MPDSR
 Community engagement in MPDSR can provide opportunities for community members to
 collaborate with health workers to explore and address the factors that contribute to maternal
 and perinatal deaths. Community engagement is anchored in global policies and guidelines,
 such as the WHO technical guidelines on MDSR (6), the Global strategy for women’s, children’s

20
````

### Trang PDF 41

````text
3. Findings from the secondary data on MPDSR policies and implementation


and adolescents’ health (7), the EPMM initiative (8) and the WHO Commission on Information and
Accountability for Women’s and Children’s Health (9). These guidelines and policies identify
community engagement as necessary for improving data collection on maternal deaths and
empowering communities to engage in social accountability for maternal and perinatal
mortality prevention.

Studies have shown that community members can be engaged in both surveillance and
response throughout the MPDSR cycle (10-13). This section explores how community
engagement functions as an integral part of this cycle.

Who is the community in the MPDSR context?
People with shared geography and social systems: community members living in the same locality
can engage in identifying and notifying deaths and undertaking death reviews (through social
autopsy, verbal autopsy or facility-based reviews), can report local factors that contribute to
deaths, and recommend actions to address these factors.

Bereaved family and community members: relatives of deceased women and babies, including
parents, can participate in MPDSR as a constituent of the community.

Community health workers (CHWs): known by many different names in different contexts, such as
community reproductive health agents (14) or accredited social health activists (15). CHWs are
typically trained to carry out some health-related activities but do not have a tertiary qualification
in health care (16) and are expected to liaise and facilitate communication between the health
system and community members (17). In many contexts, community members are involved in
selecting CHWs.

Community representatives can include village elders, local authorities, members of health facility
committees or other community groups, parent advocates, traditional birth attendants and
healers, and are appointed or selected on behalf of other community members. They can be
engaged in the MPDSR process of identifying and notifying deaths, performing death reviews in
health facilities or communities, and supporting the implementation of actions in response to
the review (11).

CSOs and other grass-roots organizations can participate on behalf of the community to advocate
for action with decision-makers or to represent community members on MPDSR committees (18).

Findings and implications
The literature search described “concerning community engagement in each part of the MPDSR
cycle” identified 7898 articles, of which 2919 were duplicates. After screening for relevant
inclusion criteria, 45 articles (from both published and grey literature) were included for data
extraction. This section summarizes findings from the 45 articles. Selected examples illustrate
how community engagement in MPDSR is implemented in different contexts and the outcomes
of the engagement process.
Mostlower-middleincome(40/45)countries.of theThirty-fourarticles reportingreportedanycommunityform of communitymembers beingengagementengagedwerein thefromidentification and
notification of maternal and perinatal deaths in the community. Twenty described some form of
community engagement in the review process, while 18 articles described community engagement
in the response steps of the MPDSR cycle. Nine articles reported community engagement in
monitoring the implementation of actions to prevent maternal and/or perinatal deaths.
````

### Trang PDF 42

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 MPDSR cycle: identification and notification examples
 A study in Malawi demonstrated the critical role played by CHWs in the identification process:
 in instances where CHWs were not involved in the identification of community deaths at the start
 of the community-linked maternal death review process, the review process failed (11). In addition,
 where a community does not respect or recognize the role and value of CHWs, they are less
 successful in supporting identification and notification efforts (19).

 Community engagement can improve the completeness of maternal and neonatal mortality data.
 Better estimates can be provided using a combination of community and facility surveillance
 systems than by relying on facility data alone (20-22). For instance, a study from Pakistan
 showed that prior to engaging community members to support notification, identification and
 reporting, maternal mortality was under-reported by 27% and neonatal mortality by 50% (23).

 MPDSR cycle: review of maternal and perinatal deaths examples
 Twenty articles reporting community engagement in the review process were identified.
 These included both maternal and perinatal death reviews and involved a range of community
 members and leaders.

 The verbal autopsy process has been expanded in some contexts beyond collecting data to
 determine the cause of death and is also used to engage community members in exploring social
 factors that could have contributed to the death (11, 24, 25). In most contexts, social autopsies
 are conducted as public community meetings, involving community members, health workers
 and other stakeholders to discuss any social barriers contributing to a death (12, 20, 26).

 In some contexts, community members are involved in the review of deaths in the community
 through verbal and social autopsies, as in Bangladesh (27) (see Chapter 4), or of deaths both in
 the community and in facilities, as in Malawi (11). The review process involves health workers and
 community members in discussions about specific maternal/perinatal deaths and reviewing any
 circumstances that contributed to the death.

 MPDSR cycle: response examples
 Eighteen articles reporting community engagement in supporting implementation of
 recommendations both in the community and in health facilities were reviewed.

 Overall, these studies suggested that after reviewing maternal or perinatal deaths to identify
 modifiable/avoidable factors that could prevent future mortality, recommendations should be
 made and implemented (28). A randomized controlled trial assessing community engagement in
 community-based health information systems in Sierra Leone demonstrated that making data
 available to community members during neonatal death reviews increased community
 willingness to engage in developing action plans and supporting their implementation (24, 29).
 Several studies demonstrated that community members engaged in reviews through verbal or
 social autopsies or community-based confidential enquiries can prioritize community-level
 actions to address the modifiable factors identified through the review process (10, 21, 24, 26).

 Several studies reported using the three-delay model to identify community-level delays that
 contribute to maternal deaths (21, 30, 31). A key recommendation often made is to educate or
 sensitize community members so as to decrease the first and second delays (Annex 1) (10, 12, 14, 32).
 Health workers proposed health education for community members as part of the response in
 MPDSR, in order to promote health seeking behaviour (12, 14, 32-34).


22
````

### Trang PDF 43

````text
3. Findings from the secondary data on MPDSR policies and implementation

3.5 Conclusion
In conclusion, the key components of community engagement and MPDSR are:
 • building relationships
 • training and supporting community informants
 • recognizing the value and contribution of community members
 • creating an enabling environment for engagement
 • managing blame culture
 • investing in research.

Community engagement requires respectful interaction between CHWs, community members and
the health system. Building on existing relationships is critical. At all levels of the MPDSR cycle the
community can be engaged to identify, notify, review and assist with the development and
implementation of action plans. These activities can and should be linked, motivating community
members to engage in the entire process. This requires investment of time, training, mentorship
and support with leadership at both national and district levels. Finally, managing the culture of
blame is as important in community-led MPDSR as it is at facility, district and national levels.
Communities, regardless of their setting, can and should be an integral part of the MPDSR cycle.


 23
````
