### Trang PDF 48

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 4.1 Introduction
 In 2010, Bangladesh implemented a maternal and perinatal death review system in one district (1)
 following WHO guidelines published in Beyond the numbers (2). That system provided a platform
 for notification of maternal and neonatal deaths and stillbirths from both community and facility
 levels. Maternal and perinatal death review committee meetings periodically reviewed such
 deaths and recommended actions for improvement. From 2016, the system was gradually scaled
 up to become the national MPDSR system after five years of pilot implementation in selected
 districts (3). Since then, the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare has led the implementation of
 MPDSR in all districts of Bangladesh, covering both community- and facility-level deaths at
 district and sub-district levels (1).

 This chapter presents the findings of a case study that assessed the past and current roles of
 community MPDSR in Bangladesh.

 4.2 Historical findings
 The 2010 pilot project demonstrated that the maternal and perinatal death review system
 effectively captured maternal and perinatal deaths in the district, and reporting of maternal
 deaths in the community improved significantly. It also showed the value of community-level
 verbal and social autopsies in identifying causes of death and underlying factors, such as delays
 in seeking care for women experiencing complications. The introduction of social autopsies
 improved knowledge, changed community attitudes towards maternal health care, and led to
 fewer home births (4). After the successful pilot, the Government, with support from development
 partners, scaled up the system in four more districts in 2011, and in 10 further districts in 2013.
 Experiences from these districts in capturing deaths and identifying causes of deaths were
 similar to those in the first pilot district. In 2016, the Government decided to scale up
 implementation across the country.

 In 2015, the Quality Improvement Secretariat facilitated the formation of working groups to
 develop key components of the guidelines on MNH, including expanding the maternal and
 perinatal death review system to become MPDSR. MPDSR guidelines, a training manual, a pocket
 handbook for health workers and other tools were prepared in collaboration with the Directorate
 General of Health Services and the Directorate General of Family Planning, and approved in 2016
 by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. MPDSR knowledge hubs were established at the
 subnational level, and MPDSR data were incorporated into District Health Information System 2
 (DHIS2), the routine health information system of the Directorate General of Health Services (1).

 The Government included MPDSR in its Fourth Health, Population and Nutrition Sector Programme
 Plan 2017–2022 and MPDSR forms part of the Maternal Health Strategy 2019–2030 (5).

 The maternal health programme office of the Directorate General of Health Services is the leading
 implementer of the MPDSR programme in Bangladesh, in collaboration with the maternal, child,
 reproductive and adolescent health programme of the Directorate General of Family Planning and
 the Quality Improvement Secretariat. The latter is responsible for the quality aspect of the
 MPDSR and is involved in developing tools and guidelines, monitoring and capacity-building.

 At the national level, MPDSR is monitored by the National Core Committee. Different quality
 improvement committees at division, district and sub-district levels are the key platforms for
 reviewing and responding to the deaths. Focal personnel for MPDSR are assigned at each level.


28
````

### Trang PDF 49

````text
4. Case study: Bangladesh


Development partners, including UNFPA, UNICEF and WHO, are closely engaged, providing
technical and implementation support.

National MPDSR reports, published annually from 2019 (6-9), present the analytical and
implementation findings from MPDSR at district and sub-district levels.

After five years of implementation, the national MPDSR guidelines were revised in 2022–2023 to
reflect new global implementation guidelines, including new sections on stillbirth reviews at
national level, addressing blame culture and MPDSR in humanitarian situations, and a closer
focus on implementing the response element of the framework (10).

4.3 Methods
Using the protocol described in Chapter 2, 22 in-depth interviews with health workers and field staff
were conducted in two districts of Bangladesh that had used MPDSR for a few years, and with
three national programme officers. All interviewees gave written consent, and each interview was
conducted in person and lasted up to 30 minutes. All interviews were audio recorded and notes
were taken. Summary transcriptions were translated from Bangla into English.

Prior to the interviews, all recent documents on MPDSR published in Bangladesh, including
national guidelines, tools, manuals, pocket handbooks, national reports and research articles,
were reviewed to gather background information.

4.4 Implementation of community MPDSR
Different levels of health-service providers and field-level health staff are responsible for
MPDSR implementation.

At primary, secondary and tertiary government health facilities, health-service providers report
maternal deaths, neonatal deaths and stillbirths to the MPDSR focal point in the facility, who in
turn reports via DHIS2. After notification, health-service providers perform a death review, and the
MPDSR committee determines causes of deaths, provides feedback and makes recommendations
for appropriate actions.

Community MPDSR, a key component of the system, follows a different pathway (11-19):
Frontline health workers from the health and family planning departments are assigned to a
catchment area for reporting. They collect information about and report each maternal death,
neonatal death and stillbirth from households in the community in their area; deaths should be
reported within three days. After notification, they report back to the community clinic to confirm
information about the death. A community verbal autopsy should be held within 7–15 days, and a
social autopsy within 15–30 days, for all reported maternal deaths and 10% of neonatal deaths.
Although stillbirths are notified, no verbal or social autopsies are currently held for stillbirths.
Both verbal and social autopsies are conducted by the field-level supervisor of the health and
family planning department. The cause of death is reported at the upazila (sub-district) and district
levels by the MPDSR focal person and medical specialists (4). Figure 4.1 provides further details.


 29
````

### Trang PDF 50

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 Fig. 4.2 Three stages of community MPDSR in Bangladesh: targets and responsibilities

 Death notification Within 3 days

 Ensure 100% death notification (maternal deaths, neonatal deaths and stillbirths)
 Responsibility: health assistant/family welfare assistant
 Data entry in death registration system of DHIS2 using individual records from community clinics/health
 and family planning centres


 Community verbal autopsy Within 7–15 days

 Verbal autopsy for 100% for maternal deaths and 10% for stillbirths and neonatal deaths
 Responsibility: health inspector/assistant health inspector / family planning inspector
 Death review and identify cause of death


 Community social autopsy Within 15–30 days

 Social autopsy for 100% of maternal deaths and 10% of stillbirths and neonatal deaths
 Responsibility: health inspector/assistant health inspector/family planning inspector
 Data entry by health inspector/assistant health inspector (in DHIS2)


 Field-level health workers receive orientation and training on death notification at sub-district
 level, and refresher training at monthly coordination meetings. A pocket handbook on MPDSR
 provides guidance on defining death and the process of reporting, and the health workers have
 access to an online video on MPDSR.

 At the national level, focal persons are oriented on the MPDSR aspects of verbal and social autopsy.
 First-line supervisors receive training on verbal and social autopsy.

 4.5 Community death notification, registration, validation and
 monitoring
 Frontline health staff use a wide range of networks, such as community groups, community support
 groups and local leaders, to ensure that all maternal and neonatal deaths and stillbirths occurring
 in the community are reported. Deaths are reported quickly to health workers by a phone call or
 SMS text. A health worker visits the deceased’s household to obtain information about the death
 and fill out a death notification slip. Within 72 hours of the death, the health worker reports this
 information to the community clinic (primary health centre). As soon as the death notification slip
 is submitted, the health worker in charge of the clinic register enters the information into DHIS2.
 A unique registration number is generated, which is used later for entering the verbal autopsy data.


30
````

### Trang PDF 51

````text
4. Case study: Bangladesh


Field-level health workers conduct regular health programmes in the community, including
satellite clinics, immunization and household visits. They are informed in these circumstances if
any deaths have occurred. Health workers interviewed said that their relationship with the
community was respectful and cordial and that their communities were proactive in providing
timely information. Health managers repeatedly emphasized the need for accurate and timely
reporting of deaths, particularly in the monthly coordination meetings.

 Community [members], most of the time, treat us as or call us their doctor, so we have
 a very good [relationship] with our community, where we have worked for a long time.
 We have our catchment areas where we routinely work; it’s quite easy for us to [obtain
 information about a] death. (Health supervisor)

 [Previously, few] deaths were reported because we didn’t have a functional, structured
 system, but now we are oriented to MPDSR and we know that deaths need to be
 reported. (Grass-roots health worker)

 We feel encouraged; our [boss] always talks about ensuring death notification in the
 monthly coordination meeting and wants the actual number of deaths from us. This
 improves our … reporting capacity. (Grass-roots health worker)

 Seeing the low number of deaths reported, I always pay close attention to deaths from
 the community. (District health manager)

 I have started to send the district hospital’s monthly deaths details to the upazila
 [sub-district] so that they can [check whether] any of those deaths [have not been]
 reported. (District health manager)

Validation of a death notified by the community involves four steps: a health worker visits the
household to validate the information received before completing a death notification slip; the
first-line supervisor validates the death before conducting the verbal autopsy; at the monthly
MPDSR meeting, a district or sub-district manager discusses the death and cross-checks the
death numbers; and the statistician at sub-district level receives a copy of the completed death
notification slip, which they use to check that the death is correctly reported in DHIS2.

Monitoring/tracking and follow-up
MPDSR is followed, not only in the monthly sub-district and district health meetings, but also
by the quality improvement committee. The monthly MPDSR findings are shared regularly, and
district and sub-district managers provide direction based on a comparison between the death
actually notified and the number expected. MPDSR focal persons play a significant role in
monitoring death notification and follow-up. District focal persons report deaths to a sub-district
focal person. The interviews conducted for this case study revealed that sub-district focal persons
follow up on death notification with the field-level health workers. An MPDSR focal person also
alerts the sub-district and district health managers about progress made. A dashboard
available within DHIS2 is monitored by both the MPDSR focal person and managers for death
notification status.


 31
````

### Trang PDF 52

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 We do routinely monitor the data, especially in the monthly meeting, where the upazila
 death numbers are presented and verified. (Sub-district MPDSR focal person)

 In the monthly district coordination meeting, the upazila managers present the
 MPDSR findings, and we try to understand how the number of deaths reported
 [compares with] the expected number. We always try to provide feedback based on
 [our joint] discussions. (District MPDSR focal person)

 We often meet virtually in videoconferences at the national level, [joined by] district
 teams including the civil surgeon, the MPDSR focal person, consultants, statistician
 and technical partners. We discuss the death numbers and check [that they are
 represented] in the online reporting. (National MPDSR focal person)

 4.6 Community verbal and social autopsies
 Verbal autopsies
 After a death is notified in DHIS2, the supervisor of the area where the death was reported is assigned
 to conduct a death review in the community. At an upazila health complex (primary health centre),
 the MPDSR focal person assigns another trained health supervisor from the catchment area to do
 the verbal autopsy. The household visit is conducted within 7–15 days of a death in the community
 and a copy of the death notification slip from the upazila office is used to confirm the death.

 The health supervisor confirms that the death accords with the operational definition and identifies
 the primary respondent to be interviewed. The respondent is carefully selected (considering
 presence at the time of death and related events) to ensure accurate information is obtained.
 In the case of neonatal death or stillbirth, the mother is the best respondent. The purpose of the
 interview is explained and written consent is obtained before the interview. The interviewer must
 maintain a no-blame approach and not pressure the respondent for a response. The respondent
 is not obliged to answer all questions and may stop the interview at any time. A structured form
 is used to collect the data. Interviews take around 30 minutes on average. The interviewer
 assures the respondent of the confidentiality of their responses, which will be shared only with
 the designated authority. The completed verbal autopsy form is submitted to the family planning
 officer or MPDSR focal person at the upazila health complex and documented by the upazila
 statistician. The area health inspector routinely follows up on verbal autopsies, along with the
 MPDSR focal person.

 We don’t delay in doing a verbal autopsy: as soon as we are assigned, we go to the
 community and collect the information. (Health supervisor)

 I observe verbal autopsies in the community, especially for maternal deaths, as much
 as I can, which in turn helps me to understand why a mother died, and at the same
 time the quality of the data are also ensured. (Sub-district MPDSR focal person)

 After completing a verbal autopsy [and] returning to the upazila, I recheck the form
 with the support of the MPDSR focal person to see if I missed anything [out]. If that
 happens, I immediately make a phone call to the deceased’s household and try to
 gather the missing information. (Health inspector supervisor)

32
````

### Trang PDF 53

````text
4. Case study: Bangladesh

Social autopsies
In Bangladesh, the social autopsy is a community contribution to MPDSR. It reveals the
community’s perspective and the challenges they face, and seeks solutions using their local
knowledge. Social autopsies take a no-blame approach, focusing on social factors relating to
maternal and newborn deaths in the community. The community (including community groups,
neighbours and the deceased’s family members) collectively determines the root causes of death
by systematically examining relevant medical and social factors, and develops strategies to
prevent similar occurrences in the future. The social autopsy process also increases community
knowledge about common complications of childbirth and when care should be sought.

When social autopsies were first introduced in 2010, there were challenges in ensuring the quality
of facilitation by health workers, and leadership by the community in implementing decisions.
These challenges have been largely overcome by better training in facilitation and close interaction
with communities. Currently, social autopsies are conducted for 65–70% of maternal deaths
occurring in the community.

In terms of process, the first-line supervisor of an area facilitates a social autopsy in
coordination with the health and family planning departments. Following the guidelines, the
facilitator maintains a no-blame approach. Grass-roots government health workers, NGO workers,
community groups and community support groups support the facilitator in organizing the
community event. Generally, a location close to the deceased’s or a neighbour’s home is
selected. Prior permission for the social autopsy is obtained from the deceased’s family.
Typically, 40–50 participants from 20–30 households participate. Members of respective
community groups and community support groups attend the meeting, including teachers,
religious leaders, Union Parishad (rural council) members, health workers and volunteers
(both male and female). Adolescent boys and girls are also included, as are pregnant women,
newly married women and male heads of households.

A social autopsy is organized 15–30 days after a death. The average duration is 45 minutes to an
hour; lunchtime or afternoon are usually preferred.

The supervisor starts the session with questions about circumstances before the death.
The community shares what the complications were, what the family did at that time, the
decision-making dynamics about seeking appropriate care, and what happened during the
management process. The supervisor then starts a discussion exploring any gaps and
challenges. After the family and community have responded, the health supervisor shares
information about maternal or neonatal complications, danger signs, referral and the scope of
treatment in referral facilities. Community members interact and can make decisions to change
their behaviour in future. The presence of community leaders triggers decision-making and
accountability for better response.

 Before a social autopsy session, … my health assistant [informs] the community [of]
 the date and time, and invites people to join. My role is to facilitate the session and
 help the community to understand [what] happened. (Health supervisor)

 I have seen a few social autopsy sessions: they look similar to a courtyard meeting,
 but here the community participates [together to deal with] a death … in their own
 community. (District MPDSR focal person)


 33
````

### Trang PDF 54

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 A social autopsy is a change event: it allows the community to think, plan and respond
 appropriately to prevent unwanted maternal or perinatal deaths. (District health manager)

 Our community [members] are always welcoming; they are supportive and want to
 listen to us and explore issues to be improved. So, we feel comfortable talking to the
 community, and it’s very interactive. (Sub-district health supervisor)

 We use social autopsy to show the social response [from] the community. [Engaging]
 the community is our strength. The community thinks it is important … to learn and
 know [what happened, so the issue of blame does not arise]. (Sub-district MPDSR
 focal person)

 Social autopsy data and quality control
 Although no separate data are generated from social autopsies, information obtained from them
 helps to inform response plans for the future. The supervisor delivers a note of the meeting to the
 MPDSR focal person at the upazila health complex. Social autopsy sessions are randomly monitored
 by the MPDSR focal person and district managers. Besides the supervisor, grass-roots level and
 other relevant health workers also participate to improve the quality of the autopsy process. The
 local elite’s presence is a catalyst for maintaining the quality of the session. The upazila reports
 online, via DHIS2, whether the social autopsy was monitored by the responsible officers.

 I have joined social autopsy sessions randomly, and my field team is well aware that
 I may join any of those, so the quality of social autopsies is maintained. (Sub-district
 health manager)

 The supervisor noted some of the gaps and challenges, and [how] the community
 decided and planned to improve the situation. Usually, delays are captured, which
 helps us to discuss during the MPDSR cause analysis and response plan development.
 (Sub-district MPDSR focal person)

 Cause of death assignment
 A general duty doctor or a consultant declares the cause of a death from verbal autopsy reports,
 following ICD-10. Cause assignment at the divisional level is also held at least once a year.
 Consultants from teaching hospitals are also invited to assign causes of deaths, with discussion
 if required among colleagues. According to the new guidelines, causes of maternal and perinatal
 deaths can be assigned at upazila level. Causes of deaths and relevant data from the verbal
 autopsy are entered in DHIS2.

 Immediately after the verbal autopsy is conducted, we request our consultant to
 review the form and assign the causes of death. Once it is assigned, we report the
 assigned causes [via] DHIS2. (Sub-district MPDSR focal person)


34
````

### Trang PDF 55

````text
4. Case study: Bangladesh


 [The Directorate General of Health Services and the Quality Improvement Secretariat]
 jointly organized five divisional-level cause analysis workshops on MPDSR, where we
 got the opportunity to see in depth what the districts have done, the situation of
 deaths in the districts, and what the challenges are. I attended one of those
 workshops last year. (National MPDSR national focal person)

 Our consultants in obstetrics/gynaecology and paediatrics play a significant role in
 assigning causes of deaths from the community verbal autopsy and facility death
 review. I myself assigned several causes of maternal deaths as an obstetrics/
 gynaecology expert during the pilot phase of MPDSR in Bangladesh. (Member of the
 obstetrics and gynaecology professional society)

 In 2021, we took the initiative, with UNFPA’s support, to run a cause analysis workshop
 at the divisional level, to accelerate the process of MPDSR after disruption due to
 COVID-19. Consultants were divided into groups and went through [all the completed
 forms from the districts and] assigned causes of death. (National MPDSR focal person)

4.7 Factors contributing to success
Several factors have contributed to the successful implementation of community MPDSR in
Bangladesh.
 • A no-blame approach in the MPDSR death notification process.
 • The community participates actively in reporting deaths in the community.
 • MPDSR is integrated into the government health system.
 • All deaths are entered in DHIS2; a dashboard on MPDSR helps to track deaths by district and
 sub-district.
 • MPDSR is discussed in the monthly coordination meetings of the health and family planning
 departments.
 • Anonymity and confidentiality are maintained during community verbal autopsies.
 • Following a verbal autopsy, the cause of death is assigned by medical doctors at district or
 sub-district level.
 • MPDSR focal persons are assigned at national, district and sub-district levels, and MPDSR
 committees meet regularly.
 • Social autopsies provide a platform for community engagement and participation, and for
 increasing knowledge, informing decision-making and developing community ownership and
 accountability in order to prevent every preventable maternal and perinatal death.
 • Periodic video conferencing between national-level actors and the districts helps to increase
 MPDSR implementation and routine monitoring.


 35
````

### Trang PDF 56

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 4.8 Challenges in community MPDSR
 There are challenges in covering all communities nationwide. There are gaps in human resources at
 the grass-roots level with some areas having no assigned health workers. These include hill tracts,
 tea plantations, wetlands (haor) and river belts, which are extremely difficult to reach. In those areas,
 health workers need transport, which is costly, and there is no incentive for reporting deaths, so
 MPDSR depends on notification of deaths by the community or other nongovernment workers.
 I am the only field-level health worker in my catchment area, I am supposed to visit
 the households once a month, but it’s become tough because I have to cover the
 vacant area as well. Therefore, I have difficulty reaching out to the community for
 [information about deaths]. (Grass-roots health worker)

 We have several vacancies in the field level, which [causes] difficulty in capturing
 deaths and reporting MPDSR from the community. (Sub-district health manager)

 There are also reporting deficiencies in some urban areas where there are no grass-roots health
 workers from the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare and only a few health workers linked to
 the city/municipal administrations: too few to report deaths in the wider urban area.

 We have low coverage in the district and sub-district urban areas; only a few
 vaccinators are working [and they are not] able to [notify] deaths routinely. (District
 MPDSR focal person)

 We have several nongovernment health workers and volunteers working in different
 urban projects. They are supposed to report a maternal or perinatal death to the Civil
 Surgeon’s office or at the upazila health complex, but we are not getting the actual
 numbers. (District MPDSR focal person)

 Several areas need further support to strengthen death notification. Maternal deaths are being
 well reported, but low numbers of neonatal deaths and stillbirths are reported.

 In some cases, the cause of death cannot be assigned, due to either insufficient data collection
 or lack of information. Some deaths are reported late, delaying the verbal autopsy. There can also
 be difficulties in reaching out to the community, especially in hard-to-reach areas. There is no
 support for travel costs, which is reported to be a challenge. Inadequate reporting of neonatal
 deaths results in low numbers of verbal autopsies for neonatal deaths.


36
````

### Trang PDF 57

````text
4. Case study: Bangladesh

4.9 Conclusions
Bangladesh has developed a functioning community MPDSR system that is built into the
government health system and implemented in all districts. Accountability for death notifications
and review is present, and MPDSR monthly coordination meetings play an essential role in
tracking and monitoring deaths. Health workers are supported in community MPDSR and work
closely with the community by building strong community networks. A no-blame culture is
maintained at all levels for both rural and urban communities.

Community MPDSR in Bangladesh, where many births occur at home, continues to be an
important way for the country to report and understand causes of death. There is an ongoing
need to cover the hard-to-reach rural areas, which have shortages and sometimes absence of
human resources. Bangladesh also needs a comprehensive plan to cover the urban population
so that all community deaths are notified and reported. Further, it is essential to improve low
rates of neonatal death and stillbirth notifications.

Functional community MPDSR has strengthened the capturing and reporting community deaths,
including identifying causes of deaths, and explored preventable factors to reduce the burden of
maternal and perinatal deaths. The system has improved the active engagement with the
community, capturing and reviewing deaths, allowing the community to participate and provide
necessary inputs; this, in turn, supports improvement of community preparedness, the
decision-making approach and planning to prevent similar types of death in future.

A strong quality control and monitoring mechanism would improve the quality of MPDSR,
including verbal autopsy data, cause of death assignments and social autopsy facilitation on the
ground. With continuing support for implementation and identification, and actions to counter
challenges, Bangladesh will be able to reduce preventable maternal and perinatal mortality.


 37
````

### Trang PDF 62

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 5.1 Background: maternal and perinatal mortality in Cameroon
 In 1991, the first DHS in Cameroon reported an alarmingly high maternal mortality rate (MMR) of
 430 deaths per 100 000 live births. This figure remained unchanged in 1998, then grew steadily to
 669 in 2004 and 782 in 2011. In 2020, the ratio decreased to 438, which is still more than three
 times the SDG target 3.1 of 140 maternal deaths per 100 000 live births (1). The neonatal mortality
 rate (NMR), although fluctuating, has remained high over the past two decades: from 33 neonatal
 deaths per 1000 live births in 1991 to 37 in 1998, 29 in 2004, 28 in 2018, and 26 in 2022 which is
 still more than double the SDG target 3.2 of 12 neonatal deaths per 1000 live births (Fig. 5.1) (2).

 Maternal deaths are largely preventable. Many factors are associated with the high MMR in
 Cameroon: age (one third of women have their first child before age 18), high parity, lack of
 antenatal care, low socioeconomic status, access and transportation issues, low education
 levels, low health seeking behaviours, cultural beliefs that prevent the use of contraceptives, and
 traditional birthing practices by untrained matrons in unhygienic conditions (3). Additionally,
 Cameroon has experienced conflict and displacement, particularly in the North-West and
 South-West regions. The high maternal and perinatal mortality rates reflect the overall health
 status of women and infants and of the country’s wider health system.

 5.2 Methodology
 Using the protocol described in Chapter 2, fifteen key stakeholders in the country were purposively
 selected for the interviews by the focal point of the Ministry of Health in consultation with the
 consultant and the WHO country office in Cameroon. Potential interviewees included those who
 were involved at various stages of the MPDSR implementation process and are aware of

42
````

### Trang PDF 63

````text
5. Case study: Cameroon


implementation challenges and solutions; those who implement MPDSR at subnational or
national levels and are involved in one or more phases of the MPDSR cycle; and representatives
of professional organizations and civil society organizations working in health.

Prior to the interviews, the national consultant reviewed available MPDSR-related documents in
the country. These included policy documents, frameworks, reports, presentations, and other
secondary data relevant to MPDSR implementation in general, and those specifically related to
the country case study focus.

5.3 Mortality surveillance and MPDSR progress to date
Cameroon’s MPDSR-related policy milestones between 2009 and 2021 are listed in Fig. 5.2 and
described in more detail below.

Fig. 5.2 Timeline of MPDSR-related milestones in Cameroon, 2009–2021

 Adopted
 Campaign to Training and
 Accelerate notification tools, Transition to Pregnancy
 Reduction of integration of Maternal and Outcome and
 Maternal Integrated Disease Perinatal Death Death Surveillance
 Mortality in Surveillance and Surveillance and (PODS) project
Africa (CARMMA) Response (IDSR) Response (MPDSR) launched


 2010 2013 2014 2015* 2018 2019 2020 2021


 Transition to Implementation MPDSR national Incident
 Maternal Death of MDSR guidelines Management
 Surveillance and developed System (IMS) *Sustainable
 Response (MDSR) Development Goals activated
 (SDGs) adopted

In 2009, Cameroon began to implement interventions and restructure administrative infrastructure
as part of renewed efforts to prioritize reduction of maternal deaths. These efforts included the
initiation of the Leadership in Obstetrics and Gynaecology for Impact and Change (LOGIC)
Initiative by the International Federation of Gynecology and Obstetrics (FIGO). In 2010, the
Ministry of Health adopted the Campaign for the Accelerated Reduction of Maternal Mortality in
Africa (CARMMA), which supported a pilot of death audit tools, developed through the FIGO
LOGIC Initiative, to perform death reviews. This initiative continued with the roll-out of tools and
training materials in six hospitals (4). However, the first national push to conduct MDSR began in
2013 with a national initiative to strengthen death registration and notification processes and to
establish death review boards throughout Centre Region and pilot projects in other regions.
Death registration and cause of death data for maternal and perinatal deaths were reported
through a reproductive health reporting system within the Department of Family Health (Direction
de la Santé Familiale) at the health facility level.

In 2014, the Ministry of Health Surveillance and Laboratory Department activated an incident
management system (IMS/système de gestion de l’incident) in response to the Ebola virus disease
outbreak in Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone. The structure was created to detect and respond to
potential Ebola virus disease transmission. The IMS was also used to improve the IDSR platform
nationally, including streamlining case data flow to receive timely information of disease trends.

 43
````

### Trang PDF 64

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 A weekly review meeting was established to assess data trends and deploy subsequent outbreak
 response mechanisms to control the spread of diseases.

 While the focus of this IMS structure was primarily for disease surveillance, the IDSR platform was
 also used to report disease-related mortality events to develop response plans. In areas where there
 was overlap with disease outbreaks, this created confusion about where maternal and perinatal
 deaths should be reported. Only the reproductive health reporting system, however, captured cause
 of death information, and it was unclear which health facilities were reporting into each system.

 In 2018, a perinatal component of MPDSR was added to the system, resulting in a new push to
 revise guidelines, implementation and operationalization of MPDSR procedures to address
 Cameroon’s high levels of maternal and perinatal mortality.

 With the shift from MDSR to MPDSR, Cameroon updated and strengthened its guidelines on
 surveillance system implementation overall. Additional efforts were needed to clarify reporting
 streams for maternal and perinatal deaths from both facility and community levels, support
 death review committees and train people in cause-of-death identification, as well as response
 development and implementation. This initiative was further developed from 2020 in the
 CDC-supported Pregnancy Outcome and Death Surveillance project which focused on
 emergency response, humanitarian populations and outbreak settings, including for COVID-19.
 This project supported capacity-building for MPDSR at national and subnational levels and the
 integration of special populations (e.g. refugees, internally displaced persons) into national
 guidelines; it also pilot-tested several tools to support response tracking at local levels.

 5.4 Maternal and perinatal mortality reporting during the
 COVID-19 pandemic
 In March 2021, when Cameroon experienced a second wave of COVID-19 infections. With growing
 numbers of deaths and limited testing, the IMS was reassessed. Rather than capturing only
 disease-related deaths, IDSR’s role was expanded to capture all births and deaths occurring in
 health facilities, including maternal and perinatal deaths. This initiated the centralization of
 maternal and perinatal mortality data in Cameroon (Fig. 5.3).

 Fig. 5.3 Reporting and notification structure for maternal and perinatal deaths

 Ministry of Public Health 1st and 2nd category hospitals

 Regional Delegation of Public Health 3rd category hospitals

 District Health Service 4th category health facilities

 District hospitals Medicalized health centre and health centres

 Community

 The IDSR platform also provided information on how districts can systematically identify Severe
 acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) infection, confirm COVID-19 cases
 through laboratory testing and assess mortality data to determine potential SARS-CoV-2
 infection history. The systematic identification of infection history among pregnancy-associated
 deaths was used to improve the understanding of maternal mortality associated with COVID-19.
````

### Trang PDF 65

````text
5. Case study: Cameroon


At the district/provincial level, MPDSR steering committees were able to request access to IDSR
data from surveillance officers in both the Surveillance and Laboratory Department and the
Department of Family Health to support the death review process.

IMS for maternal and perinatal mortality
Following the Surveillance and Laboratory Department’s success in improving disease
notification and response through IMS activation, the Department of Family Health activated a
similar IMS in April 2021 to focus on reducing preventable morbidity and mortality. This structure
included five groups that reported to the IMS coordinator: coordination and logistics; finance and
partnerships; communications and public information; operational research; and planning,
monitoring and evaluation (Fig. 5.4). MPDSR initiatives and activities thereby gained attention,
and government and nongovernmental actors were able to reassess identified weaknesses and
strengthen MPDSR components. This included improving quality of death reviews, building
capacity for MPDSR by identifying focal points at regional, district and health-facility levels, and
training several epidemiologists to respond to mortality trends in their areas.

Fig. 5.4 IMS activation structure for maternal and perinatal mortality


 IMS Coordinator


 Secretary


 Communication Planning, Coordination Finance and Operational and public monitoring and logistics partnerships research information and evaluation


 Group Role

 Coordination and logistics Oversee administrative and operational needs

 Finance and partnerships Support resource mapping and dissemination of IMS situation reports

 Communication and public Manage communications, press releases and information on IMS activities
 information

 Operational research Develop MPDSR materials and oversee operationalization of guidelines

 Planning, monitoring and Develop epidemiological bulletin, coordinate with government actors,
 evaluation supervise MPDSR implementation

The new IMS role was to monitor efforts and outcomes at national level through weekly
meetings to identify and support areas in need, essentially functioning as a national-level MPDSR
steering committee.

In the event of a maternal or perinatal death, a death notification/registration would be generated
from the facility/community level within 24 hours and reported up to the district, regional and
national levels through a centralized system. Data collection on the death would then continue at
local level for the death review process, and reports would be compiled and reported to the
````

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 regional and national levels by the district. Selected cases would then be reassessed, alongside
 the trend analysis, at regional and national levels (Fig. 5.5). Maternal and perinatal mortality data
 would continue to be fed into the national surveillance IMS presentations.

 Fig. 5.5 MPDSR roles by level within the IMS structure


 National level Trend analysis, surveillance call centre, case analysis


 Regional level Trend analysis, surveillance call centre, case analysis


 Compile data (in collaboration with District level registration/notification Field Epidemiology Training Programme fellows)

 Health facility/community Death notification/registration, data collection and analysis


 At the national level, reports are compiled weekly from data entered into DHIS2 and integrated
 into an epidemiological bulletin, Maladies à Potentiel Epidémique (MAPE). This weekly report,
 developed as part of the Pregnancy Outcome and Death Surveillance project, shows the
 completeness and timeliness of reporting by districts, births, maternal and neonatal deaths,
 major causes of death, mortality trends, challenges in implementation/reporting, and next steps.
 This improved the visibility of MPDSR and maternal and perinatal mortality throughout the
 country guides further efforts and implementation.

 IMS activation and MPDSR
 The activation of the IMS in April 2021 aimed to improve the quality and timely collection of data
 on maternal and perinatal deaths. The IMS team meets weekly to review deaths for each region.
 This helps quickly identify the most frequent causes of death, discusses solutions to prevent
 future deaths, ensures the quality and timeliness of data, and transmits it to the central level.
 The IMS has contributed tremendously to the progress of MPDSR in Cameroon. In the last few
 years, the IMS structure has been used to develop, adapt and pilot tools to support planning and
 response tracking, local-level indicator monitoring and digital data collection. These pilots were
 conducted in nine districts in Centre and Littoral regions. After piloting, the tools were adopted at
 national level and incorporated into MPDSR operationalization guidelines.

 5.5 Findings from interviews with key stakeholders
 Following the activation and integration of maternal and perinatal mortality within the IMS, key
 stakeholder interviews were conducted to examine the status and progress of MPDSR
 implementation in Cameroon, including strengths, challenges and recommendations for
 improvement. Key stakeholders included representatives from the Ministry of Health (Department
 of Family Health and regional focal points), NGOs, UNICEF, the Society of Gynaecologists and
 Obstetricians (SOGOC/ASSOGOC) and the Society of Paediatrics, obstetricians/gynaecologists
````

````text
 Multisectoral participation and commitment at the national level
 The involvement of diverse actors and stakeholders in MPDSR activities at the national level is a
 major asset to the programme. Alongside the Ministry of Health, international partners such as
 WHO, UNFPA, UNICEF, CDC and the American President’s Emergency Plan For AIDS Relief
 (PEPFAR) support MPDSR financing through a performance-based financing system.

 In addition to international partners, national associations of health workers, such as
 gynaecologists/obstetricians, paediatricians, midwives and family and welfare organizations,
 also played key roles in supporting the operationalization and dissemination of MPDSR guidelines
 and tools. Multiple respondents stated that two Cameroonian societies, SOGOC/ASSOGOC and
 the Society of Paediatrics, were key actors in the development of guidelines and tools that are
 used at the operational level. Both societies understand the operational needs and are expert in
 the fields of maternal and perinatal health, so their involvement during the development and
 dissemination phase was critical to the successful institutionalization of MPDSR.

 In each of the 10 provinces, there is an ASSOGOC office because we wanted all
 gynecologists to be sensitized to the problems of the society, and its objective,
 which is to prevent maternal and infant mortality in their respective regions.
 (Former leader, ASSOGOC)

 One respondent noted that a key strength of the MPDSR system is community-level representation
 and commitment at the national level within the MPDSR committees. This is important to
 increase awareness of and commitment to MPDSR among those who offer different perspectives
 and know the community well. The involvement of traditional authorities, such as village chiefs,


48
````

### Trang PDF 69

````text
5. Case study: Cameroon


is another example of the necessity of garnering commitment from those outside of the health
system to increase awareness of and participation in MPDSR.

Enthusiasm for MPDSR at the central level, and its inclusion within the IMS, was also a powerful
element in motivating stakeholders and increasing participation by key actors. Enthusiasm from
the central level trickled down to regional actors; however, several respondents stated that
progress seems to be plateauing and that enthusiasm has decreased from its high peak. One
respondent attributed this to the perceived lack of follow-up after death reviews.

To intensify efforts to achieve SDG 3 and sustain commitment to MPDSR, enthusiasm and support
must continue to trickle down from the central level. It is vital that actors at all levels (regional,
district, facility and community) understand the purpose of MPDSR and how to comply with the
guidelines. While the appointed champions (regional and district focal points) are a strength of the
MPDSR organizational framework in Cameroon, respondents noted the importance of appointing
people who are qualified and understand their roles and responsibilities. One respondent noted
that enthusiasm varies from facility to facility, indicating a possible weakness in consistent
communication of the importance of MPDSR at the operational level and/or differences in
capacity (human resources and funding) of facilities.

Many respondents stressed the importance of ensuring that guidelines are disseminated to the
operational level, particularly to remote facilities. Several respondents were aware of guidelines
and documents that were developed and circulated at the national level but were uncertain
whether they were received at the facility level. It is recommended that follow-up occurs at the
operational level to ensure that guidelines are received and understood. This includes ensuring
that providers from the private sector have received the guidelines and are fulfilling their MPDSR
responsibilities, which one respondent noted was an important missing link.

 One of the strengths is that this [the guidelines] was first distributed, but one of the
 weaknesses is that it was not disseminated at the health-facility level and there is no
 mechanism for follow-up. (Regional reproductive health focal point)

Funding and human resources
Lack of funding and human resources were cited as major challenges by all respondents.
Sufficient funding and staff capacity are essential elements to sustain national commitment to
MPDSR at all levels of the system.

 I have the impression that in everything, when you start it works well, and after this it’s
 the finance. It’s the money that is the important thing for the activity, I know that the
 medical personnel know the importance but now for implementation you need many
 things, such as financial resources, logistics, even the medical staff, even the human
 resources. (Paediatrician)

The lack of funding and staff capacity mentioned by multiple respondents were amplified by
concerns about overreliance on external partners and about what will happen when external
partners leave.

 I do not think the ministry is doing enough to make the actions sustainable. Because
 as soon as WHO withdraws, as soon as UNICEF withdraws ... We are too reliant on
 external support. And it should not be like this. If we are well organized, we can
 manage this ourselves. (Paediatrician)
 49
````

### Trang PDF 70

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 Training
 MPDSR training began with the national roll-out of the programme in 2014 and has been supported
 by several national-level partners and stakeholders; however, challenges with training methodology
 and funding, compounded by high staff turn-over, can hinder the effectiveness of the training.

 For some respondents, one of the biggest challenges was the selection of people from the
 regional level who would receive training, especially when there was insufficient staffing
 capacity. In some instances, a staff member from a facility received training but did not feel
 comfortable training their superiors, resulting in a lack of buy-in from the health facility.

 Because if there were a doctor in a hospital and the general supervisor were a senior
 nurse and they sent a junior nurse [to train them] there would be no trickle-down
 because the doctor or senior nurse would not want the junior nurse to train them, which
 was a big challenge for the programme. (Regional reproductive health focal point)

 In other instances, those who were trained did not work at the operational level and struggled to
 implement teaching at their health facility after training. Participants also struggled with the
 variations in training: one respondent mentioned that the MPDSR training in their district was
 only two hours long. Others mentioned that training focused on theory rather than practice or
 presented concepts that were difficult to grasp, such as ICD-10 coding.

 The last major training-related challenge highlighted by respondents was high staff turn-over,
 resulting in new staff who do not understand the importance of MPDSR or how to implement all
 the components of MPDSR.

 To address these challenges, respondents made several recommendations, including allocating
 funds to create a cohort of “master trainers” from the operational/grass-roots level. These
 trainers would be able to visit hospitals/health facilities directly and provide initial and refresher
 training or supportive supervision as necessary.

 For me the first thing is the training, and in my view before the training happens we
 need a national cohort to train the trainers. If we look at the national level now, there
 are very few qualified trainers. We are using people who have been there for a very
 long time, but from now on we must think about who is really qualified to provide
 training … Because people are often poorly chosen from the beginning, and we have
 all styles of trainers. Without qualified trainers things are seriously wrong at base.
 Or we train a national cohort with training groups by region: to have at least one
 trainer per region would already be a big advance. (Midwife, Centre Region)

 Trainers should also be responsible for ensuring the quality of MPDSR training, including using
 standardized training materials and disseminating job aids, protocols and shortened guidelines
 that highlight the exact steps and roles needed for death registration, death review, cause-of-death
 classification and response development/implementation. In addition, several respondents
 mentioned that mortality surveillance should be integrated into the curriculum for new staff.

 No, the review process isn’t in their curriculum. But obstetric and neonatal care is. It’s
 in the initial training for general practitioners. That means that the trainees who come
 out of here will have it in their curriculum. The reviews aren’t there yet but maybe in
 time this will come. (Paediatrician)

50
````

### Trang PDF 71

````text
5. Case study: Cameroon

No-blame culture
Many respondents cited fear of blame as a challenge to MPDSR, leading to underreporting of deaths
by health facilities. Respondents reported health workers being concerned that the death notification
and review process would reveal mistakes made by staff and could lead to sanctions. Specifically,
respondents feared this could delay death notifications and cause-of-death investigations.

 There is a type of resistance, a reluctance to notify promptly, to investigate honestly
 and to perform self-audits. There is a sense of guilt that cancels out the health
 training. This means that notification is sometimes veiled or intentionally omitted.
 (Regional reproductive health focal point)

Several respondents also noted, however, that it has been made clear at national level that
reporting deaths and performing audits will not lead to sanctions or punishment. The respondents
were familiar with the “no name, no blame” principle and most were optimistic that health
workers were gradually beginning to accept and believe this. This message is continually
reinforced at national conferences to emphasize that the purpose of MPDSR is to improve the
quality of care and reduce mortality, not to identify mistakes and issue punishments.

One respondent recalled a “no name, no blame” protective legal framework being circulated for
signatures, but was not aware whether it had been officially passed and disseminated, despite its
signature by the Minister of Health in December 2017. It is recommended that this policy be
sufficiently circulated, and to ensure it is implemented at the operational level and understood by
health workers.

Prioritization of perinatal death reporting and reviews
A sentiment expressed by many key informants was that perinatal death reporting is less
prioritized than maternal deaths, and that this reflects cultural views of perinatal deaths. One
respondent stated that newborn deaths are less shocking because the newborns are not known
to the community (“no one sees it or knows it … it happens unnoticed”), unlike deaths of mothers.
Another informant said that a cultural constraint prevents perinatal death reviews because they
are viewed as something that it is necessary to forget in order to prepare for the next pregnancy.

 There is a cultural component because, in my culture, when a baby dies we say, ‘do
 not cry’. God has taken him and will give another. And we must bury the baby. There is
 a delay. And this delay does not give much time to do the reviews. And when you start
 to ask people questions, they get annoyed, it’s an epiphenomenon, you must forget
 about it and move on to the next pregnancy. So this cultural aspect hinders the
 process a little. (Health director)

It is essential that hospital records enable providers to link maternal and newborn cases. One
respondent stated that hospital records are not organized to link maternal and perinatal cases,
while another stated that paperwork is being restructured to reflect the mother–child dyad in
hospital records.

One respondent noted that the Ministry of Health made it obligatory to set up committees to review
all deaths, so that perinatal death records have the same priority as maternal death reports.
Another respondent recommended that communities should also be lobbied to increase
awareness that many perinatal deaths are preventable.


 51
````

### Trang PDF 72

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 Reporting deaths
 One of the strongest elements of MPDSR in Cameroon is the visibility of death notification and
 reporting by districts. The weekly MAPE bulletins identify gaps clearly and effectively, allowing
 for swift action to be taken, particularly to increase reporting from districts with low participation.
 According to the latest bulletins, all districts are represented and are reporting some numbers
 of deaths.

 Despite this impressive growth, challenges remain in areas with limited access within a district,
 in community-based reporting, and for displaced or other hard-to-reach populations, such as
 internally displaced persons or refugees. One respondent reported a study in 38 health facilities
 revealing that 60–70% of maternal and perinatal deaths were not represented in the weekly MAPE
 bulletin. While that may not be a representative sample, coverage from all health facilities in a
 district remains a challenge. Additionally, several respondents mentioned that deaths in the
 community are inadequately captured in reporting. One respondent attributed this to concern
 that reporting deaths will raise death counts of particular facilities, another example of health
 workers’ fear of blame.

 Deaths that occurred in the community, which could be declared by health facilities,
 are not declared, because the health facilities think it will be burdensome and [will
 increase] the number of deaths [at the facility]. Verbal autopsies are not held, and this
 creates the problems you see in the statistics. (Health director)

 Timely notification and reporting of deaths also depend on human resource capacity. As one
 respondent stated, when there are insufficient staff members, data entry becomes backlogged
 and is either submitted late or not submitted at all.

 The appointment of regional- and district-level focal points and weekly MAPE bulletins create
 accountability and form the basis for follow-up. It is recommended that reporting be closely
 monitored at the national, regional and district levels. When facilities are experiencing issues
 with completeness and promptness of reporting, focal points should investigate the reasons for
 this and provide support as needed. Where weekly MAPE bulletins are not already being shared
 with facilities, it is recommended that this be remedied to increase the coverage and timeliness
 of death reporting. This could also help to destigmatize death reporting and increase awareness
 about mortality trends to initiate conversation about how deaths can be prevented.

 Death review meetings
 Several respondents noted that holding regular meetings improved the quality of death reviews
 and created space to discuss maternal and perinatal mortality. This has also led to additional
 buy-in and attention from actors at facilities who were previously not following mortality trends.
 However, several challenges remain, particularly concerning data quality.

 Several respondents mentioned that, even where death review meetings were held, there was
 sometimes insufficient information from the meetings to reveal mortality trends or enable
 responses. Furthermore, data quality issues at the operational level affected national reporting.

 Some participants felt that the materials they had for death reviews were not capturing the true
 needs, causes of and contributors to death, particularly for miscarriages and abortions. One
 respondent mentioned that this was further reflected in national statistics, and that lack of
 visibility meant they were unable to convey the true importance of the issue without further
 tailoring of the national statistics.

52
````

### Trang PDF 73

````text
5. Case study: Cameroon


 Our society had a meeting with the Ministry of Health, with the people who process
 the statistics at the ministry. They noticed a lot of weaknesses in the information that
 we can connect at the national level because we do not ask for this information.
 Therefore, the data collected are clearly insufficient to provide a clear picture.
 (Former leader of a professional association)

To improve the frequency of death reviews, recommendations included increasing hospital
administrative buy-in. In instances where administrators attended or participated in meetings,
respondents reported better-quality data and better initiatives from those meetings.

 The leaders of maternity wards, often the director, participated in these reviews.
 We sometimes invited the doctors, and they came. (Midwife)

To address data quality issues in the death review meetings, supportive supervision efforts
combined with refresher training at district or region level would benefit participants.
Additionally, further audits from higher levels would allow data to be reviewed again, and data
quality issues could be flagged to identify areas needing support.

Actionable response
As with many countries, developing and implementing responses is a challenge. Interviews with
key stakeholders confirmed this, although a few mentioned that some response mechanisms
have worked when close monitoring of mortality data enabled issues to be detected in a timely
manner, which led to targeted response. Respondents reported confusion about which levels
responses should be sent to, as many issues were better suited to district- or community-level
interventions than to interventions in health facilities.

It is still unclear how community-based responses are developed at the district level or how
responses are implemented at different levels (community, health facility, district, etc.). One
respondent also highlighted a lack of evaluation of the outcomes or impacts from
implemented responses.

 Even in the presentations we see mostly other aspects, such as the surveillance of
 reviews. But, for example, what was done at the end of a response? And the effect or
 impact are not often evaluated or measured, and that is a challenge. (Maternal,
 newborn and reproductive health specialist, United Nations organization)

Overall, response activities need to be strengthened through the use of tools, funding, supportive
supervision efforts and multilevel involvement in death review committees. This will enable
health-facility committee members to develop responses tailored to their own facility that are
actionable in a timely manner. To support this, financial and human resources should be provided
by health-facility administrators and the district to enable follow-through and implementation of
response activities.


 53
````

### Trang PDF 74

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 5.6 Conclusions
 The high MMR revealed by the DHS in Cameroon served as a national impetus to prioritize the
 surveillance and reduction of maternal mortality, and led to the creation of MDSR. The perinatal
 component was later added to create the MPDSR system, and a national IMS structure was
 activated to focus on reducing preventable morbidity and mortality. This was the first example of
 an IMS structure being used in this way, including support for centralization of data, linkages
 between disease-related maternal and perinatal mortality and MPDSR structures, increased
 visibility of data, and strengthened coordination of maternal and perinatal mortality surveillance
 training, activities and responses. The IMS structure has supported significant and noteworthy
 progress in expanding MPDSR, particularly by increasing national death reporting coverage and
 establishing death review committees within health facilities. The IMS activation also increased
 partner involvement, funding and renewed attention to MPDSR. Key informant interviews
 highlighted remaining gaps and challenges, where further support is needed to strengthen
 existing activities and support further implementation of MPDSR components.


54
````

````text
 Inter-agency Group for Child Mortality Estimation, 2022. New York: UNICEF; 2023.
 57
````

### Trang PDF 78

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 6.1 Background
 Colombia is a highly diverse and multiethnic country of 48 258 494 people, of whom 76% live in
 urban areas and 24% in rural areas (1). Colombia extends over diverse geographic regions, including
 the Atlantic lowlands, the Pacific coastal region, the Andean region, the Llanos and the Amazonian
 rainforest. The country’s five districts, 32 departments and 1123 municipalities, which include
 Indigenous territories and cover a third of the country, are referred to as territorial entities (2).

 Vulnerabilities due to factors including systemic violence, forced displacement and migration have
 led to Colombia being classified as one of the most inequitable countries in Latin America (3).
 The health risks of people living under these circumstances, with limited access to services, pose
 a challenge for reducing maternal and perinatal mortality, and for improving the quality of health
 services. Maternal mortality is a major public health problem, involving important aspects of
 gender and rights, and is also affected by social determinants of health (4).

 Public health surveillance6 has become an important strategy for improving health and
 monitoring development indicators, due to its outstanding results in the active search for cases,
 timely notification of events of public health interest, and epidemiological analysis that directly
 guide and influence countries’ plans, programmes and policies. In Colombia, the surveillance
 system is essential for national health security, enabling analysis and understanding of health
 situations defined as a national priority. National regulations define the roles and responsibilities
 of stakeholders in the system. The National Institute of Health has developed and disseminated
 methodologies and tools to strengthen public health surveillance – through standardizing
 actions, defining procedures, risk assessments and generating early warnings, and building and
 maintaining capacities to ensure the daily operation of the system throughout the country (6, 7).

 6.2 Objective of the case study
 The objective of the case study was to identify the strengths and challenges of integrated public
 health surveillance of safe motherhood in Colombia and to showcase its successes in maternal
 and perinatal mortality surveillance.

 The case study covers the public policies implemented in Colombia for the surveillance of and
 response to maternal and perinatal mortality, identifying their regulation and their impact on the
 different levels of the health system.

 6.3 Methodology
 The protocol described in Chapter 2 was implemented with the approval of Colombia’s National
 Institute of Health. A literature review was then followed by 20 in-depth interviews with key
 stakeholders in selected cities. Of these, 18 were conducted face-to-face and two virtually,
 according to the availability of interviewees. All interviewees provided informed consent. All
 interviews were transcribed, analysed and translated from Spanish to English.

 Information from the interviews was triangulated with that obtained from collated public policies,
 national programmes and published literature to allow a systematic and comprehensive
 assessment of surveillance practices in the country.


 6. In Colombia, public health surveillance is an essential function of public health associated with the state’s and
 citizens’ responsibilities for the protection of individual and collective health, involving the systematic processing of
 specific data relating to health and its determinants (MINSALUD, 2012) (5).
58
````

### Trang PDF 79

````text
6. Case study: Colombia

6.4 Historical aspects and current status of MPDSR within
Colombia’s public health surveillance and vital statistics systems
When Colombia’s national public health surveillance system (Spanish acronym SIVIGILA) was
established in 2006, maternal and perinatal mortality were included among the events requiring
mandatory notification. The ability to obtain, access and analyse data on maternal and perinatal
mortality has enabled the adoption of national public policies towards safe motherhood.
Surveillance of congenital defects was included under SIVIGILA in 2013, and severe maternal
morbidity in 2014, forming the integrated system of surveillance of safe motherhood events.
This system allows for the generation of epidemiological alerts, follow-up and mitigation of
events identified as tracers of the health and well-being of pregnant women and newborns.

The annually updated National Institute of Health’s public health surveillance guidelines apply to
all department and district entities, health-service provider institutions and national health care
schemes, and benefit plan administration entities (insurers). The guidelines for surveillance of
safe motherhood events cover severe maternal morbidity, maternal mortality, late perinatal and
neonatal mortality, and congenital defects, and designate responsibilities of postholders and
strategies by levels of care at different levels of the health system.

The SIVIGILA information subsystem supports the case notification strategy, which defines the
different levels of information flow and the responsibilities of actors (8). The subsystem of
analysis and dissemination allows for the tabulation, interpretation, analysis and dissemination
of information generated by the surveillance system to detect changes in time, place and person,
and the creation of alerts. This allows one to know how events occur and to determine control
mechanisms (6, 7). In 2015 and 2017, the National Administrative Department of Statistics
certified SIVIGILA as a statistical process of high reliability (8).

Surveillance results are monitored in real time. Updated data are disseminated monthly through
epidemiological bulletins published on the National Institute of Health website. The Primary Data
Generating Units and the Notifying Units are responsible for the mandatory, timely and continuous
notification of accurate, high-quality information required for public health surveillance (8).

The system standardizes demographic and population variables for all events of public health
interest in a single system, in addition to capturing specific information. The SIVIGILA system
captures mutually comparable information from more than 14 000 notifiers throughout the
country, and provides access to historical information, serving as a resource for health research
in Colombia. Tools are included to identify high-incidence or high-risk events in real time, such as
early warning and online capture, which has led to its becoming an accredited source of information.

The web-based Maternal Mortality Epidemiological Surveillance Information System (Spanish
acronym SVEMMBW) has also provided public health data since 2012 (9). This application
collects timely and detailed information on maternal deaths, with complementary information to
that reported in SIVIGILA. SVEMMBW generates alerts on deaths of women of childbearing age
to determine whether an event is associated with pregnancy and childbirth. It records data from
family interviews, verbal autopsies, clinical care summaries and case analyses, and
automatically generates technical reports and action plans relating to the maternal deaths
reviewed. It ensures greater attention to and monitoring of cases by those involved in the care
and monitoring of each case.

With information from vital statistics (the Single Registry of Affiliates to Social Protection) and death
certificates uploaded onto SVEMMBW, an active institutional search allows cases to be identified,
notified and entered into the application. This generates immediate alerts at the national level,
promoting the timely identification and analysis of all maternal and perinatal deaths in Colombia.
````

````text
 54 years of age to confirm or rule out doubtful cases. (Epidemiologist)
````
