````text
 When we review the level of underreporting in the country, it does not exceed 1%, and
 probably occurs only in very isolated and rural areas. We have been coordinating the
 strategies of the Ministry of Health with the vital statistics system to better capture
 those cases. (Ministry of Health official)

 6.5 The reporting system for maternal and perinatal deaths
 The legal provisions (10) state that all health-service provider institutions in the country using
 SIVIGILA must actively search for deaths of women of childbearing age that are certified by each
 institution and verify previous pregnancy histories. The weekly notification will be “zero” if there
 are no maternal deaths or no reportable events. A “zero” notification is required (where appropriate)
 to confirm that each notifying institution is active.

 Periodic comparisons and cross-checks of SIVIGILA surveillance records are made with the vital
 statistics system, and verification is done through the Information System Network of Missing
 Persons and Corpses of the National Institute of Legal Medicine and Forensic Sciences. This
 facilitates dissemination of the medical–legal information on deaths, to optimize the detection of
 significant public health events.

 The SIVIGILA application is updated annually. A highly skilled team provides technical support at
 the national, departmental/district and municipal levels. The application is accessible through
 any internet browser, facilitating information management. Once a case is reported, an alert is
 generated to all municipal, departmental and national entities. In scattered rural areas without
 access to the internet, reports must be made to the municipal level by telephone for onward
 reporting through the online application. As part of this process, an active institutional search is
 carried out through review by the Single Registry of Affiliates to Social Protection, the system for
 online registration and updating of information on births and deaths.

 Maternal and neonatal deaths and stillbirths, and congenital birth defects are surveillance priority
 events in Colombia and are included in the 10-year public health plan (7, 11). The actions implemented
 aim to improve and strengthen the surveillance system for maternal and perinatal deaths.
60
````

### Trang PDF 81

````text
6. Case study: Colombia


For maternal mortality, active weekly searches are made for deaths of women of childbearing
age (10–54 years) to identify any cases not reported to SIVIGILA as maternal deaths.

For perinatal mortality, weekly notifications are made in the SIVIGILA application, and consolidated
by the Primary Data Generating Unit before being sent to the territorial, departmental and national
entities. Subsequently, the individual in charge of the territorial entities oversees verification and
validation of the information, according to the quality criteria, providing feedback to the those
reporting the cases concerning any necessary adjustment of the information.

Currently, the high turn-over of human resources has had an impact on the integrity and quality
of the data recorded in notification forms, hindering the surveillance and response processes for
maternal mortality. For this reason, permanent technical assistance is provided to the Primary
Data Generating Units for the maintenance and compliance of the surveillance system.

Departments and districts across Colombia have different implementation strategies for rural
areas with difficult access. These include deploying CHWs to identify, manage and report on
care provided to pregnant women and newborns. In places with low health service coverage,
community agents are trained to provide culturally appropriate counselling and follow-up support
in multiethnic and culturally diverse settings.

 Routine community surveillance and capacity-building within [health-service provider
 institutions] [aims] for timely notification so that we can manage the risk. Our [system]
 has an application that sends the alert; the institution does the surveillance, and we
 then upload the information. (Community health worker)

Some community agents are members of Indigenous groups and facilitate the exchange of
knowledge of traditional and ancestral medicine with the local population and health workers,
promoting the care and protection of pregnant women and newborns. Engaging with communities
to strengthen the surveillance of maternity in Indigenous territories facilitates the follow-up and
reporting of vital events.

 We do 80% of the maternal and perinatal death surveillance at community level. We
 have our own network in the territories with affiliated populations, and we follow up
 with the network of the contracted operators, complying with the maternal and
 perinatal death surveillance system and National Resolution 3280. (Community agent)

For example, the municipality of Monteria now offers a telemedicine programme to all users
without good access to health facilities. This enables alternative sources of information, thus
improving the analysis of coverage and data matching in the national surveillance system.

6.6 Review and analysis
The National Institute of Health uses the “units of analysis” methodological process for the definitive
classification of events of public health interest and the identification of factors associated with
their outcome. Maternal and perinatal deaths are reviewed through this approach; timely reviews
ensure credibility in the management of information and generation of actions for improvement.

Members of a Unit of Analysis meet with representatives of all institutions involved in the care of
the deceased woman or the other cases under analysis. Their meeting schedule depends on the
number and type of safe motherhood events notified and the place where they occur.


 61
````

### Trang PDF 82

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 Sources of information for the analysis may be: individual medical records,
 epidemiological bulletins, case notification records, copies of epidemiological field
 investigations and verbal autopsies (if applicable), death certificates, results of
 specific diagnostic tests, information from the [benefit plan administration entities], etc.
 (Unit of Analysis member)

 The frequency of cases reviewed depends on the event (maternal mortality, perinatal mortality or
 severe maternal morbidity). All cases of early maternal mortality are analysed, regardless of the
 cause. The institution where the death occurred carries out an immediate analysis of the care
 process leading to the death and suggests actions for improvement. The insurer also carries out
 an analysis, usually a week after the death, by collecting clinical histories from all institutions
 involved in care from the beginning of the pregnancy to the last care received. The Territorial
 Entity or the Departmental/District Health Secretary leads the final analysis, summoning all the
 stakeholders. Once the findings are defined, improvement plans are finalized. These reviews are
 carried out within 45 days after the death.

 In cases of perinatal mortality and severe maternal morbidity, due to their high prevalence, a
 monthly sample number of cases is analysed by each territory, based on criteria such as the
 response capacity of each territory, number of cases, time of death, weight of the baby and/or
 related causes. Cases of congenital defects related to the central nervous system are analysed
 within eight weeks of notification (11).

 Collective analysis of the events is carried out monthly, identifying cases, calculating summary
 statistics, place and sociodemographic variables.

 The Birth Weight and Age-at-Death Boxes for an Intervention and Evaluation System (BABIES)
 matrix (12) is specifically used by the territorial entities and the National Institute of Health, as a
 complementary method for analysis of perinatal events, to classify deaths according to the time
 and the weight of the fetus/infant at death. This matrix is usually used semi-annually, but
 annually if there is a high volume of cases.

 We try to analyse perinatal deaths, although being a university hospital we have great
 difficulties because perinatal deaths must be analysed by a team that includes a
 paediatrician and an obstetrician … We try to analyse the most relevant cases. We do
 not participate in the analysis of neonatal death, being obstetricians this is done by
 paediatricians, which is a mistake. (Gynaecologist)

 The results of the review process are the minutes of the meeting, the problem analysis and the
 response plan. These analyses are performed solely for epidemiological purposes and are not
 punitive since they are not documents of legal use for judging medical practice.

 The death reviews are not punitive. Administrative efforts have been made to improve
 the confidentiality of information, both of those who participate and of the results of
 the analysis units. We have improved the way in which we communicate information,
 so that the media and the community have helped to mobilize efforts to respond to
 the problem. We have supported the territorial entities and the Secretariat of Health
 when they have faced difficult situations. (Senior Ministry of Health official)


62
````

### Trang PDF 83

````text
6. Case study: Colombia


Unit of Analysis meetings concerning individual cases are ideally attended by the
gynaecologist/obstetrics coordinator, an epidemiologist and the manager of the institution
involved, who present the clinical history summary and the identified findings in chronological
order of care. Representatives of the different health departments (public health surveillance,
insurance, provision of services, and surveillance and control) also participate: they lead a
comprehensive analysis of each case, define the improvement plan and monitor its
implementation. None of these actors is obliged to participate in legal proceedings and each
participant has the obligation and responsibility to guarantee the confidentiality of the
information and its exclusive use for epidemiological purposes.

The findings on the contributory factors and recommended actions from the reviews are sent
periodically to the National Institute of Health.

The culture of blame has been minimized thanks to the systematization of the process, the
regularity of reviews and the leadership of the National Institute of Health. Extensive Colombian
regulations supporting the surveillance and analysis of these events allow greater rigour and
confidentiality in the use of information by the actors and institutions involved.

Maternal near misses are notified through an early warning system according to near-miss
severity criteria. This is sent immediately by email. Once notification is made to the benefit plan
administration entity (insurer), they must ensure immediate attention to the users, activate the
comprehensive care routes, and follow up on the case. The purpose of this system is to identify,
collect data and analyse information on all maternal near misses that have occurred in the
country and to transfer the information electronically. At the beginning of the implementation of
surveillance, and due to the high number of notifications, review meetings were only performed
on a percentage of cases of severe maternal morbidity. Relevant information is collected by
interviewing the survivor and the analysis is performed under the methodology of the “four
delays” model (13).

 All the efforts we have made from the beginning, especially during the last six years,
 are to strengthen this surveillance because we consider that timely detection of
 maternal conditions that can lead to death can be much more successful if an
 adequate intervention is made. So, we have emphasized the development of this
 surveillance … [The SIVIGILA Safe Motherhood application] was initially started with
 the support of PAHO, and then continued to be developed with the [Institute of
 Health’s] own resources. (Ministry of Health official)

The classification of the cases is relevant, as it allows differentiation between direct and indirect
causes of a maternal death, and whether or not it was preventable. This makes it possible to
record these causes in the death certificate, and in the Single Registry of Affiliates to Social
Protection application, if required, according to the unit of analysis approach.

The migrant population, which is greater in departments bordering other countries, especially the
Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela, requires targeted MPDSR strategies. Many migrants who have
resided in Colombia for more than six months have not regularized their stay and have no access
to essential health services. Prenatal check-ups and early detection services are not available to
them, increasing the risks of morbidity and mortality. Although humanitarian agencies have
joined efforts to provide the health services required by the migrant population, ensuring access
remains a challenge. Another challenging situation affecting some areas of the country relates to
armed conflict, which prevents women from accessing health services, limits availability of
specialized services and weakens the referral system.

 63
````

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 The main challenge is to achieve homogeneity in the implementation of processes in
 the territories to build capacities for surveillance, control and response. Not all
 territorial entities have the same level of development, so our main concern is to
 reduce the gap that exists between those who have more and those who have less.
 (Department medical official)

 The COVID-19 pandemic also created challenges for the maintenance of surveillance of safe
 motherhood events and the continuity of essential services for the care of pregnant women and
 newborns, as health services were redirected to the health emergency.

 6.7 Response in MPDSR
 In 2014, surveillance of severe maternal morbidity was implemented with the reporting of cases
 of women with severe complications during pregnancy through the SIVIGILA Safe Motherhood
 application. In response to the process of identifying these women, efforts were made to activate
 immediate responses with the early warning system, which the benefit plan administration
 entities receive daily and in real time. To reduce the risk of maternal death, notification of an
 extreme maternal morbidity event made by a health-service provider institution to SIVIGILA and
 the insurer must lead to the pregnant woman receiving timely, high-quality care.

 The timeliness of the notification of maternal and perinatal deaths and near-miss cases is also
 evaluated through an active institutional search. Triangulation of data from different sources
 enables efficient management of health information, and the execution of improvement plans for
 the closure of cases in the application; and strengthens the timely and comprehensive response
 to improve maternal and perinatal health outcomes.

 In recent years, situational or risk analysis taskforces were set up in response to the increase in
 maternal and perinatal deaths. These taskforces promote the participation of institutions at the
 national, departmental and district levels, including the National Institute of Health, the National
 Superintendence of Health, the Ministry of Health’s safe motherhood team, benefit plan
 administration entities and health-service provider institutions. In addition to analysing
 epidemiological data, they review progress towards achieving programmatic targets established in
 the national MNH plan. They also develop an action plan that is followed up by the Ministry of Health.

 In compliance with the technical guidelines for the surveillance of events of public health interest,
 the national, departmental and district entities offer technical support and training to health
 workers to improve their skills, not only in the departmental, district and municipal health
 secretariats but also in health-service provider institutions and benefit plan administration entities.

 Territorial entities provide periodic technical assistance to public and private institutions, to strengthen
 surveillance processes, and to promote timeliness and quality in the notification, classification
 and analysis of safe motherhood events. Virtual training processes address important issues in
 the surveillance of perinatal mortality, maternal mortality and severe maternal morbidity (6, 7, 14).

 We have also started the kangaroo plan with Ministry of Health to follow up on
 premature children or children with low birth weight. The programme provides all the
 care and monitoring needed until the child has the right weight. It also identifies if there
 are any problems and follows up with physiotherapy, psychology, nutrition and
 ophthalmology. The outpatient neonatologist receives a comprehensive review of the
 case. (Safe motherhood care manager of a department)

64
````

### Trang PDF 85

````text
6. Case study: Colombia


Some territorial entities have carried out contracts with the network of providers, seeking to unify
the provision of low- and high-complexity care services to reduce barriers in the provision of
health services. In addition, monitoring indicators and disseminating data and bulletins are
informing decision-making.

Various monthly meetings, such as those of the epidemiological surveillance committees, are also
used to gather feedback on the findings identified. In areas where the majority of the population
is multiethnic, the benefit plan administration entities are special Indigenous groups. They use
mobile teams to provide health services to the population in difficult-to-access geographic areas.

Among the achievements is the involvement of the benefit plan administration entities in the
notification, and their active participation in Unit of Analysis meetings and in the creation and
follow-up of improvement plans. This has an impact on strengthening the analysis of maternal
and perinatal death cases and cases of severe maternal morbidity, reducing access barriers and
improving risk management.

6.8 Factors contributing to success
The standardized public health surveillance process, first introduced more than 16 years ago
(Decree 3518 of 2006 and Decree 780 of 2016), has been key to MPDSR implementation in Colombia.

Effective dissemination of information has enabled monitoring, analysis and decision-making.
Documents such as technical guidelines are updated annually, weekly publication of epidemiological
bulletins enables monitoring of events, and periodic reports provide epidemiological analysis of
each event under surveillance in the country. These and other dissemination mechanisms
established by the National Institute of Health and the territorial entities contribute significantly
to the timely generation of epidemiological alerts and decision-making for the reduction and
mitigation of maternal and perinatal mortality.

The notification network for events of public health interest has been continually strengthened,
growing from 4000 health service provider institutions in 2012 to 14 000 by 2022. This notification
network is permanent and constant, with regular contact with SIVIGILA. Moreover, the use of risk
stratification matrices to prioritize subnational areas with high cases of safe motherhood events
notified through SIVIGILA facilitates intervention, decision-making and the definition of actions
needed to reduce risks.

Mobile teams for case management, within the framework of the early warning system to
guarantee comprehensive care for severe maternal morbidity events and periodic follow-up
of women, have helped to reduce barriers to accessing health care for underserved groups.
This strategy demonstrates progress in the integration of the surveillance system into the
institutional response, and in the definition of individual and collective actions for the
improvement of maternal and perinatal care. The recognition of problems in access to care has
led to timely responses from territorial entities to protect the health of their inhabitants (15).

Committees at the district, departmental and municipal levels provide feedback on the
monitoring of indicators defined as “safe motherhood tracers”. This enables follow-up on the
findings of safe motherhood events in hard-to-reach populations (migrants, Indigenous people)
during national coordination meetings and by international development cooperation agencies.

Integrating the surveillance of other safe motherhood events. Monitoring severe maternal morbidity
along with maternal mortality strengthens the timely detection of conditions that lead to death.
Emphasis on the surveillance of perinatal and congenital defects, and some infectious diseases,
improves the classification of cases and draws attention to problems requiring intervention.

 65
````

### Trang PDF 86

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 The integration of information from different sources in a single application (SIVIGILA Safe
 Motherhood) provides a central source of official information and guarantees immediate access
 to data for all stakeholders in the Colombian General Health and Social Security System at
 municipal, departmental, district and national levels.

 The strategy for monitoring noncommunicable diseases has made it possible to involve insurers
 in identifying women at risk of severe maternal morbidity in the early warning system.

 The units of analysis approach used to review all safe motherhood events has made it possible to
 identify the failures and access barriers that partly explain the occurrence of the event, recognizing
 the social determinants, risk factors and failures in adherence to management guidelines and
 protocols. This has generated short- and medium-term actions that mitigate and prevent the
 future occurrence of maternal and perinatal deaths.

 Technical support: Colombian scientific societies in obstetrics and gynaecology and perinatology
 provide support and technical assistance in the care of pregnant women and newborns, and were
 actively involved in developing treatment protocols for those diagnosed with COVID-19 during the
 first waves of the pandemic. National and international institutions (National Institute of Health,
 Ministry of Social Protection, WHO/PAHO, UNFPA, UNICEF, etc.) provide technical advice, set up
 working groups and strengthen face-to-face and virtual training, in order to reach remote and
 underserved areas. The high level of preparation and technical quality of the officials of the
 national and territorial entities enable continuous improvement of the processes.
 6.9 Challenges and possible solutions
 Further strengthening of Colombia’s integrated safe motherhood surveillance system will require
 policies and action to address four key challenges.
 1. Achieve homogeneity in the performance of the surveillance system for safe motherhood
 events. There are differences in implementation across territorial entities according to their
 level of development and their technical and contracting capacities.
 2. Improve the management of MPDSR and the publication of the information collected, to
 identify and implement quality improvements and risk management actions needed to
 address fragmentation in health-care delivery.
 3. Change the regulation process for migrants so that those who have been in the country for
 more than six months can access health services. Early and targeted interventions are
 needed to mitigate the occurrence of morbidity and mortality events.
 4. Strengthen CHWs’ skills and strengthen capacity in the most vulnerable populations to
 guarantee their right to timely access to comprehensive health services and to improve the
 identification of risks and warning signs in all pregnant women and newborns.


66
````

### Trang PDF 87

````text
6. Case study: Colombia

6.10 Conclusions
Colombia’s safe motherhood surveillance system is a comprehensive, robust, complex yet
efficient process providing access to timely information on maternal and perinatal deaths, severe
maternal morbidity and congenital defects. Involving different stakeholders from the national
level to the community level in surveillance has resulted in wide coverage and facilitated the
processes of identification, notification, follow-up and timely analysis of negative health events.
This is demonstrated by the system’s positive impact: the information it provides offers
possibilities for preventive intervention in individual cases, and the implementation of
increasingly effective policies and strategies to improve maternal and perinatal health outcomes.

The SIVIGILA Safe Motherhood application is used by more than 14 000 notifiers throughout the
country who actively report in a standardized manner, enabling internal comparability and
decision-making in almost real time. This application is based on the active institutional search
for cases, and improves the timeliness of case detection and the participation of stakeholders in
the analysis and implementation of response actions.

Due to the fundamental role that community health agents play in the surveillance of safe
motherhood events, insurance companies are increasingly contracting them to promote
community-based public health surveillance processes.

The integrated safe motherhood surveillance system has made it possible to periodically monitor
management and outcome indicators, facilitating the generation of actions to prevent and
control fatal outcomes and avoidable disabilities. This has improved the quality of health
services and guides the formulation of public health policies in Colombia.


 67
````

### Trang PDF 92

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 7.1 Background and study objectives
 Nigeria, with its population of 233 million (2024), is the sixth most populous country in the world (1)
 and accounts for 28.5% of all maternal deaths globally in 2020 (2) (the United Nations estimated
 an MMR of 1047 per 100 000 live births; uncertainty interval [UI]: 793–1565), among the highest
 infant and under-five mortality rates globally (3). However, the MMR reported in the Nigerian DHS
 is much lower (512 per 100 000 live births in 2018 and similar to MMRs reported in earlier surveys)
 (4-6). According to national data, the NMR (estimated at 34 per 1000 live births; UI: 24–51 in 2022
 [3]) declined slowly from 55 per 1000 live births in 1990 to 39 in 2018 (5), but it varies widely
 across the country, with rates in the north-western states five times those in the south-west (7).
 The estimated stillbirth rate was 22.5 per 1000 births (UI: 13.7–36.7) in 2021 (8). Successive DHS
 reports indicate continued low levels of antenatal care attendance, births in health facilities,
 contraceptive use and uptake of child health services. Maternal and child mortality remain
 unacceptably high. Additionally, maternal and child mortality and morbidity data from the national
 HMIS is limited and does not accurately reflect the burden in the community, which may be why
 Nigerian DHS reported mortality rates are lower than estimates from United Nations organizations.

 Studies have been carried out to determine the extent, underlying causes and determinants of
 maternal and perinatal deaths in Nigeria, to improve understanding so as to develop effective
 intervention strategies. Drawing national inferences from these studies has been limited because
 of their variation in geographic coverage, time periods and methodologies (9-12). However, two
 studies conducted five years apart with national coverage, similar methodology and facilitators
 have provided better insight into these deaths, albeit from tertiary referral hospitals.

 The first study, undertaken in 2012–2013, involved 42 public tertiary hospitals. Data were
 collected on 91 724 live births and 5910 stillbirths. The MMR during the course of the study was
 1088 per 100 000 live births. Causes of maternal deaths included hypertensive disorders (29%),
 obstetric haemorrhage (24.4%) and indirect causes (19.6%). Delayed referral, late presentation to
 thenonavailabilityhospital,ofdelayedrequiredtreatment,blood/bloodlackproductsof healthalsoinsurance/inabilitycontributed to theseto paydeathsfor services(10). and

 The second study, conducted between September 2019 and August 2020 in 54 tertiary referral
 hospitals, reported similar results, with an MMR of 1361 per 100 000 live births and a perinatal
 mortality rate of 76 per 1000 births (11). Leading causes of maternal mortality were also similar to
 the earlier study. None of the hospitals in the study was involved in the MPDSR programme
 adopted by the Federal Ministry of Health (FMOH) for implementation across the country in 2015.

 Because of Nigeria’s persistently high burden of maternal and perinatal deaths and failure to
 achieve Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) 4 and 5, new MPDSR strategies were sought to
 achieve the SDG targets of an MMR below 70 maternal deaths per 100 000 live births and 12
 neonatal deaths per 1000 live births by 2030 (6).

 Aim and objectives of the case study
 The aim of this case study was to document lessons learned from MPDSR implementation in
 Nigeria, with a special focus on bolstering political will and commitment through the
 development and implementation of a MPDSR legal framework.

 The specific objectives were: 1) to outline Nigeria’s successes and innovations with MPDSR;
 2) to identify and understand gaps and challenges in MPDSR implementation, and how these
 were overcome; and 3) to identify and share key learning points from Nigeria’s process of
 developing and implementing its MPDSR legal framework.


72
````

### Trang PDF 93

````text
7. Case study: Nigeria

7.2 Methodology
The protocol described in Chapter 2 was implemented. A desk review was undertaken, along
with in-depth interviews with key stakeholders, between 21 July and 31 August 2022. A total of
96 documents relevant to MPDSR in Nigeria were reviewed. These included MPDSR committee
reports, project and programme reports, peer-reviewed publications, and survey and monitoring
data. A total of 24 in-depth interviews of MPDSR stakeholders were conducted. The findings from
the desk review and interviews are presented in the following sections.

7.3 Steps leading to the national MPDSR programme
Routine clinical audits to improve quality of care existed in Nigeria for several years in all pre-service
clinical training and in most health facilities, and included clinical staff directly or indirectly
involved in care of the deceased (12). However, as this practice was unstructured, limited to
secondary- and tertiary-level facilities, and often used to settle personal scores and recriminations,
it failed to improve quality of care and health outcomes.

Prior to implementation of the national MPDSR programme, maternal death reviews were
initiated in some states between 2008 and 2015. These ranged from single-state initiatives to
programmes involving a cluster of states, mostly focused on maternal death reviews, with
funding from international partners (13).

The steps leading to the establishment of the national MPDSR programme (14) are
summarized below.
 • In 2004, the WHO publication Beyond the numbers (15) recommended that all countries should
 establish medical auditing systems to reduce maternal deaths.
 • In response, Rotary International introduced its quality assurance in obstetrics programme in
 2008 and the Ondo State Government’s “Abiye” programme the following year.
 • In 2012, FMOH partnered with professional organizations, the Society of Gynaecology and
 Obstetrics of Nigeria (SOGON) and FIGO, in their LOGIC initiative, organizing workshops in
 Abuja for training and development of maternal death review tools, protocols and guidelines,
 which were validated in 2013.
 • Pilot maternal death review implementation at all federal tertiary health facilities and in Lagos,
 Ogun, Delta and the Federal Capital Territory was planned, with a review after one year,
 followed by scaling up.
 • In 2014, Nigeria’s endorsement of the 67th World Health Assembly’s resolution to eliminate
 preventable newborn deaths and stillbirths by 2030 necessitated the transformation of
 maternal death reviews into MPDSR.
 • The National MPDSR Steering Committee and its subcommittees were established in 2015;
 the national MPDSR implementation plan was approved by the National Council on Health,
 and the first MPDSR guideline was published in 2015.
 • MPDSR training workshops were held in the six geopolitical zones for core members of state
 steering committees of all 36 of Nigeria’s states and the Federal Capital Territory in October
 and November 2015. These committees were mandated to use partnerships to establish the
 programme in their states.
 • A national baseline assessment on MPDSR using mixed methods was conducted in
 September–October 2016 and published in 2017 (16). Of the 10 programmes implementing
 death audits, all focused on maternal deaths and only three included perinatal mortality.

 73
````

### Trang PDF 94

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 • In 2017 and 2018, the first MPDSR annual reports were published by individual states.
 • In 2017, Rotary International partnered with FMOH to introduce the National Obstetric Quality
 Assurance e-platform for uploading and managing MPDSR data across the country.
 • The need to scale up MPDSR to cover private health facilities, primary health centres and the
 community prompted a revision of the national MPDSR guidelines and training toolkits: the
 second edition was published in 2019.
 • In 2020, FMOH partnered with the Chairman of the Senate Committee on Health, SOGON and
 Rotary International to develop a National MPDSR Bill. The Senate and the president are now
 working to conclude the process of approval of the MPDSR Bill.
 • In July 2022, the Minister of Health launched the Third national guidelines and training toolkits
 on maternal, perinatal and child death surveillance and response (MPCDSR) (17), incorporating
 into the pre-existing MPDSR a maternal and perinatal database for quality, equity and dignity
 (MPD4QED), child death audits and e-platform use, and redefined the approach to expanding
 MPDSR to the primary health care level. It also introduced the transition from ICD-10 to ICD-11.
 • In August 2022, a major training of trainers was held on MPCDSR to initiate the cascading of
 training through the geopolitical zones to the states.

 7.4 Current status of MPDSR implementation
 MPDSR implementation has progressed rapidly with the support of different stakeholders.

 We still have the SOGON [at] the forefront of it. We now have other associations, like
 the paediatrics associations, the newborn associations, and a lot of implementing
 partners also supporting. Of course Government is there and the subnational
 governments. Within the United Nations agencies, WHO is a key partner, UNICEF
 participates, and also UNFPA. They have been very supportive. (FMOH official)

 Findings from the case study undertaken for this report were compared with those reported in
 the 2017 MPDSR Baseline Survey (16).

 In 2017, national stakeholders reported that MPDSR is an FMOH priority, noting its inclusion in
 major policy documents, such as the National Strategic Health Development Plan. However,
 although state-level stakeholders reported widespread orientation and dissemination of the
 national MPDSR guidelines, processes to implement MPDSR were limited. Many facilities were
 unaware of or were not using the new national MPDSR guidelines, and others found the tools
 voluminous and the language too technical. In all but one state, tertiary, secondary and primary
 health facilities had either not implemented or were still at the level of creating awareness of
 MPDSR. Reviewing causes and avoidable factors relating to maternal deaths and recommending
 changes were not widespread. There was poor documentation in clinical records. Stillbirths and
 neonatal deaths were not included in notification and data collection, and there was almost no
 review of care received prior to these deaths. Political commitment was considered low, and it
 was felt that there was neither a strategic implementation plan for MPDSR nor linkage to the
 national quality improvement framework. MPDSR was reported to be poorly funded and largely
 donor-dependent. The number of health workers was inadequate, and they were reportedly
 resistant to change and unaware of, or misinformed about, the “no name, no blame” mortality
 audit approach. It was also reported that MPDSR committees did not pursue the implementation
 of recommendations.

 By 2022, there was a greater sense of the Government’s commitment to MPDSR implementation.

74
````

### Trang PDF 95

````text
7. Case study: Nigeria


Three factors that reflect this commitment were identified by an FMOH official: the Government
has taken ownership and MPDSR is now a programme in FMOH; a bill is currently on its way
towards presidential assent; and guidelines have been developed at the national level and are
being adapted at the subnational level.

Implementing death reviews resulted in positive outcomes. According to a state MPDSR
committee member:
 The one thing that has been significant … is the improvement in the quality of care …
 We were able to standardize treatment protocol[s] and management. We have enough
 information and feedback in some areas of reproductive health to understand what
 the challenges [are] and how to overcome them.

National MPDSR guidelines and training toolkits became widely available and health workers
received training at national, zonal, state, facility and community levels. Annual MPDSR reports
were available from 13 out of 36 states and the federal capital territory (38%), some of which
included data on death notification, reviews and recommendations for up to five consecutive years.
As one state MPDSR committee chairman reported:

 “MPDSR is fully established at all facility levels, from primary [and] secondary to
 tertiary; we have facility-based MPDSR [committees] that are very established, and
 they are reporting actively … we have not really been able to actually break through
 [with] … community-based MPDSR activities such as verbal autopsies.”

Funding for MPDSR was less of an issue in 2022. A senior FMOH official reported:
 [At the federal level] we have our own budget line in Government that supports
 activities, especially [the development of] policies and capacity-building. We have our
 donors through the implementing partners that support [us], and United Nations
 agencies like WHO that provide a lot of technical assistance, along with other United
 Nations agencies like UNICEF.

A director of a secondary health facility reported:
 In 2018, our request for funding was met, and since then we’ve [received] dedicated
 funding within the annual budget for MPDSR. We have also had funding support from
 Evidence for Action.

In another state, the Government gave written approval to hospital medical directors to fund
MPDSR activities and implement recommendations from their internally generated revenue.

All respondents in this study who were involved in MPDSR at state level concurred that training
had allayed health workers’ fears of blame or recrimination resulting from death reviews and
confirmed that they were not aware of any such occurrences. Additionally, in 2022 the National
MPDSR Bill provided for a “no name, no blame” environment in MPDSR practice.

Counting maternal and newborn deaths and stillbirths
MPDSR committees operate at field level (where deaths occur) and at local, state and national
levels. Every facility committee is expected to identify maternal and perinatal deaths occurring
within that facility and to notify deaths using national tools and an e-platform. The MPDSR
officer is expected to complete the death notification and death review forms within 24 hours.
 75
````

### Trang PDF 96

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 The notification form is given to the medical records officer, who uploads its contents to the
 national MPDSR e-platform. The completed review form is given to the facility MPDSR committee
 secretary, who uses it to present the mortality case to the next committee meeting for review and
 action planning, after which it is forwarded to the medical records officer for uploading. Stakeholders
 reported that, although almost all maternal and perinatal deaths in facilities were notified, this
 often occurred after 24 hours, because of the heavy schedules of the staff responsible.

 Community informants in each community determine whether there has been a death of any
 woman of reproductive age (15–50 years). Any maternal deaths identified are promptly notified to
 the responsible party at the primary health centre, who alerts the community MPDSR committee.
 Three committee members conduct a condolence visit to the family of the deceased within
 48 hours, complete the death notification form, identify potential respondents for the verbal
 autopsy, and secure appointments to conduct the verbal and social autopsies within seven days.
 Data are entered into the national tools and dispatched to the local governmental monitoring and
 evaluation (M&E) officer, who uploads them onto the national MPDSR e-platform.

 Reviewing maternal and perinatal deaths
 The national guidelines require death reviews to be conducted at monthly or fortnightly committee
 meetings (depending on the prevailing local death burden). According to stakeholders interviewed,
 compliance with this requirement varied. The same committee reviewed both maternal and
 perinatal deaths, but the frequency and timing of their meetings were irregular, because these
 were determined by the availability of the committee chair and members whose routine duties
 frequently necessitated postponements.

 During death review meetings, a grid analysis guide (17), which is an appendix in the MPDSR
 guidelines, is used after each case presentation to identify modifiable factors and to articulate
 recommendations and action plans. Information from the facility MPDSR is uploaded to the
 national e-platform, accessible by the state’s MPDSR steering committee, the National Centre for
 Disease Control and the National Population Commission.

 A major challenge identified in the current processes of maternal and perinatal death reviews
 was that reviewers were only able to identify causes of death and contributory factors for 40% of
 cases. Reviews at tertiary facilities identified the highest proportion of causes and contributory
 factors, while those at community level identified the fewest. Possible reasons suggested by
 respondents included the following.
 • Responsibility for and expertise to determine cause of death rested with the trained medical
 officer or coroner. Both are frequently unavailable in lower-tier health facilities.
 • Pre-service training gaps in death certification and ICD-10 coding.
 • Certificates of cause of death issued in health facilities had widely varying content and design,
 and differed from the international version.
 • Incomplete clinical documentation in case files, although some attempts were made to
 overcome this by inviting clinicians to provide additional verbal information during reviews.

 Responding to findings from maternal and perinatal death reports
 Until 2019, annual reports published by 14 states included characteristics and causes of maternal
 and perinatal deaths recorded, remediable factors identified, recommendations, and the outcome
 of implementation of recommendations. Since 2019, the MPDSR action and recommendations
 form is expected to be completed during death review meetings. It includes any issues or
 modifiable factors identified in each death and recommendations by the committee.

76
````

### Trang PDF 97

````text
7. Case study: Nigeria


Implementation of these recommendations is expected to be monitored and reported to the
committee by its response officer.

A lack of consensus exists about cause of death and thus the appropriate response. When asked
about recommendations made at death reviews addressing the delays in care (using the three-delay
model), three state MPDSR committee members who were interviewed offered different responses.
For example, one felt that 70% of deaths were caused by a failure to seek care in a timely fashion,
while another said that only 10% were due to that delay. These differences may have arisen from
differences in the information available to reviewers. Prehospital arrival information is often not
documented because of the critical clinical conditions of these women on arrival.

Most interviewees agreed that over 80% of the recommendations made by review committees
were achievable within two years. These included employing more health workers and improving
blood banking with solar-powered generators. They also agreed that inadequate funding was the
most serious threat to the implementation of recommendations.

MPDSR data management
When MPDSR was introduced in 2015, data processing was designed to be paper-based.
Completed forms from all facilities and communities were submitted to the local governmental
authority’s M&E officer, who sent the data to the state MPDSR committee’s desk officer, the
National Centre for Disease Control and the National Population Commission. In 2016, Options’
Evidence-for-Action (Mamaye) programme introduced the first e-platform for MPDSR data
management in Lagos and Ondo states. However, in 2017, Rotary International partnered with the
German Government to develop and introduce the electronic National Obstetric Quality Assurance
platform to support MPDSR implementation. Almost all states currently use this system for
secondary and tertiary health facility MPDSR data.

As described above, access to the MPDSR e-platform is strictly guarded. Medical records officers
upload data for facilities and local governmental M&E officers upload data from communities
through an app installed on their personal mobile phones.

The e-platform is versatile. Data can readily be analysed and presented as tables, graphs or
narrative reports. Aggregated and processed data are downloaded by the technical subcommittee
of the state or national steering committees for use at review meetings.

Each stakeholder representative on the multidisciplinary MPDSR committees in facilities and
communities is expected to communicate relevant feedback to their stakeholder group. The
response officers appointed by the MPDSR committees are expected to monitor implementation
of recommended actions.

The key challenges to Nigeria’s current MPDSR data management processes include the
continued use of paper forms for data collection in facilities and communities, which has funding
and logistic implications.

Reliance on medical records officers’ use of their personal mobile phones assumes that they all
possess devices that allow installation of the e-platform app and can afford to upload data
regularly. Respondents confirmed that these issues have not been satisfactorily addressed and
may contribute to gaps in data being reported.

One benefit of the MPDSR data management systems is that MPDSR data feeds into the
national HMIS, the National Centre for Disease Control and the National Population Commission
systems. However, stakeholders confirmed that although the MPDSR e-platform is accessible
to these offices, they still operate three separate data systems, with no data triangulation.

 77
````

### Trang PDF 98

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 This is further compounded by the reported need to also integrate the e-platform developed in
 the recently concluded WHO-funded MPD4QED study, which is transitioning data from 54 federal
 government-owned tertiary health facilities to the national system. There has been steady
 progress in national dissemination of MPDSR data. Most outputs have been from state MPDSR
 steering committees, researchers and partners, in the form of annual reports and publications in
 scientific journals and newspapers. Fourteen of the 36 states had compiled and disseminated
 annual reports. A state commissioner reported:

 In a northern State, the state MPDSR committee published excerpts from their annual
 report in a newspaper in 2018, captioned “30 hospitals record 123 maternal deaths in
 six months”. Within 48 hours of this publication [the state administration] made
 announcements of interventions to improve blood banking and electricity power
 supply to hospitals and to employ medical officers for primary health centres.

 MPDSR funding
 Until recently, as much as 90% of all funding for MPDSR in Nigeria came from international partners.
 In 2017, the Federal Capital Territory administration allocated 10 million naira (approximately
 US$ 13 200) annually to MPDSR. Other states, including Delta, Ekiti, Lagos and Ondo, followed
 suit. Federal and state funding of MPDSR has been increasing, albeit slowly. The federal
 Government’s recent establishment of the interministerial platform covering reproductive,
 maternal, newborn, child, adolescent and elderly health plus nutrition introduced budgetary
 funding that was used to publish the 2022 national MPCDSR guidelines and training toolkits. In
 many states, government funding of MPDSR has been extra-budgetary: the Government simply
 authorizes each hospital’s medical director, who also heads the facility MPDSR committee, to
 fund the committee’s activities (including implementation of recommendations) from the
 institution’s internally generated revenue. Many respondents stated that this strategy may have
 accounted for the swift national expansion of the MPDSR programme at a time of dwindling
 resources. An FMOH official said: “There is also widespread acknowledgement that MPDSR is
 about the least expensive MNCH strategy yet introduced to this country!”

 7.5 Developing a national legal framework to support MPDSR
 When maternal death reviews and subsequently MPDSR were introduced, it was understood that
 their full implementation would require legislative support, given the novel concept and methods
 involved. This was underpinned by the need to safeguard its “no name, no blame” ethos, and the
 confidentiality of information, and to protect its operators from blame and recrimination, without
 impeding the rights of deceased’s families to seek legal redress.

 Although none of the expected incidents of blame relating to MPDSR have been reported across
 the country, efforts for MPDSR legislation began in 2013 (16). SOGON, which initiated this
 process in partnership with Jhpiego and Kogi and Ebonyi state governments, successfully urged
 members to advocate for MPDSR laws in their own states. Laws were enacted in Delta, Kogi,
 Lagos and Sokoto states; the process is ongoing in four additional states: Ebonyi, Imo, Jigawa
 and Ondo. SOGON built on its facilitation of the establishment of maternal death review and later
 MPDSR programmes in Ebonyi and Kogi states by urging their ministries of health to sponsor
 executive bills in their respective State Houses of Assembly to entrench MPDSR. SOGON, with
 partners, developed a template that could be adapted by the two states. Kogi State’s bill provided
 the template for the national bill. Rotary International’s country director advocated for its
 replication at the national level, convening successive meetings of MPDSR stakeholders to
 develop a roadmap for its achievement.
78
````

### Trang PDF 99

````text
7. Case study: Nigeria


Using the SOGON template, an implementation plan was developed to pursue a National MPDSR
Bill, but it was uncertain whether the bill would be presented to the national legislature by a private
member or as an executive bill (from the FMOH). Progress ceased for about seven months
because no lead could be found for the next step. This stalemate was alleviated after Rotary
International’s country director advocated to the FMOH leadership: a tripartite discussion took
place with the chairman of the Senate Committee on Health, followed by a series of consultative
meetings of stakeholders in the office of the senator. These efforts led to the crafting of a private
member’s bill sponsored by the senator, which passed the initial mandatory readings in Senate
unchallenged. Although the Bill’s public hearing, ahead of the third and final reading at Senate,
was very well attended by stakeholders, opposition was expressed by some stakeholders in
federal government ministries, departments and agencies that alleged non-inclusiveness during
its development. Fresh consultations followed, capturing all stakeholders’ inputs, and consensus
was reached with a new Bill, which was fully accepted by the FMOH, then submitted to the
Senate for a successful third reading.

At the time of the writing, advocacy efforts to secure the President’s assent to the National MPDSR
Bill are ongoing: at the 11th International Scientific Conference of SOGON (22–26 November 2022),
SOGON’s president made a passionate appeal to the President of Nigeria to “sign the Bill and
make it one of his legacies to Nigerians”.

The resolve of Rotary International’s country director and its financial support, the unwavering
commitment of the FMOH leadership and head of its Family Health Department, coupled with
the resilience of SOGON, the Paediatric Association of Nigeria and other stakeholders, created
momentum for national MPDSR legislation. The chairman of the Senate Committee on Health,
a physician experienced in development work, with his counterpart in the lower House of
Representatives, were key enablers within the legislative process. The cost of the entire legislative
process, including hosting the stakeholders’ meetings and advocacy sessions, was estimated by
one international organization at US$ 10 000.

Nigeria’s experience clearly indicates the essential elements of the MPDSR legislative process,
including:
 • identifying, encouraging and supporting champions of the process, as was done by Rotary
 International’s country director;
 • unity, commitment and dedication to a common cause by stakeholders, without wavering
 when challenges emerge;
 • good stakeholder mapping and ensuring inclusivity throughout the process, to avert
 interruptions and delays such as those encountered during the public hearing on the draft bill;
 • identifying allies, synergies and potential facilitators within the legislative organization, whose
 involvement will enhance and expedite the law-making process;
 • sourcing adequate funds to implement the activities necessary before, during and after the
 legislative process;
 • strong political will and commitment, as demonstrated by the consistent support from the
 head of FMOH.

The comprehensive National MPDSR Bill lays out the framework for the establishment, composition
and functions of MPDSR committees at all levels, including for the private sector. It specifies
operational procedures, duties and responsibilities, along with offences and penalties for
breaching laws relating to death notifications, confidentiality and maintenance of no-blame
requirements during the review process. Dissemination of the Bill will occur after securing the
President’s assent; no detailed plan has yet been developed for this.

 79
````

### Trang PDF 100

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 It was observed that only stakeholders directly involved at various stages of the Bill’s development
 were aware of its provisions. Careful planning is therefore needed for the dissemination of the
 Bill and the monitoring of its implementation to ensure that all the stakeholders are acquainted
 with its provisions.

 7.6 Factors contributing to successful MPDSR implementation
 The key successes of Nigeria’s MPDSR implementation include: its uniform nationwide acceptance
 and implementation; improving quality of care in health facilities, uptake of evidence-based
 advocacy for improved community behaviour and practices; countrywide use of an MPDSR
 e-platform for improved data management; steadily increasing political will and commitment,
 exemplified by increasing budgetary allocation to MPDSR and supporting legislation at both
 federal and state levels; and the dissemination of outcomes by state governments in scientific
 journals, MPDSR annual reports and wider media.

 Stakeholders at various levels have affirmed the success of the country’s MPDSR programme.
 An FMOH official noted the successful institutionalization of MPDSR:

 Government has taken ownership … It is now a programme in the Ministry of Health …
 at the national level and in most states, so this is a success story.

 The head of a secondary health facility noted:
 Every death that comes is accounted [for] and is in the consciousness of all our
 facility staff now. No death should occur without questions being asked. So they are
 more aware, and they are alert to their responsibilities.

 Professional associations played a key role in the evolution of MPDSR in Nigeria. Most interviewees
 concurred that the successful implementation of MPDSR was largely attributable to the deep
 commitment and engagement of professional associations such as SOGON, the Paediatric
 Association of Nigeria and the Nigerian Society of Neonatal Medicine. They consistently facilitated
 advocacy and capacity-building activities alongside other partners. Their nationwide membership
 network and expertise helped to achieve implementation at all levels.

 A staff member of a United Nations organization noted:

 I think the level of commitment and interest is very high, based on the numbers of
 these organizations and their level of involvement in the processes, especially in the
 legislative process. That galvanized people and organizations to take more interest in
 MPDSR, and from that process … it gained momentum.

 7.7 Challenges and potential solutions
 Table 7.1 summarizes the challenges to Nigeria’s MPDSR programme and the solutions
 suggested in the publications reviewed and the interviews conducted in this case study.
````

````text
 Additional persisting challenges need to be addressed. The proportion of deaths for which
 reviewers were able to identify causes and contributory factors was estimated to average about

 For many births outside of a facility, potentially harmful traditional practices persist. One example,
````

### Trang PDF 103

````text
7. Case study: Nigeria

7.8 Conclusions
Six years after the baseline MPDSR assessment was conducted in Nigeria, this case study used
evidence from published reports, scientific articles and other works to demonstrate the
considerable progress achieved across the country. Not only has MPDSR been accepted and
institutionalized in many states, but it has led to the establishment of a nationwide e-platform.
It has received formal budgetary funding and delivered on its quality improvement agenda.
There has been a steady increase in the number of annual MPDSR reports published by states.
Furthermore, a national bill has been passed that assures mandatory reporting of maternal and
perinatal mortalities, budgetary funding and protection of confidentiality, and eight states have
followed this example.

As reported by its stakeholders, MPDSR has performed well in Nigeria in the health facilities and
states in which it has been established, but major gaps exist in its coverage, requiring a significant
amount of work for the country to derive the full benefits of MPDSR.

The existing decentralized health system, coupled with wide differences in human resources for
health across states, remains a major constitutional impediment to preventing maternal and
perinatal deaths. Almost 60% of states are not engaged in MPDSR activities and need further
encouragement and support. The primary health care and community levels of the health
system, where most of maternal and perinatal deaths occur and where key solutions to prevent
even facility deaths could be found, are yet to be covered – the quote from Kaduna State
indicates the huge potential of verbal and social autopsy to improve preventive care uptake and
reduce type one delays, if only it is more widely implemented. Private health facilities, widely
believed to provide 60% of the country’s health services, are about to commence MPDSR.
Integration of MPDSR and MPD4QED has just commenced for tertiary health facilities.

Unfortunately for MPDSR, the period since its introduction in Nigeria 2015 has been one of
economic downturn coincident with an upturn in security challenges – both of these are direct
threats to the health and well-being of women and newborns, and to the MPDSR initiative. If
appropriately supported and pursued, the Nigerian MPDSR initiative that “counts every death
and makes every death count” is likely to reduce the country’s high burden of maternal and
perinatal deaths.


 83
````

````text
 This case study showcases North Macedonia’s experiences in implementing perinatal death reviews,
````
