### Trang PDF 109

````text
8. Case study: North Macedonia

8.2 Methodology
The protocol described in Chapter 2 was implemented and conducted from February to August 2022.

Documents reviewed included national reports, situation analyses, publications on perinatal and
maternal data collection, and information about past and current interventions that affect maternal
and neonatal health. Fifteen stakeholders interviewed had experience in implementing maternal
and perinatal death reviews in North Macedonia and included 11 obstetric and neonatal care
providers. Other interviewees were responsible for data collection and analysis or were actively
involved in MCH programmes in the Ministry of Health, professional organizations or CSOs
working in MCH.

All interviews were conducted by an obstetrician experienced in perinatal audit and data collection,
and a member of the national Perinatal Mortality Review Committee (PMRC), using the topic guide.
Each interviewee provided informed consent.

8.3 Present situation and historical aspects
Maternal and neonatal deaths and stillbirths are registered with the Office of the State Registry
and Vital Statistics, which shares data with the State Statistical Office. Data are published every
quarter, and a web-based annual report on vital statistics published every June includes data on
maternal and neonatal deaths and stillbirths. The reports include crude numbers collected from
all health facilities in North Macedonia. A process of revision of coding (using ICD-10 classification)
follows, involving experts from the Institute of Mother and Child Health and the Institute of
Epidemiology. The revised data are then shared with the Institute of Public Health for additional
analyses. These processes are affected by incomplete data forms, and by lack of specific data
about adverse conditions during and after pregnancy and childbirth, and their links with maternal
and neonatal deaths. Inadequate coding of causes of death hampers the further classification,
analysis and revision of cases.

Over the last 40 years, several attempts have been made to establish a review process that
encompasses the collection and analysis of maternal and perinatal health data and the
drafting of evidence-based recommendations to improve maternal and perinatal health in
North Macedonia. Between 1983 and 1989, every maternal and neonatal death at the University
Clinic in Skopje was reviewed. However, the breakup of Yugoslavia interrupted this review process.
It was restarted in the early 1990s when the Ministry of Health established the Centre for
Reproductive Health in the University Clinic of Obstetrics and Gynaecology in Skopje. A network of
maternity institutions was established, and each was required to submit data relating to the
medical/obstetric history of mothers and neonates. Experts from the University Clinic visited
maternity units to verify and correct the data when necessary. All data collected were manually
entered onto computers and analysed. Annual perinatal reports (1) included the numbers and
rates of perinatal mortality, but not maternal mortality cases. Following the publication of these
reports, heads of maternity wards and birthing rooms would jointly discuss the results, identify
problems, find solutions and recommend improvements. During this period (1992–1994), a new
medical and obstetrical history form based on WHO recommendations was introduced. North
Macedonia also joined the obstetrical quality indicators and data collection project on quality
development in perinatal care in Europe. However, in-depth analysis of the causes of perinatal
and maternal mortality was still not carried out.

The Ministry of Health’s National Strategy for Sexual and Reproductive Health, 2010–2020 (6) set
specific targets to reduce maternal and perinatal mortality, including skilled attendance at all births,
MMR to be less than five deaths per 100 000 live births, perinatal mortality rate to be reduced by 30%
and all hospitals to be subject to accreditation according to their “mother-and-baby friendliness”.
 89
````

### Trang PDF 110

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 During this time, the Government’s official databases showed worsening trends in perinatal
 health indicators. This led to NGOs conducting further analyses in 2016 and 2019 to discover the
 underlying reasons. These organizations assessed the quality of antenatal and perinatal care and
 searched for the causes of worsening perinatal health, especially among vulnerable groups such
 as the Roma population (7, 8).

 Perinatal Mortality Steering Committee (PMSC)
 North Macedonia took an important step in the analysis of perinatal mortality in 2019, establishing
 the PMSC at the Ministry of Health. The PMSC was formed by ministerial order on 20 February
 2019 and tasked with analysing all stillbirths and neonatal deaths (over 22 gestational weeks and
 up to 28 days of life). The PMSC is an interdisciplinary team of obstetricians, neonatologists,
 paediatricians, midwives, nurses, social workers, pathologists, epidemiologists, anaesthesiologists,
 public health specialists, representatives of the Ministry of Health, and representatives of UNICEF,
 UNFPA and WHO. The PMSC aims to identify possible systematic issues and modifiable factors in
 the provision of health care and to recommend actions to reduce North Macedonia’s perinatal
 mortality. It has supervisory and advisory roles in the data collection and review processes.

 Strong initial support was provided by WHO, UNICEF and UNFPA in the training of PMSC members
 on the WHO methodology relating to the perinatal audit process and the translation of the WHO
 guidance documents (9, 10) into Macedonian. Expert advice from international consultants
 helped to boost the perinatal review process through developing high-quality data collection
 tools and supporting comprehensive, in-depth data analyses.

 The PMSC supported the development of the perinatal mortality audit system, and a framework
 for its work was developed. PMSC members signed agreements with the Ministry of Health that
 guaranteed confidentiality and immunity from any judicial proceedings connected with the
 deaths reviewed. Rules on the appointment of members to the committee were established, and
 it was expected that sustainable funding would be ensured through the existing MCH programme
 at the Ministry of Health.

 Perinatal Mortality Review Committee (PMRC)
 A subgroup of academic and clinical members of the PMSC (obstetricians, neonatologists and
 paediatricians) form the PMRC. The PMRC is required to collect data on perinatal deaths, collate
 data according to predefined variables, and classify and produce in-depth analysis of perinatal
 mortality in the country. In PMRC meetings, members decide which cases should be analysed in
 depth. Cases are selected depending on the total number of reported perinatal deaths (to get a
 manageable number to review) and the possible influence of findings for improving aspects of
 perinatal care (e.g. perinatal deaths in late pregnancy or during labour may be selected over fetal
 deaths at 24 weeks).

 A network was established to obtain medical histories of all stillbirths and neonatal deaths in the
 country, including “zero reporting”. The technical officer at the Ministry of Health collects and
 delivers data to the chair of the PMRC. The process is paper-based, using anonymized medical
 histories. Sometimes cases are reported without necessary medical data, which makes it difficult
 to determine the real causes of perinatal deaths. Diagnoses and causes of death are classified
 using the WHO application of ICD-10 to deaths during the perinatal period, which links perinatal
 deaths to contributing maternal conditions.

 The PMRC published its first national perinatal mortality audit report in 2020 (1), which analyses
 perinatal deaths identified and reviewed in 2019. It highlights key findings, identifies delays in
 seeking and receiving appropriate health interventions, and includes strong recommendations

90
````

### Trang PDF 111

````text
8. Case study: North Macedonia


for improving the quality of health care. The report showed that quality of care for women and
babies during and after pregnancy and childbirth in North Macedonia was suboptimal and that
actions to address these shortfalls were required to avoid adverse outcomes. Among the
problems identified were lack of access to client information and limited knowledge among
primary health workers (“primary obstetrician–gynaecologists”) about antenatal care visits,
detection of risk factors complicating pregnancies, and the need for screening tests for
maternal infections such as HIV and syphilis that affect perinatal outcomes. Improving for
primary health workers’ and hospital doctors’ access to antenatal care information was
therefore an important recommendation.

Other health interventions and outcomes found to be suboptimal included: infrequent fetal heart
rate monitoring during labour; high incidence of birth asphyxia; high proportion of stillbirths
delivered by caesarean section; failure to administer antenatal corticosteroids for suspected
preterm labour; failure to administer magnesium sulfate for neuroprotection; inadequate referral
process; and lack of prereferral stabilization of neonates. In-depth analysis of specific cases
showed that the major causes of neonatal deaths were asphyxia and respiratory distress relating
to prematurity. Evidence-based preventative and treatment options, although available, were not
used. Essential newborn care, a package of simple interventions to improve newborn outcomes,
was not provided routinely.

One neonatologist interviewed said the most important thing is to analyse all newborn deaths
regardless of whether they were born prematurely or at term. For example, the increasing number
of newborns who are born at term and die from asphyxia “are a big enigma for us. We should
have more data because we constantly asked ourselves what happened during the birth”.

The PMRC’s 2020 perinatal mortality audit report led the Ministry of Health to develop the Perinatal
Care Master Plan for 2020–2030 (11), which provides consolidated analyses of the current
organization of the perinatal care system across the entire spectrum of service delivery. The
Master Plan also sets out recommendations for improving maternal and neonatal health
outcomes by establishing risk-appropriate care and by rationalizing and optimizing maternal and
newborn service provision, with strong quality assurance systems and relevant health
information support. Recommendations for service delivery improvements include service
organization by level of care, infrastructure, equipment, human resources, transport and the
referral system. A framework of perinatal care quality indicators was established, with a
standardized system for high-quality perinatal data collection, analysis, reporting and utilization.

The PMRC’s findings and recommendations were shared among professionals, especially in the
institutions where PMRC members work (some members are heads of neonatal intensive care
units and thus personally involved in many of the cases reviewed). The resulting peer influence
led to significant changes in practices in those institutions. However, the PMRC works only at
national level, and none of the institutions conducts facility-level reviews.

The PMRC’s second perinatal mortality audit report, which will include COVID-19-related data, is
expected soon (1).

8.4 Factors contributing to success
Leadership from the Ministry of Health was an important factor, facilitating implementation and
investment in perinatal death reviews and the creation of a supportive community of stakeholders.
WHO regional and country leadership supported the formation of the PMSC to implement
perinatal death reviews. United Nations organizations supported the preparatory phase of
translating tools into Macedonian and training of members of the PMSC.

 91
````

### Trang PDF 112

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 In general, the proposed activities usually come from the health-service professionals,
 health associations and United Nations organizations. The role of these organizations
 is to give recommendations and advice and to press the ministry to improve national
 statistical data. For the last 10 years the ministry has had an open policy in accepting
 recommendations from professional associations and has [cooperated widely with
 nongovernmental and international organizations]. (MoH official)

 How to convince the Ministry of Health how important this topic is, and the value of
 [MPDSR]? By discussing, by elaborating on the importance of perinatal mortality, and
 with certain special measures, great results can be achieved. It should be explained
 that good results can be achieved with viable measures … and not very expensive
 activities. (Neonatologist)

 The anonymization of data established by the PMSC in the regulatory framework ensured
 confidentiality, protected personal data and specified ways of destroying confidential data
 after analysis.

 Transparency of the perinatal mortality audit process promoted a “no-blame culture” to protect
 stakeholders and health workers who were identified during PMRC meetings. The director of every
 hospital with labour rooms, the chief obstetrician and the chief midwife were invited to participate
 in a one-day meeting where the importance of close collaboration between institutions was
 explained. By discussing and clarifying the process of data collection and audit, unanimous
 support for the project was obtained, making this one of the strongest aspects of implementation.

 I think that if someone explains that they will not be charged any penalty, and that
 data will not be used in court proceedings, every health worker will tell the truth,
 because every health worker wants to work in better conditions. Every health worker
 is well aware of what is missing in his institution, but cannot make changes alone.
 So, when problems are pointed out, the whole system can be operationalized to
 improve conditions. (Public health expert)

 The PMRC established a step-by-step process with in-person meetings. All members were able
 to join the discussion, follow up the process and be advised by experts. Since this form of audit
 was new for all PMRC members, step-by-step work with international experts clarified the
 methods used and provided guidance for in-depth reviews. Experts answered questions arising
 during training and explained how to extract important data while maintaining objectiveness,
 and how to structure a report after data analysis. This support was very important to PMRC
 members, giving them confidence in case analysis. When writing reports, every member
 can now ask for further analysis and suggest important findings for inclusion in the report.
 Key findings and evidence-based recommendations are based on the results of the audit and are
 organized in a manner that is easy to follow and implement.

 A complete audit … would include good statistical epidemiological data on perinatal and
 neonatal mortality in the country [and] make a comparison with surrounding countries at
 the same level as we are. [The aim is] to find the real causes [and] where the problems
 are – maybe at a local level, … [or at] the primary, secondary or tertiary health care level …
 and act there. There is no need to point out mistakes to any health worker when the
 real challenge is to find out [the level at which the problem] was missed. (Gynaecologist)

92
````

### Trang PDF 113

````text
8. Case study: North Macedonia


Strong motivation among PMRC members, educated and supported by international consultants
and the Ministry of Health: PMRC members now feel engaged in and encouraged to continue the
process based on the knowledge and experience gained.

 Monitoring should be serious: not just to see whether or not the guideline is being
 implemented but, if not, why it is not being implemented, and to support staff capacity.
 Once the clinical guideline is implemented, we should develop a strategy for adequate
 transfer of information and transportation [of patients] between health facilities.
 (Gynaecologist)

8.5 Implementation challenges
The case study revealed several challenges for implementing perinatal mortality audits in
North Macedonia.

Data collection process. In many cases, essential information was not recorded at primary care
level, so the data used are mainly from hospital records. There were inconsistencies and
inaccuracies in the registration of perinatal deaths, despite efforts to train health personnel and
provide them with definitions and codes relevant to antenatal and postnatal care (12).

Some general data about perinatal indicators are collected by the Institute for Mother and Child
Health, but these are not publicly available. The Institute’s report (5) is distributed by email
annually to some stakeholders and can only be obtained on request from the Institution.

Regulations govern the electronic forms used for data collection (demographic, antenatal,
perinatal and other data), which should be filled by health workers at all levels of care during and
following pregnancy and childbirth. In reality, forms are not completed, hampering the integration
of data about all levels of antenatal and perinatal care.

 In general, I think that doctors have little interest in dealing with statistical processing of
 data. My professor taught me that if you operate on one woman and save one life … you
 can save another woman as well. Legal regulation exists, but some institutions have a
 manager/director who wants to skip certain situations because that situation could
 affect the clinic’s reputation and could have repercussions on its work. When a [woman]
 or a newborn dies, all the blame falls on the doctor who was part of the intervention,
 but I disagree with that: I think that the system in general is a problem. (Gynaecologist)

Technical assistance from the Ministry of Health. Data are supposed to be collected and
summarized by the Ministry of Health. The ministerial order provided that all data received should
be collected every 15 days, and training was provided for this at the beginning of the project.
However, occupants of the post have changed, and at the time of writing the post is vacant.

Concerns about sustainable funding. Although the programme was established as a high priority for
the Ministry of Health, all its funding was from external sources. Funding should have been allocated
specifically for the PMRC’s activities, such as expert analyses, report drafting, and disseminating key
findings and recommendations to health-service institutions, health workers and the general public.

 The state should constantly invest in the health system to improve it and facilitate the
 work of health workers, and this would improve people’s health. … in my opinion at
 least five years of investment are needed. (Paediatrician)

 93
````

### Trang PDF 114

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 COVID-19 restrictions raised another barrier to the work of the PMRC, by preventing physical
 meetings. Discussions by email or online did not compensate for the more open and constructive
 meetings before the pandemic. This obstacle will hopefully reduce over time.

 The pandemic had a negative impact, especially on the work of our committee.
 Despite the fact that we had the histories for analysis, we were not able to have
 physical meetings because many of the doctors were sick. We could not discuss
 cases as we did before COVID-19, face to face. (Neonatologist)

 Maternal mortality data collection and review are non-existent in North Macedonia. Although all
 deaths are registered, inadequate coding and lack of linkage to pregnancy status results in cases
 of maternal mortality not being fully registered. In 2010, when six to eight women died during
 pregnancy and labour within a short period of time, a new group made field visits to analyse the
 conditions and causes leading to those deaths. However, that process was not sustained in the
 following years.

 Urgent actions for the implementation of the [PMRC’s] recommendations are the
 inclusion of maternal mortality and near-miss cases … Updated questionnaires/forms,
 and well structured regional analysis, are needed in order to [prepare] more
 appropriate reports. (Gynaecologist)

 8.6 Limitations of the study
 Few publications have addressed issues around perinatal health in North Macedonia. Many
 documents reviewed were prepared by ad-hoc working groups and are not publicly available.
 However, a general understanding of the trends, gaps, under-registration and situation analysis
 could be obtained from the available documents.

 As noted earlier, the interviewees were deeply involved in perinatal health-care processes,
 preparing documents to improve the data collection process, analysis and review of perinatal
 and maternal mortality, and were all experts in their field. Some were part of different review
 processes; others were chairs of committees that influenced Ministry of Health decisions.
 Despite having different backgrounds, their opinions overall are very similar: the process of
 perinatal and maternal review was initially supported by the Ministry of Health and international
 organizations when mortality rates were high but, over time, the Ministry of Health’s responses
 to proposed recommendations from the review process have become suboptimal. With no
 internal budget allocated to this process, the committees’ work was adversely affected when
 international funding ended. At times, activities for identifying and reviewing perinatal and
 maternal mortality overlapped or were absent. Not all interviewees were aware of pre-existing
 processes of the Ministry of Health or other organizations.

 In their opinion, if accountability is imposed for data collection (with regulations, fines and rewards),
 institutions and individuals will be obliged to comply with specified criteria for reporting.
 Otherwise, without a no-blame culture in all institutions, health workers may choose not to report
 cases of mortality, especially maternal deaths.


94
````

### Trang PDF 115

````text
8. Case study: North Macedonia

8.7 Conclusions
The Government of North Macedonia has embraced the concept of UHC and is committed to
improving the health of all its citizens, regardless of where they live and irrespective of their
capacity to pay. In its transformational journey for quality and efficiency in health care, strong
emphasis should be put on implementing MPDSR as an essential process for determining the
quality of the health system and for providing MNH care.

Much has been learned about the challenges of improving MNH in North Macedonia, with the
establishment of the PMRC, publication of the first national report on perinatal mortality, and the
Perinatal Care Master Plan for 2020–2030.

Strengthening the collection and analysis of data will place North Macedonia in a better position
to identify and address gaps and bottlenecks in health-care provision to accelerate national
progress to improve the health of women and children.

Efforts should be made to sustain support for the MPDSR process, because effective
implementation of the response phase of the MPDSR cycle will improve the performance and
quality of maternal and perinatal health services.

Despite the uncertainties in the region, the Government of North Macedonia, through its
Ministry of Health and professionals, must strive to provide optimal care for the country’s women
and children. Continuing to implement MPDSR, with Government support, is one way to achieve
this goal.


 95
````

### Trang PDF 120

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 9.1 Background

 Maternal and neonatal mortality in Pakistan
 A steady reduction in maternal mortality has been observed in Pakistan over the last two
 decades. In 2020, the United Nations reported that its MMR was 154 deaths per 100 000 live births
 (UI: 109–226) (1). Based on national data from 2000–2019, MMR decreased from 294 to 186 (2).
 According to Pakistan’s 2019 maternal mortality survey, the MMR varied greatly across different
 provinces, with Punjab having the lowest (157) and Balochistan having the highest (298). The
 same report highlights 96% of maternal deaths were from preventable obstetric causes,
 including postpartum haemorrhage, high blood pressure, eclampsia and placenta previa (3).

 In 2022, the United Nations reported an NMR of 39 deaths per 1000 live births for Pakistan
 (UI: 30–50) (4). Based on national data, the NMR was 42 deaths per 1000 live births in 2018 (5).
 The major causes of neonatal mortality in Pakistan include preterm birth, intrapartum asphyxia
 and neonatal infections (5): conditions that can be addressed by better care during and
 immediately after childbirth.

 Objective and methods
 This country case study was conducted to explore the successes and challenges in implementing
 MPDSR in Pakistan, with a specific focus on the use of digital technology.

 The protocol described in Chapter 2 was implemented to collect information on MPDSR in
 Pakistan, focusing on the mobile application described below. A literature review was conducted
 by reviewing available resources on maternal and neonatal mortality in Pakistan between 1998
 and 2022. Key informant interviews were performed and followed a topic guide. Purposive
 sampling (n = 20) of professionals involved in the implementation of MPDSR was used in
 consultation with focal points from the Ministry of National Health Services, Regulations and
 Coordination (MoNHSR&C) and WHO Pakistan.

 Surveillance systems
 The four main health reporting surveillance systems in Pakistan are District Health Information
 System (DHIS), Lady Health Worker (LHW) Management Information System, Integrated Disease
 Surveillance & Response (IDSR) and CRVS.

 Maternal mortality is currently being reported in all 166 districts of Pakistan through the DHIS.
 The DHIS tools report maternal and neonatal indicators from the information on obstetric registers.
 The DHIS tools for primary and secondary health facilities report a number of indicators, including
 monthly antenatal and postnatal care visits, deaths due to birth asphyxia, neonatal sepsis or
 other causes, number of stillbirths, normal vaginal deliveries, caesarean sections and total live
 births. A constraint of (facility-based) DHIS is that maternal deaths are not disaggregated on an
 individual case basis, so the exact cause of death cannot be determined. In response to the fact
 that the information from the DHIS tools is retrieved monthly (not in real time) and is limited to
 the total number of maternal deaths, at least two provinces have rolled out DHIS2 with real-time
 data. Both use data from facilities in a district, compiled from LHWs and facilities.

 Maternal and infant deaths in the community are reported using the LHW Management
 Information System and verified later through verbal autopsies conducted by LHW supervisors.
 The CRVS focuses mainly on location and causes of death, although often does not capture all
 details, resulting in under-reporting and misclassification of maternal and neonatal deaths.


100
````

### Trang PDF 121

````text
9. Case study: Pakistan


The three-delay model can be used to highlight the key challenges to maternal and perinatal
mortality surveillance in Pakistan. Cultural factors (e.g. low decision-making power of women in
households), limited education/knowledge and poor socioeconomic status contribute to the first
delay. Long travel distances, issues with transportation (availability and funds) and weak referral
mechanisms contribute to the second delay (6, 7). Issues remain at the point of care, such as
lack of availability of skilled care providers and poor-quality services; however, interviews for this
case study have reported that the third delay has been reduced by the establishment of MCH
centres at various primary and tertiary health facilities, such as maternity wards and labour
rooms in tertiary health facilities with up-to-date equipment and medicines.

Policies adopted to improve maternal and neonatal mortality indicators
To address concerns about maternal and neonatal mortality, the Government of Pakistan developed
the Reproductive, maternal, newborn, child, adolescent health & nutrition strategy (2016–2020),
setting out 10 priority action areas and a five-year plan of action for regions and provinces. Its
core components include improving access to and quality of care, by empowering CHWs, such as
LHWs and midwives, and providing them with adequate facilities and resources, as well as
reducing barriers to care seeking and strengthening health systems (8). These policies create
national impetus to strategically address root causes of maternal and neonatal mortality, and
aim to provide sustainable solutions to reduce future deaths.

The Government is committed to the SDGs and is proactively following the ENAP and EPMM
initiatives to provide support at the levels of policy, service delivery, social behaviour and
quality of care for saving mothers and their infants. MoNHSR&C is collaborating with the
Pakistan Paediatric Association to develop national guidelines for small and sick newborn care
at the district level, jointly supported by WHO and UNICEF. MoNHSR&C has actively engaged
with provincial departments of health to introduce quality of care tools, such as the Robson 10
group classification for optimizing caesarean sections, early essential newborn care and the
latest WHO labour care guidelines. A recent collaboration with the Society of Obstetricians &
Gynaecologists of Pakistan focused on emergency obstetric and neonatal care training at
district levels to improve the management of maternal and newborn cases at primary and
secondary health-care levels. This society also supports maintenance of a registry at some
tertiary care hospitals to register neonatal deaths and stillbirths.

Private partners are also working on MNH. For example, the Community Health Department of
the Aga Khan University has developed a birth registry for approximately 30 districts of Sindh,
which is being used to collect information regarding stillbirths, emergency obstetric and neonatal
care services, maternal mortality and neonatal mortality.

9.2 MPDSR implementation: historical perspective and
current status
In 2013, when WHO published MDSR technical guidance (9), maternal death reporting in
Pakistan was being done through the verbal autopsy questionnaire developed by the National
Committee on Maternal and Newborn Health. MoNHSR&C and provincial departments of health,
together with partners such as WHO, UNICEF and UNFPA, undertook stakeholder consultation
to adopt MDSR guidelines. It was jointly agreed to develop national protocols on MDSR.
National guidelines were finalized in 2017, and a training package was developed, focused on
facility-based MPDSR.


 101
````

### Trang PDF 122

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 Additionally, the inclusion of a perinatal component was essential. While the perinatal component
 was introduced globally following WHO guidance on reviews of stillbirths and neonatal death in
 2016, in Pakistan it was only briefly discussed in in 2018 and protocols were recently updated
 with inclusion of perinatal component in 2023/24.

 The roll-out of MPDSR in 2017 varied by province and region and was undertaken by different
 agencies. From February 2020, the MPDSR system was significantly hindered by the COVID-19
 pandemic. Maternity caseloads at hospitals fell by 50% due to lockdowns and transportation
 bans, increasing the practice of home and community births. It was not until June 2020 that
 maternal death reporting began to rise again.

 In November 2020, provincial MPDSR review missions were conducted in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa
 and Balochistan provinces focusing on the selected teaching hospitals implementing MPDSR
 system. The leading causes of maternal deaths identified were preventable, with postpartum
 haemorrhage being the most common, followed by eclampsia and sepsis. Several maternal
 deaths due to COVID-19 were also reported in 2020.

 In 2021, the introduction of facility-based MPDSR was supported by WHO Pakistan in Punjab
 and Sindh provinces. Training of trainers was conducted in four hospitals in Punjab and five
 hospitals in Sindh in 2022. Table 9.1 summarizes milestones of MPDSR implementation reached
 by year-end 2017 and year-end 2022.
````

````text
MoNHSR&C, with support from WHO Pakistan, is currently in the process of updating national
MPDSR guidelines, training packages and tools to coincide with recent global guidance.

Simultaneously, the MDSR mobile application is being upgraded to include perinatal and maternal
near-miss forms along with tools for action planning and response tracking. MoNHSR&C is
coordinating with provincial departments of health to ensure regular MPDSR data reviews by
committees and taskforces, and extending support for data-driven responses to reduce
preventable maternal and neonatal deaths in the participating hospitals. A legal framework to
make all maternal and perinatal deaths notifiable and mandate death reviews has been drafted by
MoNHSR&C and is being pursued with the Ministry of Law for further action. Similarly, all four
provinces have been advocated for developing legal bills to support MPDSR implementation at scale.

9.3 Factors contributing to successful MPDSR implementation

MPDSR mobile application
An innovation developed by Pakistan, with support from WHO, is the MPDSR mobile/web application.
The motivation behind its development was to reduce health workers’ reporting burden and make
it easier for them to immediately report a maternal death in real time.


 103
````

````text
 Linked dashboard on the National Institute of Health server for real-time
 data reporting
 Data collected through the application is shown in the MPDSR dashboard (Fig. 9.1). This dashboard
 presents information such as the total number of maternal deaths in Pakistan, with separate tabs
 for provinces, districts and facilities, showing trends in maternal mortality, causes of maternal
 deaths and associated delays, age distribution of deaths, district-wide distribution of maternal
 deaths using colour codes, mode of delivery among the maternal deaths and frequency of
 causes of deaths.


104
````

````text
As a result of the mobile MPDSR application and linked dashboard, data on maternal deaths
(which were sometimes reported months after the event and with no probable causes) are now
being reported in real time and are digitally available to health workers, hospital administrators
and policy-makers. During the key informant interviews, respondents insisted that digitalization
of data were a key innovation to MPDSR implementation in Pakistan.

 It is going to be the future method of data collection. There is no going back to the
 paper method. We need to move on, and we need to put our emphasis on the [usage
 of] electronics [methods and] gadgets. (MoNHSR&C official)

Recently, the MPDSR dashboard has been transferred to the National Institute of Health server to
improve use of the data for proactive policy- and decision-making to reduce future maternal and
perinatal deaths in Pakistan.

Provincial situational reports
Using the real-time data collected through the mobile application, provinces began producing
situational reports (one-page summaries briefly communicating necessary information) and
sharing them with stakeholders, providing a snapshot of steps taken at the selected hospitals to
address maternal and perinatal mortality. Buy-in by health-care professionals (doctors and
managers) is increased when they see the situational reports and facility-level efforts to reduce
maternal deaths. Consequently, health workers are more interested in using the MPDSR
application regularly and scaling up its usage.

Establishment of committees
Review committees were established at provincial and facility levels in all four provinces and
Islamabad Capital Territory.

 105
````

### Trang PDF 126

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 In terms of the small milestones that we have been achieving … many MPDSR facility
 committees [are] in place [to] review maternal deaths. We also have provincial
 taskforces, provincial [maternal death review] committees, which have been
 supported to meet [regularly]. The original idea was to have these meetings annually
 at least, if not biannually. (United Nations staff)

 The establishment of committees to review deaths regularly increases involvement and buy-in by
 provincial authorities to monitor and identify common causes of maternal and perinatal deaths,
 and to respond with data-driven interventions and policies.

 Facility-level reviews
 Facility-level reviews by provincial committees and task forces led to the expansion of the
 maternal, newborn and child health programme and monitoring visits to primary health facilities
 identified as needing capacity-building. Hence, training was provided on pregnancy, childbirth,
 postpartum and newborn care for maternal and neonatal health workers. As a result of the
 quality-of-care issues identified, the WHO safe childbirth checklist was introduced in the
 maternity wards and labour rooms, and the quality of maternal and neonatal health care was
 assessed using a standardized tool. Additionally, steps were taken at the hospital level to address
 the needs of incoming maternal cases.

 We have received very good stories from various hospitals regarding succeeding in
 establishing some key services after facility-level review of the maternal deaths. For
 example, the hospitals talked about establishment of high-dependency units, bringing
 in the blood bank/blood transfusion centres, roster changes of doctors and nurses,
 etc. (MoNHSR&C official)

 9.4 Challenges for successful MPDSR implementation
 MPDSR mobile application
 Several issues were observed regarding the performance of the MPDSR mobile application.
 Health workers often encountered technical issues while using the application. Although
 technical support is available, solutions can be time-consuming and frustrating for health
 workers already under stress and with limited time.

 Issues were also observed with uptake of the application by users, such as frontline workers,
 who were less familiar with mobile application devices. To effectively capture community-based
 maternal and perinatal deaths, the application must be accessible and easy to use. Limited
 digital competencies must be taken into account, and it may be necessary to modify the
 application to improve its user-friendliness.

 In remote areas, internet connectivity was a challenge for using the application. This can lead to
 delays in filling out forms and under-reporting of deaths. Transferring knowledge of how to use
 the application was another challenge because many users who were trained in its use had
 retired or left their posts.

 Lastly, forms were often not filled in thoroughly and did not capture the full suite of information
 necessary for accurate and complete reporting. These challenges clearly indicate that while the
 mobile application is innovative and advantageous, many aspects of it still need improvement to
 facilitate its use by all of Pakistan’s health workers.

106
````

### Trang PDF 127

````text
9. Case study: Pakistan

Prioritization of perinatal death reports
To date, the focus has been on reporting maternal deaths, and initiatives to include perinatal
deaths have not yet been reflected in the data. It is good to note that the country has prioritized
the perinatal component of MPDSR implementation in Pakistan.

Fear of blame
Maternal mortality is a very sensitive social issue in Pakistan, and many health workers are
reluctant to join the MPDSR initiative for fear of being penalized and criticized, despite the “no
name, no blame” policy outlined in MPDSR guidelines. This contributes to under-reporting and
misclassification of maternal deaths.

 [Health workers] are also afraid of being sidelined or being criticized or penalized for
 this. So they are actually afraid, [and] that is one reason why [maternal deaths are] not
 properly reported. (Provincial coordinator)

Commitment at all levels
Although maternal mortality is a Government priority, additional commitment and support are
required at the national and provincial levels to advance MPDSR implementation and to increase
coverage, particularly of perinatal death notification and reporting. It is especially important to
overcome the fear of blame expressed by key informants. National commitment also plays a
critical role in creating accountability at provincial and local levels.

 Regular meetings for death reviews have been a challenge. They always need a push
 to conduct the data reviews. The provincial taskforces are being supported to meet at
 least at some frequency. The process is even slower at higher levels. There is yet to
 be a national-level committee where provincial committees could come in and show
 their progress. (United Nations staff)

High-level support and awareness building can reduce fear of blame and create accountability
for reporting, and thereby increase data that can be used to prevent future maternal and
perinatal deaths.

Key informants also noted lack of commitment from provincial governments.

 There is absolutely no communication of hospital data review committees with the
 local provincial government, which leads to no support from the provincial
 administration in implementing the actions we recommend to prevent deaths.
 (Obstetrician–gynaecologist)

There is also a strong need for administrators of health facilities to commit to garnering support
for MPDSR implementation. Challenges were observed when MPDSR was introduced into
obstetrics and gynaecology departments that lacked full support from hospital administrators.

 The hospital administration is cooperative but not serious. The facility review
 committee meetings are conducted because of the push and motivation of the
 gynaecologists. The hospital administration is hardly interested in these meetings.
 (MoNHSR&C official)

 107
````

### Trang PDF 128

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 Funding
 Lack of sustainable funding is a major challenge to the implementation of MPDSR at all levels of
 the system. Training, human resources and maintenance of the MPDSR application are examples
 of elements of the MPDSR system that require sustainable funding and support from the
 Government. Highlighting this concern, an obstetrician–gynaecologist noted: “if the funding has to
 be directed anywhere, it should be towards building the political will of the provincial governments.”

 Weak referral systems
 Many referred complicated cases have incomplete referral notes, due to a shortage of trained
 staff at primary health facilities, especially in periphery facilities. This makes it difficult to assign
 cause of death and capture delays. An obstetrician–gynaecologist said: “Patients come in with
 no referral notes [and it] creates issues in understanding the cause of maternal death, and filling
 [in] the online forms.”

 Limited capacity of health workers
 In tertiary level hospitals, there are large caseloads and severe understaffing. This leads to
 exhausted staff, with limited capacity to participate in reporting and notification for MPDSR.
 According to one respondent, health workers saw the MPDSR initiative as an increase in their
 workload with no additional incentives, but did not think it would be sustainable or possible to
 provide such incentives.

 If we give them incentives then as soon as the incentives are stopped the work will
 stop too. This initiative needs to be run through and sustained by sensitization [and]
 advocacy instead of incentivizing. (MoNHSR&C official)

 Limited capacity also hinders the sustainable institutionalization of MPDSR.

 We take things to the facilities [and] they always think that we have come [t]here
 because we want to do our research, and once we are done with our research we will
 publish and not come back. The other reason is because they always think that
 [MPDSR application is] a timebound activity and after six months we would go away
 and we would not have to do this again, so they don’t develop any real interest. They
 think that this is a “project” that is supposed to end soon, while it is actually an
 “initiative” which is meant to be institutionalized, sustained and expanded.
 (MoNHSR&C official)

 9.5 Lessons learned
 The following bullet points report suggestions and lessons learned to strengthen MPDSR
 implementation in Pakistan responding to the challenges revealed by the key informant
 interviews. Several relate to MPDSR application and to improving the collection and use of data
 to inform responses to decrease maternal and perinatal deaths from preventable causes.
 • Urge law makers to pass the federal MPDSR bill that has been drafted by MoNHSR&C and is
 being pursued with the Ministry of Law. This bill is necessary to mandate maternal and
 perinatal death notification while maintaining both legal protection (indemnity) for health
 workers and confidentiality concerning deaths.

108
````

### Trang PDF 129

````text
9. Case study: Pakistan


• Establish a national MPDSR committee to coordinate and organize training of trainers,
 oversee provinces, disseminate guidelines, monitor and evaluate MPDSR implementation, and
 provide overarching direction and guidance to increase the coverage and functionality of
 MPDSR implementation.
• Develop and implement a plan to monitor and evaluate MPDSR implementation using clearly
 defined indicators
• Scale up MPDSR for all facilities, starting with the public sector, and increase prioritization of
 perinatal death reporting to the same level as that for maternal death reporting.
• Hold monthly review committee meetings to review data from facilities and districts and to
 discuss appropriate responses to issues identified and how to implement them. Track, record
 and report the frequency of these meetings to create accountability and measure progress.
• Enable facility-based midwives to report maternal and perinatal deaths in the MPDSR
 application to reduce the burden on other staff.
• Engage and train community frontline workers, such as LHWs and community midwives, to
 use the MPDSR application to increase reporting of deaths in the community.
• Promote national awareness campaigns on the preventability of most maternal and perinatal
 deaths, and the importance of implementing MPDSR to save lives in future.
• Include MPDSR in training curricula at facilities and for new staff, as well as in academic
 settings for students (both undergraduate and graduate).
• Conduct regular refresher workshops at the facility level. These present an opportunity to
 inform users about modifications or updates to the MPDSR application.
• Reinforce messaging around the “no name, no blame” policy to ensure wide understanding
 that MPDSR is not a punitive system. Publicize the legal framework (currently in draft form)
 that will provide legal protections to guarantee this.
• Use existing data information systems (DHIS/DHIS2) and merge these with MPDSR to
 produce a single system for reporting maternal and perinatal deaths in the community and at
 primary, secondary and tertiary health facilities to avoid confusion and reduce demand on
 health workers.
• Encourage good working relationships between all levels of the health system involved in
 MPDSR implementation (from national to community levels) to manage this system
 effectively. Reporting mechanisms should be established with a clear flow of information
 between levels. A monthly or quarterly bulletin showing the number of facilities in each
 district/province that reported maternal and perinatal deaths would increase the visibility of
 MPDSR and create accountability.
• Engage diverse actors and associations involved in maternal and perinatal health to support
 the operationalization and dissemination of MPDSR guidelines and expand the use of the
 mobile application.
• Establish information technology (IT) teams at provincial and regional levels that can support
 mobile application users in resolving technical issues and ensure timely troubleshooting.
````

### Trang PDF 130

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 9.6 Conclusions
 Although Pakistan’s MMR and NMR have improved gradually over the years, considerable work is
 still needed to achieve SDG targets 3.1 and 3.2. The implementation of MPDSR is increasing the
 prioritization of maternal and perinatal death reporting and review, with the goal of using data
 generated systematically through this system to inform responses and policy-making to reduce
 future mortality. MPDSR has been implemented in selected tertiary and secondary hospitals in
 four provinces and Islamabad. To date, the focus has been mostly on reporting and reviewing
 maternal deaths, but the perinatal component was recently added. Key informant interviews were
 conducted to highlight successes and challenges in MPDSR implementation in Pakistan. A key
 finding was that the use of the MPDSR mobile application was an innovative approach to
 reporting deaths, with real-time data being visible in a dashboard. Although promising, the
 application poses challenges to users that need to be addressed to improve its functionality.
 Recommendations address these and other challenges to MPDSR, including limited capacity of
 health workers to report deaths in the mobile application, fear of blame and lack of coordinated
 support from all levels of the health system.


110
````

### Trang PDF 134

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 10.1 Background
 Sri Lanka is a lower-middle-income country in South Asia with a population of 22 million. Its MCH
 services cater for 3.9 million eligible families and an annual cohort of 342 000 pregnant women (1).
 With universal antenatal care and facility births, the country has relatively low levels of maternal (29.2
 per 100 000 live births), infant (8.2 per 1000 live births) and perinatal mortality (6.6 per 1000 births) (1).

 Sri Lanka has unified systems to review maternal and perinatal deaths through facility-based
 reviews and community-based verbal autopsies. A key element of the maternal and perinatal
 death surveillance cycle is “response”, which involves the formulation and implementation of
 targeted recommendations at local and national levels to prevent future deaths. This chapter
 presents the findings of a case study undertaken to document Sri Lanka’s experiences with
 reporting maternal and perinatal deaths and implementing maternal and perinatal death reviews,
 particularly the responses that have led to significant reductions in maternal and perinatal deaths.
 10.2 Methodology
 A mixed-methods approach was used, including a literature review and key informant interviews.
 Available documents relating to MPDSR implementation in Sri Lanka in general, and to the country
 case study focus specifically, were reviewed. Twenty-one key stakeholders were selected for
 interviews in consultation with the national Ministry of Health and ranged from divisional health
 managers to directors-general of health services and past presidents of professional colleges. All
 interviews were conducted in English by the national consultant, mostly by tele- or videoconference.
 10.3 Counting maternal deaths: historical aspects and
 current status
 Maternal deaths have been counted in Sri Lanka since 1881. Under British colonial rule, the voluntary
 CRVS system started in 1887 became a legislative requirement in 1897. Maternal morbidity and
 mortality data and key health service data were available from reports of the registrar general,
 the director of health services, the principal civil medical officer and the inspector-general of
 hospitals from 1901 (2) and from Ministry of Health annual health bulletins since 1980.

 Since the 1990s, the country has had three systems for reporting maternal deaths. First, maternal
 deaths in hospitals are recorded on a monthly maternity statistics form, which all government
 hospitals send to the Medical Statistics Unit of Ministry of Health. Second, public health midwives,
 assigned to each household in the country (3) collect essential data using the Reproductive
 Health Management Information System implemented by the Family Health Bureau (FHB) in 1986.
 Each midwife submits a monthly statement to their divisional health manager, including the
 number of maternal deaths and the number for which a local death investigation was conducted
 by the field health-care team. Divisional health managers send consolidated quarterly MCH
 reports to FHB. The third system registers deaths through the CRVS: a network of around 1000 lay
 and medical registrars throughout the country send copies of death certificates monthly to the
 Registrar General’s Office in Colombo. Although all three mechanisms are still functioning, since
 1996, the FHB system has been recognized as the official source of maternal mortality statistics.
 10.4 Counting perinatal deaths: historical aspects and
 current status
 Registration of stillbirths has been legally required since 1885. Legislation in 1954 restricted the
 disposal of stillbirths without registration (4). Older weekly health reports included data on stillbirths
 in urban areas (5) and data on infant deaths. Since 1986, the Reproductive Health Management
114
````

### Trang PDF 135

````text
10. Case study: Sri Lanka


Information System has published the numbers of stillbirths and infant deaths reported by primary
health centres and public health midwives annually. Hospital-level systematic counting of perinatal
deaths, begun in two Colombo hospitals, expanded by 2006 to include all specialized hospitals.
However, there was no mechanism to share perinatal data with FHB until 2014, when FHB started
collecting paper-based individual perinatal death data nationwide. In 2016, national perinatal mortality
surveillance was further improved with the adoption of the WHO perinatal mortality classification.

Perinatal data collection through a web-based data portal on the DHIS2 platform started in 2019,
and a population-based stillbirths register has been implemented nationally since 2022 (6).

Stillbirth data for Sri Lanka are now available from four sources: hospitals, perinatal mortality
surveillance and review (PDSR), CRVS and the community.
10.5 Reviewing maternal deaths: historical aspects and
current status
Since the early 1950s, public health midwives have investigated maternal deaths informally to
identify the factors leading to the death and reported their findings to their area medical officer of
health, a public health doctor and the divisional health-service manager.

In 1959, a pilot project on maternal and neonatal death review was initiated in two divisions by
the director of health services. A maternal death investigation committee was established and
supported by the Department of Health (7). While the committee conducted frequent review
meetings (8), maternal death investigations were ad hoc.

Sri Lanka’s national review meetings to monitor its immunization programme from the mid-1980s
facilitated interactions between field health-care staff and central Ministry of Health staff. In
response to perceptions of low prioritization of women’s survival in comparison with child
survival, maternal death reviews were included in these meetings. Community members and
hospital staff were invited to the meetings, organized by FHB and chaired by the secretary,
Ministry of Health. The Sri Lanka College of Obstetricians and Gynaecologists (SLCOG) and
University of Colombo academics were also invited.

Reported maternal deaths were reviewed and discussed in detail annually at national-level maternal
death reviews conducted by Ministry of Health and FHB in collaboration with SLCOG and the
College of Anaesthesiologists. At these “individual case reviews”, public health and hospital staff
presented details of each maternal death. Cases were analysed to establish the cause of death
and to identify key contributory factors according to the three-delay model. Corrective actions
were suggested; some issues were immediately addressed at the meeting.

 This was also the beginning of a new partnership between obstetricians and public
 health officials working together to reduce maternal deaths in the country. (Former
 National Programme Officer, UNICEF)

In 1986, maternal deaths were made notifiable. FHB also explored the possibility of requiring the
village-level government agent (Grama Niladhari) to notify maternal deaths to ensure completeness.

Guidance on procedures to be followed when investigating maternal deaths was issued by
Ministry of Health in 1991 (9). Notification was to be made by all government and private
hospitals and field-care health teams. Notified deaths in hospital were to be investigated within
three days and those in the field within seven days. Separate structured investigation forms for
hospital and field deaths were produced. The head of the hospital was responsible for organizing
facility-level reviews and sending completed forms to FHB. In the community, the medical officer
 115
````

### Trang PDF 136

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 of health was expected to collect information through verbal autopsy at the deceased woman’s
 home. The regional director of health services was instructed to conduct maternal death review
 meetings every three months at the district level with the participation of hospital heads, medical
 officers of health and district-level administrators. A quarterly report on maternal deaths by the
 regional director of health services was sent to the provincial director of health services, director
 of FHB and director-general of health services (DGHS).
 The importance of conducting death reviews was reflected in an Ministry of Health circular: “A
 head of an institution who does not investigate a maternal death within three days of the death
 will be called to give explanation why the investigation was not done. Ministry of Health will take
 a serious note of lapses on the part of the head of an institution in this regard” (9).
 In 2001, the maternal death investigation procedures in Sinhala and Tamil were activated in all
 provinces. Forms for presenting maternal deaths in hospitals and in field settings were developed
 in consultation with SLCOG, academics, administrators and district-level medical officers.
 Preventability of deaths, unmet need for family planning, and delays in seeking, reaching and
 obtaining care were objectively assessed. Issues identified and recommendations made at
 annual district-level maternal mortality reviews were recorded. Progress in implementing
 recommendations from previous review meetings was also reported.
 In 2009, the first national programme manager (NPM) was appointed for MCH morbidity and
 mortality surveillance. A comprehensive evaluation of the existing MDSR mechanism at each
 level was undertaken. Triangulation of maternal death notifications from various sources showed
 disparities and under-reporting. Many maternal death investigation forms were incomplete,
 autopsy rates were low, and there were issues with conducting timely maternal mortality reviews
 and participation by relevant stakeholders.
 From 2010, central-level monitoring was tightened, with every report scrutinized and data gaps
 addressed. The national maternal death database was updated, and guidelines on maternal
 death investigation (10) were included in training for field health workers (11).
 In the early 2010s, in response to concerns about confidentiality, some non-physician categories
 of health workers were excluded from case discussions in district national maternal mortality
 reviews. The then director of FHB reported, “It was a hard decision. But we had to heed to the
 growing pressure from the hospital sector”.
 In 2011, discussions between the ministries of Justice and Health, the College of Forensic
 Pathologists of Sri Lanka and SLCOG led to Ministry of Health (12-17) mandating autopsies on all
 probable maternal deaths by a consultant forensic pathologist. Autopsy guidelines were issued
 in 2017 (18).
 Copies of all hospital clinical records and documents from the field were compiled by FHB for
 each maternal death. Participation by all care providers involved in the care of the deceased and
 all relevant stakeholders was made mandatory by DGHS. However, as it was practically
 impossible for all categories of specialists to participate in district-level meetings, in 2012 the
 NPM decided to obtain expert opinion on selected cases through desk reviews.
 A special clause was added in 2015 to the death declaration form completed by a medical officer:
 “A death of a female due to any cause either during pregnancy or 3 months after the termination
 of pregnancy is considered as a probable maternal death. ln such instances please request an
 inquest and autopsy and do not fill this form” (19). Field health workers were instructed to send a
 copy of the death certificate to FHB, initiating the process of sharing details of confirmed
 maternal deaths with CRVS.
 Between 2009 and 2020, MDSR achieved 99% capture of maternal deaths from hospitals and
 communities, reviewed 100% of reported maternal deaths (n = 1411), conducted national
116
````

### Trang PDF 137

````text
10. Case study: Sri Lanka


maternal mortality reviews in all districts every year, compiled minutes (100%) and disseminated
recommendations for action (100%). Coverage of autopsies on maternal deaths remained at
more than 95% over the period 2013–2020.
````

### Trang PDF 138

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 Governance: The MDSR system clearly specifies governance mechanisms that involve
 government and private hospitals, the community health system, and district and provincial
 health administration. Strict implementation of the MDSR guidelines is ensured by FHB.
 Notification: All health workers providing care to women are legally bound to notify all deaths
 among women of reproductive age within 24 hours to FHB. This broader criterion ensures that all
 probable maternal deaths are captured by the surveillance system.
 Postmortem examinations: Almost all specialized hospitals (where 95% of births in the country
 occur) have consultant forensic pathologists. Since guidelines were issued in 2017, some 99% of
 probable maternal deaths have been subject to an autopsy.
 Verbal autopsy (community level): Field health workers led by the area medical officer of health
 visit the household of the deceased within 14 days. A structured field investigation reporting form
 facilitates the identification of gaps in service delivery, contributory personal, family and
 community factors, and determination of cause of death.
 Facility-based maternal death reviews: Reviews of maternal death in health facilities are conducted
 within 14 days of death. Participants include all health workers involved in the management of the
 deceased woman, the forensic pathologist, and field health workers from the mother’s residential
 area. From 2021, representatives of FHB and SLCOG also attend these review meetings to
 strengthen the review process. Details of the review are documented and sent to FHB.
 Triangulation of reported deaths and data quality assurance: Maternal death data from different
 sources, including media reports of maternal deaths, are triangulated and verified with the MDSR
 data. When investigation forms are incomplete, FHB seeks missing data from data originators.
 Central-level data compilation: Copies of all clinical records, investigation reports, autopsy
 reports and field documents (eligible family register, family planning records, pregnancy records,
 diagnosis cards) are sent to FHB, together with a script of the inquest proceedings by the
 coroner. A docket with all the relevant records is maintained for each notified probable maternal
 death. Selected variables are entered in the National Maternal Death Registry.
 At FHB level, cases are initially reviewed by NPM and categorized as “clearly maternal”, “clearly
 non-maternal” and “probably maternal”. With technical inputs from a SLCOG representative, cases
 are selected for review and discussion at each maternal mortality review at the district level.
 In cases selected for further review, a medical officer compiles a structured case scenario in a
 time sequence, including information on the social background, medical history, and care
 received from the time of registration in the eligible family register to the death.
 Immediate response to maternal deaths: Since January 2018, Ministry of Health has implemented
 an immediate response to maternal deaths through face-to-face meetings chaired by DGHS with
 technical experts and hospital and field health workers directly involved in the care pathway of the
 deceased woman. Cases with significant issues warranting immediate response are selected by NPM
 for review. Participation at such technical review meetings is mandatory. No disciplinary action can
 be based on discussions at these meetings, but they facilitate effective and timely decision-making
 by the highest level of Ministry of Health to address specific service deficiencies. Proceedings and
 recommendations are documented by NPM, and progress on implementation is reported to DGHS.
 Central-level desk review: A desk review is conducted of all selected cases of a district, with the
 participation of representatives of SLCOG, public health specialists, anaesthetists, forensic
 pathologists, health administrators and other specialists, one to two weeks prior to the
 scheduled national maternal mortality review. Compiled case scenarios are presented to the
 experts by NPM. Data gaps, hospital and field-level deficiencies, issues in care provision, and
 suggested actions are identified at desk review meetings.
118
````

### Trang PDF 139

````text
10. Case study: Sri Lanka


District-level case review: Some districts conduct their own local review meeting with the
participation of health administrators, hospital clinical teams and field health workers. In
addition, FHB coordinates annual district-level meetings – national maternal mortality reviews –
with participation of national experts to review all maternal deaths reported from the district in
the previous year.

The regional director of health services organizes national maternal mortality reviews, which are
chaired by the provincial director of health services.

As the first step, the medical officer of MCH reviews the implementation of recommendations
from the preceding maternal mortality reviews. NPM describes the actions taken at the
national level. Challenges facing the execution of the recommendations are discussed and
solutions identified.

Anonymized copies of cases are given to the expert panel. A confidentiality-assured, non-threatening
environment and a no-name, no-blame approach are ensured without compromising accountability.
A modified clinical audit process is conducted where quality of care provided is reviewed against
standard protocols at the local level, with due consideration to available facilities and contexts.
Cases are reviewed using the three-delay model, for unmet need for family planning and the
preventability of death. Supported by autopsy findings and the clinical scenario, the cause of death
is determined by consensus among the experts. Issues are identified and recommendations for
action are formulated wherever applicable. A responsible official or agency is identified to
execute the recommendation. Minutes of the national maternal mortality review for each district
are sent to all relevant officials and agencies by FHB.

Dissemination: After completing national maternal mortality reviews for each year in all districts,
the NPM analyses confirmed maternal deaths, computes national and district MMRs, and compiles
and issues a comprehensive annual report. FHB organizes annual national maternal death
dissemination seminars, chaired by DGHS and attended by all national- and subnational-level
stakeholders. The outcomes of the maternal death reviews are presented, and issues raised are
discussed. Policy-level decisions are made to further improve maternal care service delivery and
prevent maternal deaths. The findings of maternal death reviews are published annually by FHB
and widely shared.

10.6 Reviewing perinatal deaths: historical aspects and
current status

Facility-based PDSR
A systematic hospital-based perinatal death review process was formulated in 2006 by FHB,
SLCOG, the Sri Lanka College of Paediatricians and the Perinatal Society of Sri Lanka. Under the
National Perinatal Mortality Surveillance System, all specialized hospitals are required to
document all stillbirths and early neonatal deaths, review them monthly and report them to FHB.
Individual perinatal death data were collected in a structured form using the Wigglesworth
pathophysiological classification. Data were compiled at the hospital level and utilized at
monthly perinatal death review meetings. Only information on the numbers of deaths and the
outcomes of meetings was sent to the central level.

However, there were issues with coverage, regularity, participation, timeliness, availability of
reports and implementation of lessons learned. Between 2010 and 2013, hospital reporting was
streamlined with the introduction of a new user-friendly form. Each obstetric or neonatal unit
completes an individual form for every perinatal death. A tracking system ensures timely receipt

 119
````

### Trang PDF 140

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 and completeness of monthly reports. Reported perinatal deaths were analysed, and action
 points identified and discussed at national-level technical advisory committees to translate
 lessons learned into actions or policies. By 2014, full 100% coverage was achieved in conducting
 perinatal death audits at specialized hospitals.

 In 2022, Sri Lanka initiated a comprehensive fetal and under-five child death surveillance system,
 covering deaths in both communities and hospitals (Fig. 10.2). Under this system, all hospitals
 should notify all fetal deaths (> 22 weeks of gestation) and under-five child deaths within
 24 hours, investigate them within 14 days and send completed death investigation forms to FHB.
 A judicial or pathological postmortem should be conducted in all fetal and under-five child deaths
 when the cause of death cannot be accurately determined. A Certificate of Stillbirth, confirming
 the death of each fetus over 28 weeks of gestation, should be completed by a medical officer and
 copied to the registrar general (CRVS). All relevant variables of each Hospital Fetal Death Case
 Abstraction Form should be entered into the web-based National Hospital Fetal Death Register.
 A fact-finding ward/unit-level death review should be performed by the consultant in charge of
 the unit where the death occurred within 14 days.

 Hospital perinatal mortality review meetings are conducted by every specialized institution
 (including the private sector) providing perinatal care, held monthly on a fixed date and chaired
 by the head of the institution. These meetings are attended by hospital teams, including
 specialists, the medical officer of MCH and field health workers. Individual cases are discussed
 based on the three-delay model, and recommendations made to prevent similar deaths in future.
 Proceedings of the monthly meetings and number of perinatal deaths are sent to FHB. A zero
 report should be sent if there are no perinatal deaths.

 Community-based PDSR
 From the inception of the Reproductive Health Management Information System, public health
 midwives have conducted field stillbirth investigations, supported by a supervising public health
 midwife and/or public health nursing sister. Specified data are sent to the area medical officer of
 health. These deaths, and any issues in field health services, are discussed at the monthly
 conference of the divisional health office.

 With the introduction of the national fetal and under-five child death surveillance system in 2022,
 a web-based national stillbirth register was also initiated.
````
