### Trang PDF 142

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 MPDSR review atmosphere
 MDSR and PDSR evolved separately to enable confidentiality-assured and non-punitive systems.
 Both systems foster a supportive and non-threatening environment for health workers to
 participate in MPDSR reviews at all levels, maintaining a “no name, no blame” culture. The focus
 is on what went wrong, leading to a systems-thinking perspective, rather than on individual
 deficiencies. Details of MPDSR cases are kept confidential and anonymized. Care providers are
 not named during meetings, allowing more open discussion about gaps in case management.
 Although there is no established framework of legal indemnity for MPDSR, there has been no
 litigation relating to MPDSR. FHB, supported by SLCOG, has lobbied the Ministry of Justice for
 legal immunity. The Sri Lanka Medical Council recently confirmed that MPDSR-related
 documents cannot be used in disciplinary processes. The Ministry of Health system of
 disciplinary accountability mechanisms for negligence has been kept separate from MPDSR
 activities from its inception. However, the absence of immunity may have prevented some
 community representatives from participating in MPDSR.

 10.7 Responding to maternal and perinatal deaths
 Sri Lanka has effectively used maternal and perinatal mortality data to formulate and implement
 targeted recommendations: the ultimate objective of the surveillance cycle and the focus of this
 case study.

 With the initiation of regular maternal death reviews at the national level in the late 1980s, the
 secretary of health and their team visited areas with high maternal deaths to study the causes
 and take preventive actions on-site. For example, the rural area of Morawaka had a high level of
 maternal deaths relating to staff shortages. Extra health workers appointed after the team’s visit
 resulted in a reduction of maternal deaths.

 This section describes how Sri Lanka translates lessons learned from maternal deaths into
 changes in practice, programmes and policies at all levels: community, hospital (government and
 private sector), district (health region), provincial and national (MoH and professional colleges).

 Community level
 Every divisional health office holds a monthly progress review meeting with all grass-roots
 public health workers and heads of hospitals in the division. Findings of field maternal death
 investigations are discussed, and corrective actions formulated. The medical officer of health is
 responsible for the immediate implementation of corrective actions at community level. All
 actions taken are reported on the field maternal death investigation form and shared with FHB.

 Stillbirths and infant deaths are reported to the medical officer of health by public health
 midwives; issues arising are also discussed at the monthly conference. Solutions are suggested
 for any service deficiencies identified. In addition, relevant service gaps are raised at monthly
 perinatal mortality review meetings at the hospital.

 Hospital level
 At facility maternal death reviews, recommendations are made by participants. Issues at the home
 and field levels are also reviewed and actions suggested. Awareness about pregnancy-related
 matters among pregnant mothers, family members and the public are also addressed. The head
 of the institution is responsible for the immediate implementation of any corrective actions within
 the hospital. Medical officers attend to strategies for implementation at community and district
 levels. FHB and SLCOG representatives take up issues and suggest actions to the central level.

122
````

### Trang PDF 143

````text
10. Case study: Sri Lanka


Private sector hospitals follow the same course of action. For example, when several pregnant
women were found to have died of sepsis, dengue and pneumonia due to late admission to
hospitals, a directive was sent to all field health workers and first contact physicians to facilitate
the immediate admission of pregnant or postpartum women with fever for inpatient care,
irrespective of the cause.

Perinatal mortality review meetings are held routinely in all hospitals providing specialized
perinatal care. Individual case reviews followed by open discussion often result in changes in
practice in both hospital and field settings. A designated medical officer documents the
proceedings and actions. The minutes are transferred to local authorities and the central level in
a standard form. FHB monitors individual death data entry and receipt of reports of monthly
perinatal review meetings.

District and provincial levels
Regional health authorities conduct quarterly reviews of MCH and annual district maternal
mortality reviews. Each divisional team is required to present maternal, stillbirth, infant and child
mortality data in addition to other MCH statistics. Issues raised at hospital perinatal death
reviews, and service gaps identified in field and hospital maternal death reviews, are further
discussed, and remedial actions are formulated at the MCH review. The regional director of
health services and the medical officer of MCH, with inputs from public health teams, facilitate
actions to improve maternal care service delivery to further reduce maternal and perinatal deaths
in the district. Usually, a district maternal mortality review is conducted as a preparatory meeting
for the national maternal mortality review. Maternal deaths reported in the year are reviewed and
preventive actions appropriate for local conditions are planned and executed after the meeting.

FHB visits each district annually to review maternal deaths with national experts at the national
maternal mortality review. These visits are an important aspect of MDSR, providing a forum to
discuss and learn lessons at the district level. Hospital clinicians prioritize national maternal
mortality reviews as a platform to raise issues and hear recommendations from district, provincial
and national authorities. The substantial time allocated for open discussion in a non-threatening
environment following each maternal death case gives an opportunity to identify service
deficiencies and formulate preventive strategies. Local practicalities are taken into consideration,
allowing for regional disparities in resources, cultural practices and attitudes of health workers.

At national maternal mortality reviews, actions to address local service delivery problems can be
initiated immediately after the review. Matters requiring higher-level involvement are referred to
the central level by representatives from Ministry of Health, FHB and SLCOG. Actions are decided
by consensus, and a responsible official or agency is identified, with a time limit for action where
feasible. The minutes of proceedings issued by FHB include anonymized case vignettes, and act
as strong arguments for changing practices, programmes and policies. Provincial and regional
directors of health and medical officers of MCH act on the recommendations to strengthen
maternal health-service delivery in the districts.

MoH

FHB level
As the central agency for implementing the MCH programme, FHB plays a pivotal role in the
response element of MPDSR. On receiving notification of a maternal death, if the NPM perceives
that immediate action is needed, steps are taken to address issues arising from the death.
Technical teams led by the FHB director may visit hospitals if necessary.

 123
````

### Trang PDF 144

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 The monthly review by the FHB’s consultant community physicians is another platform for NPM
 to present MPDSR findings. Here issues can be shared between those overseeing antenatal,
 intrapartum, postnatal and newborn care, family planning, gender and women’s health. Collective
 contributions are made to formulate recommendations specific to programme areas. Policy
 decisions on identified solutions are also facilitated. For example, increasing maternal suicides
 and related investigations revealed the need for multiple screening points in the antenatal period
 in addition to current screening during the postnatal period. This led to the establishment of an
 expert group to develop a screening process to be incorporated into antenatal care.

 Technical advisory committees
 Two national technical advisory committees, the Maternal Care and Family Planning Committee
 and the Newborn and Child Health Committee, oversee policy-level decision-making at Ministry
 of Health. They are chaired by the deputy director-general of the Public Health Service, and MCH
 experts serve as members. They meet every other month and review MPDSR outcomes. Issues
 highlighted in monthly reports from hospital perinatal mortality review meetings that warrant
 high-level decisions are discussed at these technical advisory committees. The highest forum
 for MPDSR action is the National Committee on Family Health, chaired by the secretary of
 health, which meets every four to six months. Matters originating at technical advisory
 committees and other policy decisions requiring high-level ministry concurrence (e.g. appointing
 two obstetricians to all emergency obstetric care facilities, upgrading all blood bank services to
 operate 24/7) are brought to the National Committee on Family Health.

 Administrative progress review forums
 Health Development Committee meetings are convened by DGHS and address policy
 implementation, strategic guidance and feedback from implementing stakeholders within the
 government health sector. Hospital directors convene monthly meetings to monitor hospitals’
 performance and activities, chaired by DGHS. The FHB director or NPM presents the MPDSR
 findings. Generic and specific directives are issued to all hospitals on areas requiring
 improvement in maternal and perinatal care.

 Professional colleges
 Many professional organizations help to drive MPDSR. These include SLCOG, Sri Lanka College of
 Paediatricians, College of Anaesthesiologists and Intensivists, College of Forensic Pathologists of
 Sri Lanka, Perinatal Society of Sri Lanka, College of Community Physicians of Sri Lanka, Ceylon
 College of Physicians, Sri Lanka College of Cardiologists and Sri Lanka College of Pulmonologists.

 SLCOG has been a key stakeholder from MPDSR’s inception and is indispensable in implementing
 and sustaining this process. The establishment of a Maternal Mortality Committee at SLCOG
 showcased the leadership and ownership assumed by obstetricians in the MDSR process. The
 SLCOG president participates in all national maternal mortality reviews. SLCOG members serve
 as reviewers on an honorary basis. SLCOG, Sri Lanka College of Paediatricians and Perinatal
 Society of Sri Lanka are represented on the technical advisory committees and the National
 Committee on Family Health, and contribute to policy-level decision-making. They are also
 involved in executing recommendations by developing national clinical guidelines and circulars,
 leading training, and participating in facility visits. SLCOG also played a lead role in initiating a
 maternal near-miss surveillance system and by lobbying for transforming the system into a
 national confidential enquiry into maternal deaths programme.

 The Perinatal Society of Sri Lanka laid the groundwork for a national perinatal death surveillance
 system from the beginning of 2001.

124
````

### Trang PDF 145

````text
10. Case study: Sri Lanka

10.8 Key actions in response to findings of death reviews
Many key actions in response to MPDSR findings are executed following instructions issued in
Ministry of Health circulars. Examples from the initial phases of MDSR include circulars making
the officer in charge of a hospital responsible for conducting MCH clinics, appointing medical
officers to conduct clinics, and mandating obstetricians to personally examine all cases referred
from the periphery (1988). A red sticker for pregnancy appointment cards was introduced to
identify high-risk antenatal cases in 1993.

Other actions in response to death reviews include:
 • mandatory examination of pregnant women by a medical officer;
 • steps to prevent deaths due to heart disease, to improve care for women with hypertensive
 disorders and to strengthen postpartum care;
 • provision of 2500 mopeds for public health midwives to facilitate MCH domiciliary care,
 funded by the World Bank and UNICEF in 2006 (22).

With the reduction in MMR and the total number of maternal deaths, actions have shifted over the
past decades from broader service delivery to more focused strategies to manage specific conditions,
leading to better survival rates among women with these conditions. Examples are described below.

Obstetric haemorrhage
MMR relating to obstetric haemorrhage decreased from 11.4 deaths per 100 000 live births in
2001 to 2.5 per 100 000 in 2019. During this period, policy and programmatic decisions led to the
expansion of blood transfusion services, increased availability of specific blood products,
appointments of transfusion specialists to major hospitals, greater availability of Bakri balloon
catheters, strengthening of referral pathways for the management of morbidly adherent placenta,
use of the rotational thromboelastography-guided bleeding management algorithm, and introduction
of blood transfusion protocols, resulting in fewer deaths following obstetric haemorrhage.

Septic abortions
FHB and SLCOG developed guidelines in 2015 on comprehensive high-quality post-abortion
care services (23) in response to the significant contribution (10–12%) of unsafe abortions to
maternal deaths.

Improving management of maternal morbidities
National guidelines were developed for the management of dengue haemorrhagic fever and
pneumonia during pregnancy. Specific instructions were issued on fast-track referral of pregnant
women with suspected heart disease, priority care for them at hospital clinics, and field-level follow-up.

Register of Eligible Women in Danger (RED book)
Many women with high-risk health conditions or complex social scenarios suffer death
associated with childbirth. Women in socially vulnerable circumstances have been viewed by
some health workers as “difficult customers”. Many pregnancies were socially stigmatized and
not readily visible or not known to the medical officer of health. Public health midwives often
failed to provide these women with optimal care. The RED book, kept at the divisional health
office, allows public health midwives to record details of “difficult customers” to facilitate
close follow-up by medical officers of health, who are instructed to manage each woman in
the RED book by visiting her home and providing targeted care.

 125
````

### Trang PDF 146

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 Responding to maternal suicides
 In response to the growing number of suicides, with support from the Sri Lanka College of
 Psychiatrists, a psychological autopsy tool for maternal suicides was introduced in 2016 to
 improve understanding of maternal suicides. A psychiatrist visits the household with the field
 health-care team and conducts a verbal autopsy. Sri Lanka may be the only country in the world
 to study maternal suicides so methodologically. FHB has recorded case scenarios and reviewed
 465 maternal suicides to date with psychiatrists, obstetricians and community health teams.
 FHB analysed collated data, disseminated findings and helped to improve mental health in
 women of reproductive age.

 The response mechanism was further refined towards a case-by-case approach. This led to the
 practice of responding immediately to more complex cases and those necessitating high-level
 administrative involvement.

 Immediate response to maternal deaths
 Helped by technical managers and experts, the strategy of responding immediately to maternal
 deaths has been highly effective. Many advantages were gained, such as: strengthening
 multidisciplinary team meetings for critically ill women by mandating the physical presence of all
 specialists; establishing a working group to administer influenza vaccine to pregnant mothers;
 making oseltamivir available to pregnant women who refuse inpatient care; standardizing
 national procedure for obstetric referrals between consultants; strengthening RED book strategy
 for high-risk women; expediting the revision of postpartum haemorrhage guidelines; developing
 new guidelines on family planning methods; appointing cardiologists to specialized women’s
 hospitals; and distributing infusion pumps where needed. It is estimated that the strategy of
 immediate response to maternal deaths has contributed to a 30% reduction of total number of
 maternal deaths (from 128 to 89) between 2017 and 2020.

 The response mechanism has been greatly streamlined since 2010. Minutes of national maternal
 mortality reviews for all districts for all the years with case vignettes, issues, recommendations
 and responsible officials/agencies are available to 2019. Table 12.1 shows the recommendations
 and the status of their implementation for the years 2014–2018.
````

### Trang PDF 147

````text
10. Case study: Sri Lanka


Between 2001 and 2020, MPDSR helped reduce maternal deaths by 46% (from 167 to 90) and the
national MMR by 37% (46.6 to 29.2 per 100 000 live births). MPDSR efforts from 2009 to 2019
also facilitated Sri Lanka’s gradual reduction of cause-specific MMRs (per 100 000 live births)
for obstetric haemorrhage (from 6.9 to 2.5), abortion (from 5.3 to 1.6) and hypertensive disorders
in pregnancy (from 6.1 to 1.9).

Sri Lanka also reported over 90% capture of perinatal deaths in both hospital and the
community for the six years 2015–2020. Surveillance of perinatal deaths resulted in action at
community, facility, regional and national levels, helped by the availability of several guidelines,
including clinical guidelines to introduce quality monitoring systems for intrapartum care.
This work contributed to the reduction of perinatal deaths and the improvement of care during
and around birth.

10.9 Factors contributing to success
Sri Lanka has been successful in implementing MDSR over the last seven decades. A data-driven
culture, established by colonial rule from the late nineteenth century, set the precedent for
systematically collecting accurate data on live births and counting maternal deaths through
registration. Such data collection is now complemented by reliable health information from both
households and hospitals.

The MPDSR system is administered by central Government with engagement by the highest-level
health officials. Strong Government commitment and involvement are reflected in the ongoing
refinement of the process and the provision of administrative and financial support for MPDSR.

Strong governance, supported by circulars, guidelines and regulations, instilled commitment
among health workers and managers. Central-level close monitoring sustained over the years
imposed accountability for the execution of recommended actions. Making maternal death
notification mandatory, enforcing that requirement and obliging professionals to participate in
MPDSR are examples of best practice.

MDSR was initially a public health activity that capitalized a strong orientation towards prevention.
CHWs, who were much engaged in the review culture, were largely receptive to MDSR. The
commitment of successive NPMs and directors of the FHB and Ministry of Health played a
crucial role in sustaining and improving the system. Programme managers initially exploited the
emotional triggers of health workers and managers to act on maternal and perinatal deaths, and
gradually embarked on evidence-based approaches.

The positive attitudes of professional bodies and committed health staff were advantageous.
Participation by health workers from different fields as experts in the review process emphasized
the need to improve quality by reflecting on practice. The goodwill and enforcement of MPDSR
policies by national professional bodies supported the execution of each element of the MPDSR
cycle. Their active involvement not only showed their commitment to the process but also
encouraged practical implementation of the findings.

Timely ongoing restructuring of the process, with the addition of innovative improvements, has
allowed the system to evolve as a model for other countries.

Even without legislation to prevent claims for wrongdoing arising from MPDSR, Sri Lanka has
maintained a no-blame ethos throughout the MPDSR process. This has allowed free discussion,
leading to effective changes in practice.

Other factors contributing to success include: obtaining accurate data on the circumstances and
treatment of women at various levels of health care; systematic processing of information;

 127
````

### Trang PDF 148

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 careful in-depth reviews at multiple levels; and appropriate identification and dissemination of
 recommendations for remedial action at all levels of the health system.

 Sri Lanka’s MPDSR system has multiple platforms and mechanisms to support administrators,
 health facilities and health workers in implementing responses to improve the quality of care
 throughout the health system, and has yielded significant benefits.

 10.10 Challenges
 Working with numerous specialists from different fields and with different expertise towards a
 common target – the prevention of maternal and perinatal deaths – is not easy.

 From the outset, some clinicians urged for a fully confidential system of enquiry into maternal
 deaths. Maintaining confidentiality at each level of review is difficult. As district-level reviews are
 relatively large gatherings, those involved in care of the deceased may be made uncomfortable
 by large audiences of health workers.

 Any remarks of a fault-finding nature can prevent smooth process. In cases of obviously
 substandard care or unprofessional behaviour, reviewers can find it difficult to criticize colleagues.

 10.11 Conclusions
 Many of Sri Lanka’s successes and challenges in developing its MPDSR systems would be of
 interest to other lower-middle-income countries. The country implements separate MDSR and
 PDSR processes.

 MPDSR activities were facilitated by strong leadership from central Government and support
 from regional and district managers and professional bodies. The timely reshaping of the MDSR
 and PDSR processes, in response to health-system demands, has improved the quality of the
 review process. The PDSR runs optimally at facility level and has not achieved the full-scale
 national adoption that the MDSR has.

 MPDSR has produced many changes in maternal and perinatal care provision, such as the focus
 on quality of care, increased accountability and policy changes, and has reduced perinatal and
 maternal deaths. Allowing responses to be made and considered at different strategic levels has
 produced meaningful results. While responses previously focused on broad areas of the health
 system, in recent years they have focused more on specific health conditions. Systematic
 tracking of the implementation of recommended actions shows that most recommendations
 have been accomplished. However, to be able to use MPDSR findings more effectively, a renewed
 strategy with more timely and closer monitoring is needed.

 Sri Lanka has achieved remarkable success in reducing maternal and perinatal deaths. While
 MPDSR may not have been the sole catalyst for this achievement, it nevertheless decisively
 influenced the entire spectrum of players in maternal and perinatal care to engage in all possible
 strategies to improve the quality of maternal and perinatal care.


128
````

### Trang PDF 152

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 11.1 Background
 Considered to have some of the most progressive refugee policies globally (1, 2), Uganda currently
 hosts over 1.5 million refugees and asylum seekers and, notably, the highest number of refugees
 in Africa (3). The forced migration of Sudanese people to Uganda began in the mid-1980s,
 followed by subsequent waves of asylum seekers and refugees from Somalia, Rwanda, Burundi,
 Eritrea, Sudan and Ethiopia over the past three decades. Most recently, Uganda has experienced
 large influxes of refugees from South Sudan and the Democratic Republic of the Congo, with as
 many as 150 000 new arrivals anticipated in 2022.

 The vast majority of refugees (94%) reside alongside Ugandan communities in 13 settlements
 across 13 districts in the northern and south-western parts of the country (3, 4). Refugee
 settlements are largely supported by UNHCR and resemble “open” villages (rather than restricted
 camps), in that refugees have the right to migrate freely, work and establish businesses
 throughout the country (1, 2). Refugees can also access health and education services at the
 same establishments as Ugandan nationals. With an integrated national public health and
 refugee health system since the early 2000s, more than 76% of health facilities in refugee-hosting
 districts are accredited by Ministry of Health (3). Historically, refugees in Uganda have had better
 access to higher-quality, well-equipped and well-staffed health facilities than the national
 population, due to the humanitarian response: integration with the national system was then
 encouraged to promote equity by improving health care for Ugandans (5). Nevertheless, despite
 those integration efforts, health services are still largely supported and managed by international
 and local NGOs serving as implementing partners for UNHCR and other bilateral agencies in
 collaboration with the Government of Uganda.

 Uganda is one of the 29 countries with a 2023 United Nations humanitarian response plan or
 appeal. As described in Chapter 4, there is an urgent need to address the high levels of maternal
 and perinatal mortality in humanitarian-affected countries to achieve global targets and
 milestones, including the SDGs.

 11.2 Methodology
 A descriptive case study was conducted to examine MPDSR implementation in districts with
 refugee settlements. This chapter presents the findings of a desk review and interviews with
 15 key informants, including representatives based in national and subnational offices of United
 Nations organizations (4), Ministry of Health (1), international NGOs (7), health-facility MPDSR
 focal points (2) and other organizations currently engaged in directly implementing or providing
 support to MPDSR within the refugee settlements. Interviews were conducted virtually via
 Zoom in English, using a semi-structured interview guide, and synthesized using thematic
 content analysis.

 11.3 MPDSR in Ugandan refugee settlements
 While at national level, MPDSR has evolved from the introduction of maternal death reviews in
 2002 (Box 11.1), early efforts to implement MPDSR within refugee populations were spearheaded
 by UNHCR, which in 2007 developed and introduced the Maternal Death Review Report and
 subsequently implemented maternal death review systems in all refugee settlements (6, 7).
 Currently, in collaboration with Ministry of Health, MPDSR within refugee settlements is led by
 United Nations organizations (UNHCR, UNFPA and UNICEF) and the CDC through key
 implementing partners (e.g. the International Rescue Committee, Medical Teams International
 and CARE).

132
````

### Trang PDF 153

````text
11. Case study: Uganda


Box 11.1 National introduction and scale-up of MPDSR in Uganda


 In the past two decades, MPDSR has been an increasing national priority in Uganda, as a
 targeted strategy to reduce maternal and perinatal mortality (8).

 Maternal death reviews were first implemented by Ministry of Health as part of a safe
 motherhood campaign in 2002, leading to the adoption of maternal death review guidelines. In
 2008, the president of the Republic of Uganda declared maternal mortality to be a national
 emergency and passed a presidential decree requiring routine reporting and review of all
 maternal and perinatal deaths in health facilities. As a result, maternal and perinatal death
 review committees were initiated at national and regional referral hospitals and were
 subsequently cascaded to secondary-level health facilities as standardized training and tools
 were developed. In 2012, maternal and perinatal deaths became notifiable conditions to be
 reported on the IDSR platform. Five years later, in 2017, the national guidelines were revised to
 reflect global recommendations (3) to support implementation of a full facility- and
 community-based MPDSR system. In the following two years, Ministry of Health, with partner
 support, developed a national MPDSR training curriculum and led training at national, regional
 and district levels. In 2019, UNICEF also led a national training scheme for trainers to reinforce
 the capacity of perinatal death reporting and review. This introductory training continues,
 using a facility-based mentorship approach both to maintain capacity (e.g. refresher training)
 and to recruit new staff to work on MPDSR.


MPDSR implementation follows the national MPDSR guidelines (8) in all refugee settlements,
and in some it is supplemented with UNHCR maternal death review guidance and reporting
requirements (9). As part of the integrated national MPDSR system, health facility and district
review committees have been established in all districts, including refugee-hosting districts, to
hold monthly meetings to review deaths and coordinate appropriate responses with the national
review committee. Many actors responsible for supporting and implementing MPDSR within the
refugee settlements (e.g. health workers, health facility administrators, review committee
members and quality improvement focal points) have benefitted from national training workshops.
However, the cascading of MPDSR training and ongoing refresher and training opportunities
within the settlements have been largely partner-dependent.

Despite the integration of the national and refugee health systems, dual reporting systems have
been established and maintained (10), requiring health workers in many refugee settlements to
submit death notification and reports to both DHIS2 and UNHCR’s Integrated Refugee Health
Information System. In particular, until 2020, disaggregation of refugee data was not obtainable
through DHIS2. Similarly, UNHCR tools for death reviews are used concurrently with Ministry of
Health tools, documenting more comprehensive information about each death case while
significantly increasing the documentation burden on health-service providers and staff. Through
DHIS2, an interactive event tracker and live MPDSR death notification and death review form has
been developed to improve reporting and coordinated response at the national level. Weekly
reports on maternal and perinatal deaths are disseminated, including those from refugee
settlements. The Sexual and Reproductive Health Technical Working Group, a coordinating body
for the refugee response, has also been used by United Nations organizations and implementing
partners as a coordination mechanism, enabling the dissemination and discussion of MPDSR
results and responses.

 133
````

### Trang PDF 154

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 11.4 Policy to practice
 The successful translation of MPDSR guidelines into practice in Ugandan refugee settlements
 varied according to the supporting agency and implementing partner funding, mandate and
 programming priorities. Partners within the refugee settlements reported high levels of facility- and
 community-based maternal death reports and reviews, with all facilities participating in the
 system and many surrounding communities engaging in community-based reporting processes
 and verbal autopsies. Coverage of perinatal deaths was less thorough. While all partners within
 the refugee settlements reported capturing and reviewing perinatal deaths, many acknowledged
 challenges relating to identifying intrapartum stillbirths and reviewing high caseloads, and were
 thus more likely to focus on neonatal deaths occurring in facilities and/or reviewing only a
 sample of reported perinatal deaths.

 Timely notification and review of facility-based maternal and perinatal deaths within the refugee
 settlements were also reported by partners. However, some delays in the notification and review
 (by verbal autopsy) of community-based deaths persisted. Data limitations and low reporting
 quality often impeded the review step of the MPDSR cycle. As reported in many other contexts,
 poor documentation of maternal and neonatal health care in patient records and registers was
 prevalent, especially for perinatal deaths. Case reports were often incomplete, and many lacked a
 cause of death classification. Misclassification of perinatal deaths was also commonplace.

 Interviewees reported strong functionality of review committees within refugee settlements.
 Facility-based review committees met for all reported maternal deaths and most (or a
 predetermined sample of) perinatal deaths. Many partners within refugee settlements also
 supported district review committees, often rendering them more functional than those in
 non-hosting districts; however, in cases where additional support was not available (e.g. financial
 resources to hold meetings), district representatives were instead invited to participate in health
 facility review committee meetings.

 Overall, the response to the need for improvement identified during death reviews was variable.
 Most agencies established systems to hold implementing partners accountable for identifying
 and addressing recommendations from maternal and perinatal death reviews. However, some
 partners reported receipt of vague or high-level recommendations unfit for improvement efforts
 at the facility level and/or unfeasible with available funding. New tools developed to help monitor
 the response were also being pilot-tested in many refugee settlements (e.g. CDC’s action
 planning response tracking tool).

 11.5 Complex implementation environment
 Implementation within refugee settlements in Uganda has been bolstered by a supportive
 political environment in the three to five years to 2022/2023. Recognizing MPDSR’s increasing
 national priority, one implementing partner stated:

 For me, what stands out is how conversations about MPDSR are happening at every
 space where you discuss maternal and neonatal health, be it at the district level or
 health-facility level; that is a very important aspect of building momentum to improve
 the implementation of MPDSR.

 Growing political attention to and advocacy for MPDSR have been matched with strong
 partner collaboration in the national MPDSR review committee, which includes representatives
 from United Nations organizations, development and humanitarian partners, USAID, academia,
 the private sector and health professional associations. In addition, improved (albeit still

134
````

### Trang PDF 155

````text
11. Case study: Uganda


insufficient according to interviewees) funding and financial commitments have been reported
by implementing partners, including funds to integrate MPDSR within primary health care and
quality improvement programming, as well as some dedicated funding for implementing MPDSR
(e.g. specified partner budget lines to support death review meetings).

Implementation of MPDSR is highly dependent on health worker and health facility administration
buy-in. Health workers within the settlements, as in other contexts, were at times reluctant to
participate, and perceived the MPDSR system as punitive and shrouded in a pervasive blame
culture, exacerbated by Uganda’s history of politicizing deaths and criminalizing health workers
for negligence. Widespread human resource issues characteristic of humanitarian settings,
such as high health worker stress, workload and attrition, have also thwarted health worker
participation within the refugee settlements.

An implementing partner in the Bidibidi refugee settlement said:

 Here at [health facility], the staffing level is at 38%, which is way below the
 Government’s 50% benchmark, so this usually poses a very big challenge for the
 available staff [to implement MPDSR] … the existing staff are overstretched with
 fatigue and burnt-out.

Health workers from two implementing partners have requested psychosocial support to cope
with the trauma of working within humanitarian response, witnessing high mortality and reliving
events during death reviews. To combat these issues, health facility managers in refugee
settlements have served as MPDSR champions with the power to ensure participation, navigate
internal dynamics to reduce blame and garner trust, and set the tone for MPDSR as a learning
opportunity for quality improvement.

In many refugee settlements, MPDSR is integrated into broader quality improvement frameworks
and aligned with activities focused on continuous improvement processes. For example, one
implementing partner has established settlement quality improvement focal points, responsible
for addressing recommendations and action points identified during death reviews and ensuring
accountability for the response component of MPDSR. In addition, quality improvement training
sessions have started to improve the understanding of the purpose of MPDSR among key
implementing actors, resulting in increased health worker participation. Family and community
member engagement as a part of quality improvement efforts was also key for shifting the
narrative and addressing the MPDSR blame culture. Often reliant on humanitarian aid,
community members within refugee settlements were disincentivized to report deaths by fear
of consequences relating to future accessing of health care and services and ration reductions
due to reduced household sizes. However, community dialogue initiated through quality
improvement programmes and community involvement in the MPDSR process via family health
teams and CHWs has fostered trust and buy-in in refugee settlements, while strengthening
community-based surveillance.


 135
````

### Trang PDF 156

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 11.6 Conclusions
 This case study highlights the complexities of implementing MPDSR within humanitarian
 settings. Distinguished by its integration into the national health system, findings from key
 partners supporting and directly implementing MPDSR within refugee settlements in Uganda call
 for a customized approach to implementing MPDSR that best serves the immediate needs of the
 crisis, aligns with stakeholder priorities, and supports health workers in providing care to the
 most vulnerable populations. Strengthening systems for mortality surveillance and quality
 improvement interventions across the varied humanitarian-affected settings highlighted in
 Chapter 4 will require dedicated investment and context-specific adaptations. If prioritized,
 improved mortality surveillance may draw more attention to supporting quality improvement
 initiatives, ultimately improving access to timely and respectful care for women and newborns in
 the most vulnerable settings.


136
````

### Trang PDF 159

````text
12. Ongoing challenges and
future opportunities


Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response is an approach to end preventable
maternal and newborn deaths and prevent stillbirths. Many countries have adopted policies to
notify and review deaths and put in place mechanisms to support the MPDSR process.
However, there is less information on the implementation of MPDSR, especially at subnational
level. As a result, this report aims to highlight experiences on implementation of MPDSR in
different WHO regions, illustrating how MPDSR works in practice despite various social,
economic and systems factors that impact mortality. The eight country case studies highlight
not only the ongoing challenges of MPDSR implementation but also many successes. There is no
single challenge or barrier to implementation, just as there is no single solution, but by examining
the unique experiences across a range of settings more closely, much can be learned.

Many of the challenges experienced in MPDSR implementation were shared by most countries.
For example, reporting of deaths is not complete and remains a challenge, especially where
births take place at home. Data collection can be inconsistent and incomplete even when done
electronically for births in health facilities. Deaths in private health facilities or at home may not
be reported. Reporting of perinatal deaths is low. Perinatal deaths, especially stillbirths, are
considered lower priority than maternal deaths. Data on maternal and perinatal mortality are
often collected separately and not analysed together. Incomplete documentation and poor-quality
data hinder the identification of the cause of and contributory factors to deaths and response
planning. In places where a culture of blame persists, clinicians could be reluctant to notify
deaths or participate in the review process, especially in the absence of legal protection of
MPDSR committee members and communities. In some countries, successful steps have been
taken towards legislation to support MPDSR and towards a no-blame approach. Community
engagement in MPDSR is generally limited. Accountability around maternal and perinatal
deaths is often absent. Shortages of adequately trained human resources and of funding often
interrupt MPDSR activities.

The case studies also show factors common in many countries that were linked to successful
outcomes. Political will, leadership, strong governance and accountability at the highest level all
move the MPDSR process forward in a timely fashion. Individual champions are essential and
include a range of individuals, including politicians, professionals (nurses, midwives, obstetricians,
paediatricians and others), community health workers, and of course community members,
pregnant individuals and their families.

The integration and use of MPDSR with existing data management systems minimizes reporting
requirements, makes data available in real time at subnational levels, and improves quality and
efficiency. Engaging stakeholders at all levels is important for success. Professional associations
are closely involved in MPDSR implementation, advocating to governments at both national and
subnational levels. Engagement with communities enhances the quality of the MPDSR reviews
and actions at community level. Close, transparent and equitable partnerships between national
institutions, private sector and international partners support sustainable implementation.

It is a challenge for MPDSR systems to capture deaths at home. In some contexts, community
review of deaths through verbal and social autopsies not only provide additional information to
capture medical and contributory cause of deaths that occur at home, but also enhance education
within the community, leading to more effective and timely responses to identified concerns.

 139
````

### Trang PDF 160

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 While the past decade has produced improvements in maternal and perinatal survival, the rates
 of decline have plateaued since 2016. Maternal and perinatal mortality, particularly in most
 lower-middle-income countries, remain unacceptably high and unprecedented levels of reduction
 are needed to achieve the SDG targets by 2030. MPDSR is a promising approach to accelerate
 reduction in mortality, when coupled with other interventions, in an effort to prevent similar
 deaths in the future and promote accountability for political will around preventable deaths.
 MPDSR can provide the information needed to prioritize quality improvement activities at
 subnational and facility levels, and enable appropriate allocation of resources (human resources,
 commodities, infrastructure, financial resources, etc.) based on identified needs and concerns.

 The findings of the eight country case studies provide valuable lessons for ways to strengthen
 MPDSR.
 • Reviewing perinatal deaths cases in combination with maternal death cases allows
 professionals and policy-makers to link the causes of perinatal mortality with the maternal
 conditions, thereby enabling them to propose recommendations and changes to correct the
 issues. Solutions such as the selection of perinatal death cases and review of those each
 month would be an advancement. Moreover, starting with the review of early newborn deaths,
 intrapartum and antepartum stillbirths, making sure that MPDSR committee members,
 paediatricians and neonatologists are in agreement and informed would ensure good
 communication of the findings.
 • When the MPDSR system is integrated with existing national routine health information systems
 (IDSR, RHIS, etc.) there is no information overlap or duplication, and data can be better
 monitored over time and by location. Moreover, this enables MPDSR to be part of one national
 surveillance system, which could include near-misses, birth defects and other notifiable events.
 • Putting a legal framework in place leads to enhanced transparency and legal accountability,
 and ensures confidentiality – separating the MPDSR system from any legal proceeding. This
 successfully decreases the fear of blame and punitive actions and establishes MPDSR as a
 routine practice for staff.
 • MPDSR data collection on electronic reporting systems linked to existing national routine
 health information systems ensures the use of standardized clinical forms and improves the
 reporting and timely collection of better-quality information and the routine publication of
 MPDSR reports or bulletins.
 • A community of practice established at national and district levels under the direction and
 leadership of the Ministry of Health enables evidence to be gathered of integration between
 policies and sustainable practice improving accountability.
 • Civil society, professional organizations’ and communities’ involvement in the MPDSR
 process improves the quality of death reviews; acts as a “pulling force” to hold all the
 stakeholders responsible for their roles during the process; and enables engagement with
 parliamentarians and Ministry of Health to address local challenges.
 • When the MPDSR response is developed with quality of Care (QoC) teams and response
 actions are routinely monitored, follow-up actions and clinical practice are improved. This is
 still a major challenge for all countries, since the MPDSR committee and QoC teams often
 have separate structures or one of the two do not exist at the same level of the health system.
 Chapter 10 gives an example of how Sri Lanka was able to monitor the implementation of
 recommended changes through its health information system. Moreover, when the national
 health-care schemes (insurers) and service-provider institutions are engaged in the
 surveillance system through national agreements under the leadership of Ministry of Health,
 there is a sustained follow-up in actions and quality improvements (QI).

140
````

### Trang PDF 161

````text
12. Ongoing challenges and future opportunities


Concrete actions by specific actor groups to support the interventions are listed in Table 12.1.

Table 12.1 Actions to be taken by specific groups of stakeholders to advance MPDSR

 Stakeholder group Actions based on MPDSR global report findings

 Policy-makers, To improve the quality of MPDSR implementation, policy-makers should focus on:
 Ministry of Health, • establishing a national community of practice with stakeholders at all levels to parliamentarians foster accountability and reduce the blame culture;
 • ensuring accountability in counting stillbirths and newborn deaths, with a selected
 number of cases to review each month;
 • supporting the integration of MPDSR into the national routine information system
 to foster quality and timely reporting – developing or integrating with existing
 electronic data collection systems improves the quality of documentation in and
 storage of medical records;
 • ensuring wide sharing the national policies and guidelines on MPDSR at all levels;
 • adapting, if needed, the national MPDSR tools to up-to-date global guidance – to
 simplify the data collection process;
 • adopting a new or amending existing legal and regulatory frameworks to avoid the
 blame culture for health workers, communities and families, and to ensure the
 confidentiality of the MPDSR process;
 • establishing MPDSR at all levels of the health system: national, district/regional,
 facility and community levels; every level should be clear on their role, including
 clear code of conduct and terms of reference (see WHO MPDSR materials to
 support implementation [1]);
 • creating synergies between the MPDSR and QoC teams at national, district and
 facility levels to codevelop and monitor response, allocating sufficient budget to
 improve MNH services;
 • planning for capacity-building activities at district and facility levels within health
 facilities to better understand challenges and opportunities and monitor progress.

 United Nations and To improve the quality of MPDSR implementation, United Nations and cooperation
 cooperation organizations, NGOs and civil society should focus on:
 organizations, • supporting the government and country partners in receiving up-to-date guidance NGOs, civil society and tools;
 • advocating to government on reporting and reviewing a selected number of
 perinatal deaths;
 • supporting the capacity-building of national-level stakeholders with possible
 lessons learned from other countries;
 • advocating with the government and country partners to use MPDSR as a complex
 system including quality of care and behaviour change; harmonizing data
 collection tools across the country, including with private hospitals, to ease
 national and subnational reporting;
 • harmonizing the MPDSR work and activities within other MNH interventions and
 national plan and not as a silo;
 • finding and nurturing potential national champions of MPDSR who can drive the
 change for better MNH outcomes;
 • supporting a learning agenda within and across countries.


 141
````

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 Stakeholder group Actions based on MPDSR global report findings

 Professional To improve the quality of MPDSR implementation, professional associations should
 associations focus on:
 • advocating for enriching the knowledge, capacity and resources of professionals,
 including on managerial skills to foster learning and quality;
 • creating opportunity for exchange to learn to work in multidisciplinary teams;
 • supporting pre-service and in-service capacity-building activities and mentoring
 for health workers;
 • understanding the power of your authority and mediation between health workers
 and the government;
 • promoting a blame-free culture.

 Health workers and To improve the quality of MPDSR implementation, health workers and health
 health managers managers should focus on:
 • understanding that MPDSR is not an extra-work activity, but it is embedded in the
 health workers’ job ToRs and patient safety strategy of your facility/hospital;
 • learning how to document and record quality data;
 • making sure you have evidence-based clinical protocols/standard operating
 procedures (SOPs) and guidelines available in your unit;
 • managers: using MPDSR as a management tool to support your staff in setting
 clear roles and responsibilities and enhance their skills in delivering better care;
 • managers: adopting a code of conduct for your MPDSR committee and always
 keeping the information confidential and anonymized.

 MPDSR committee To improve the quality of MPDSR implementation, MPDSR committee members
 members should focus on:
 • adopting a code of conduct signed by every member of the MPDSR committee and
 always keeping the information confidential and anonymized;
 • improving the data collection and analysis skills of the MPDSR committee
 members, performing quality checks on death reviews and not working in silos;
 • planning to attend regular capacity-building training to improve the classification
 of causes of death and review a death and how to make SMART recommendations
 and on quality improvement processes;
 • promoting peer-review and mentorship on self-evaluation;
 • learning communication and leadership skills;
 • creating an open environment addressing the multifaced issue of MPDSR and not
 blaming the individuals – psychological safety for health workers is essential to
 improve learning, performance, management and avoid burnouts and repercussions;
 • creating opportunities to link with community members, building relationships and
 recognizing the value and contribution of community members.
````

````text
12. Ongoing challenges and future opportunities

 • participating in training that supports community informants on how to report all
 deaths and participate in verbal autopsies or facility MPDSR meetings; CHWs
 could be trained and mentored to conduct verbal autopsies and provide
 bereavement counselling; community death review meetings provide a channel for

 Donors To improve the quality of MPDSR implementation, donors should focus on:
 • actively supporting the standardization of tools and indicators used to assess

 • supporting the MPDSR committees at facility level to see tangible change in
 implementing MPDSR recommendations with allocated resources, and increase in
 their capacity and skills;
 • supporting the global community in advancing evidence on how to adapt the
 MPDSR process and MNH guidelines to different contexts for optimal
 implementation to enhance QoC.


The ultimate challenge is to achieve better maternal and perinatal outcomes by achieving high-quality
care. This requires not only accurate data collection and thoughtful review of cases of maternal
and perinatal deaths, but also focused responses that address causes of death and severe
morbidity in specific settings. The findings from the surveys and country studies highlight the
benefits of collaboration and of learning how problems have been addressed elsewhere: they
should be used by others to map their next steps. Learning from others’ experience, rather than
working in silos, is an important step towards improved maternal and perinatal care globally.
````

### Trang PDF 165

````text
Annexes

Annex 1. Historical aspects
Millennium Development Goal (MDG) 5 provided a target for reducing global maternal mortality
by 75% from the estimated maternal mortality ratio (MMR) in 1990, by 2015.

Most maternal deaths occur in low- and middle-income countries and most result from
preventable causes. Fresh thinking on the usefulness of previously implemented strategies to
reduce maternal mortality, such as training traditional birth attendants, led to recognition of the
role of the skilled care provider, and the importance of prevention, and improved management of
complications in maternity care (1). To end preventable maternal and perinatal deaths, better
information is needed to understand and address medical and non-medical reasons why women
die during and following pregnancy and childbirth.

WHO recognizes the contribution made by maternal death reviews to improving maternal health
care and has actively promoted its use in countries since 2004, when it published Beyond the
numbers (2), a guide on different methods for review of maternal deaths and near-misses. Over the
following years, many countries around the world initiated maternal death reviews with support
from international partners. However, the uptake and quality of the review process varied widely.
While some positive experiences were documented, in many countries the process ended with
the review, with no follow-up action based on its findings.

The concept of a maternal death surveillance and response (MDSR) system – which included
maternal death identification, reporting, review and response – emerged in 2011 to ensure more
robust collection and use of information for action to prevent future maternal deaths and
improve the measurement of maternal mortality (3). This new approach was introduced by WHO
and partners in 2012 to improve accountability for maternal health. WHO convened a global
MDSR Technical Working Group (TWG) comprising United Nations and bilateral agencies,
professional organizations, academic institutions and advocacy groups. MDSR was introduced
globally through orientation and capacity-building workshops at regional, subregional, national
and subnational levels in all WHO regions. These workshops facilitated regional learning,
provided opportunities for planning strategic actions, and were supported by members of the
TWG. The TWG also drafted technical guidance on MDSR implementation, published in 2013 (4).

During the same period, mortality among children under five years of age had fallen in response
to improvements in immunization coverage, water quality and sanitation, and effective management
of diarrhoeal and respiratory illnesses. However, improvements in child survival were unevenly
distributed across different ages, with the least improvement made in the first four weeks of life.
Also, large numbers of babies were stillborn. The Every Newborn Action Plan (ENAP), endorsed
by the World Health Assembly in 2014, recognized the key role of perinatal care – the care of the
mother and her baby during and following pregnancy and childbirth – in reducing mortality
among newborns, as well as its role in reducing maternal mortality and stillbirths (5). That same
year, WHO released a consensus statement on targets and strategies for ending preventable
maternal mortality (EPMM) (6), which was subsequently endorsed by UNICEF, the United Nations
Population Fund (UNFPA), the World Bank Group, the United States Agency for International
Development (USAID), and numerous international professional organizations and maternal
health programmes.


 145
````

### Trang PDF 166

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation


 An ENAP milestone was reached in 2016, when WHO published Making every baby count (7),
 with technical guidance on surveillance and review of stillbirths and neonatal deaths. WHO and
 partners introduced these new tools through regional and national workshops and the guidelines
 were adopted by many countries.

 Many countries have historically described preventable maternal deaths by the three delays
 model. The Three Delays Model, initially formulated by Thaddeus in 1994, serves as a
 comprehensive framework for comprehending the obstacles that may impede timely access to
 emergency obstetric care, consequently influencing the health outcomes of both mothers and
 newborns (8). Although initially crafted to decipher the factors underlying maternal fatalities
 following the onset of obstetric complications, this model’s scope has since expanded to
 encompass all facets of maternal care, beyond just immediate obstetric emergencies (9-15).

 The original model identifies three main delays.

 (1) Delay in decision-making: this refers to the delay of the decision to seek care. It can be
 influenced by lack of awareness about danger signs during pregnancy or childbirth, cultural
 beliefs, fear and inadequate information.

 (2) Delay in reaching care: this may include barriers that prevent or hinder a woman from
 physically reaching a health facility in a timely manner, such as long distances, lack of
 transportation, poor road infrastructure and financial constraints.

 (3) Delay in receiving adequate care: this delay occurs when a woman reaches a health facility
 but experiences delays in receiving appropriate and timely care. It can be caused by factors
 such as lack of staff, inadequate resources, lack of skilled health workers, poor quality of
 care or discriminatory practices.

 This approach aids in spotting common delays tied to three main aspects: seeking care,
 reaching care, and getting care at a medical facility, as well as the number of deaths linked to
 each delay (10-12, 14).


146
````

````text
Maternal and perinatal death surveillance and response: global report on decade of implementation

 Annex 2. Regional maternal and perinatal death surveillance
 and response (MPDSR) capacity-building activities
 Countries in the six WHO regions. The United States Agency for International Development’s
 (USAID’s) Momentum Country and Global Leadership, WHO, UNFPA and UNICEF have developed
 an integrated MPDSR capacity-building package that can be used to support country capacity
 for MPDSR through virtual and in person means (1). Capacity-building materials for maternal and
 perinatal mortality surveillance have been combined into one package, including updated
 guidance and COVID-19 modules. The package was pilot-tested in the South-East Asia Region in
 2020 and then expanded to other WHO regions. Capacity-building on MPDSR using this package
 was conducted in these regions in collaboration with WHO, UNICEF, UNFPA, Momentum Country
 and Global Leadership, Jhpiego and professional societies.
 Regional professional associations (South Asian Federation of Obstetricians and Gynecologists
 andcapacitybuildingAsiaactivities.Oceanic FederationNational professionalof Obstetriciansassociationsand Gynecologists)(Federation ledof Obstetricthe regionaland Gynecological

 and Gynecological Society and Nepal Society of Obstetrics and Gynecology) led the in-country

 followedpostcourseknowledgeby a four-dayassessmentcapacity-buildingto completeworkshopthe activity.and mustFromsecurethis pool,85%selectedor more participantsin the
 attend a two-day Facilitators’ Preparation Workshop and complete an online MPDSR course
 developed by the Latin American Perinatal Centre of WHO Region of the Americas.
 After completing both activities, participants become eligible for co-training with master
 facilitators. These cofacilitators cascade the capacity-building activities in the country and
 hospitals under the supervision of a master facilitator. After conducting at least two co-training
 activities, a co-trainer is deemed competent to lead the MPDSR workshop independently and to
 be certified as an MPDSR facilitator. The virtual workshop uses Zoom for video conferencing,
 while WhatsApp is used for mentorship among master facilitators, cofacilitators and participants
 before, during and after the capacity-building activities. Momentum Country and Global
 Leadership maintains an Excel-based central database that is periodically updated when
 capacity-building activities are conducted.
 The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) course is an online course developed by PAHO/WHO
 staff in collaboration with Jhpiego, the International Confederation of Midwives and other
 partners (2). The course has been available since November 2019 to users from all WHO regions.
 PAHO maintains a database of the professional backgrounds and locations of course participants.
 As noted above, successful completion of this course is required for certification as a master
 trainer in Momentum Country and Global Leadership-led courses in other WHO regions.
 Eastern and southern African countries. During the COVID-19 pandemic, capacity for MPDSR
 implementation needed to be increased, but in-person capacity-building activities were hindered.
 WHO, UNFPA and UNICEF, working with USAID’s MOMENTUM Country and Global Leadership
 programme, decided to provide virtual MPDSR training for seven target countries in the
 subregion that had specifically requested this support, with the caveat that they would
 implement the lessons in selected “learning sites” in the countries. Several webinars on MPDSR
 and related topics were organized, attended by participants from within and beyond these
 countries. As demand for remote learning increased, the three United Nations organizations
 worked with the World Continuing Education Alliance to adapt MPDSR training modules for
 e-learning. The course, which addresses the various aspects of MPDSR implementation, are
 available, on the Alliance’s platform (3), in English, French and Spanish (4-11). It includes
 assessments (knowledge tests) that facilitate the certification and awarding of continuing
 professional development points for participants.
````
